===Chinese===

> 一个士兵停下脚步，对我说：「长官，在右边——下一扇门。」我心不在焉地跟着他走了，毫不怀疑这是我们自己的一名卫生员，随着一小群围着一辆颠簸的轮式伙房走动的士兵往前去。那伙房让我纳闷，因为我知道我们支队并没有带伙房出来，但我琢磨着，或许是师里的军医们求我们的人去帮军队卫生员们的忙。

> 我们匆匆穿过右边一扇门，进到一个似乎是某种院子的地方，伙房安在了那里；随后，士兵们仿佛从地底下钻了出来，端着铁皮罐围拢到伙房四周。带队的那名士兵用推心置腹的耳语对我说：「麦粥（*kasha*）有的是，长官，汤也够我们喝的。」

> 「很好，」我困惑地说。听见这古怪的口音，那士兵望了望我，我也望了望那士兵。那士兵于我是生人（一个讨人喜欢的圆脸汉子，咧着一张大笑嘴，长着双下巴），我于那士兵也是生人。

> 「呃，」那士兵望着我，说，「我还当……」

> 「我还当——」我说，浑身不自在。

> 士兵们缩回了伙房四周的黑暗里。听得见窃窃私语声。

===Original===

> Now, that’s the end, thought I. I’m shot as a German spy.

> I looked at the soldiers, clustered like bees round the kitchen, then I slipped through the gate into the dark road. I stood there listening. The battle seemed to have drawn away, because I could hear rifles, machine-guns, cannon muffled round a corner of the hill. Here there was now silence, broken only by soldiers who hurried up the road or went in and out at the villa gates. I felt abandoned. How was I to discover Nikitin again? Before what gate had I stood? I did not know; I seemed to know nothing.

> I moved down the road, very miserable and very cold. I had stupidly left my coat in one of the wagons. I walked on, my boots knocking against one another, thinking to myself: “If I’m not given something to do very soon I shall be just as I was the other night at Nijnieff﻿—and then I shall suddenly take to my heels down this road as hard as I can go!”

> It was then that I tumbled straight into the arms of Nikitin, who was standing at the edge of the forest, watching for me. I was so happy that I felt now afraid of nothing. I held Nikitin’s arm, babbling something about kitchens and Germans.

> “Well, I don’t understand what you say,” I remember Nikitin replied; “but you must come and work. There’s plenty of it.”

===Chinese===

> 得，这下完了，我心想。我要被当作德国间谍枪毙了。

> 我望了望那些像蜜蜂一样围着伙房的士兵，随即溜出那扇门，到了黑暗的路上。我站在那里听。战斗似乎已抽身远去，因为我听得见的步枪声、机枪声、炮声都闷在了山的那一道拐弯后面。这里此刻是一片寂静，只有路上匆匆奔走的士兵和别墅门前进进出出的人影把它打破。我感到自己被抛下了。我再到哪里去找尼基京呢？我方才站的是哪一扇门前？我不知道；我似乎什么都不知道了。

> 我顺着路往前走，心里凄惶，身上发冷。我愚蠢地把外衣落在哪辆大车上了。我一路走，两只靴子磕磕碰碰，心里想着：「要是再不派点事给我干，我就会又变成前几天夜里在尼日涅夫的那副样子——到那时我会突然撒开腿，顺着这条路能跑多快跑多快！」

> 正在这时，我一头撞进了尼基京的怀里——他正站在森林边上守着等我。我高兴得此刻什么都不怕了。我抓着尼基京的胳膊，语无伦次地念叨着些伙房和德国人的话。

> 「行了，你说什么我没听懂，」我记得尼基京是这样回答的，「可你得来干活了。有的是活儿。」

===Original===

> We moved to a cottage on the very boundary of the forest, where a little common ran down to the moonlight. Passing through a narrow passage, I entered into a little room with a large white stove. On the top of the stove, under the roof, crouched a boy or a young man with long black hair and a white face. This youth wore what resembled a white shirt over baggy white trousers. His feet were bare and very dirty. Nothing moved except his eyes. He sat there, in exactly that position, all night.

===Chinese===

> 我们挪到紧贴森林边界的一座小农舍去，舍前一小片荒地一直漫到月光里。穿过一条窄窄的过道，我走进一间有白色大炉子的小屋。炉顶上、屋檐底下，蹲着一个少年，或者是个年轻男子，黑发长长的，脸色苍白。这青年穿一件像是白衬衫的衣服，底下是肥大的白裤子。他光着双脚，脏得很。除了两只眼睛，他一动不动。他就那样蹲在那里，分毫不改那个姿势，蹲了整夜。

===Original===

> The room was small but was the best that could be obtained. Within the space of ten minutes it became a perfect shambles. The wounded were brought in without pause and under the candlelight Nikitin, two sanitars, and I worked until the sweat ran down our backs and arms in streams. It dripped from my nose, into my mouth, into my eyes. The wounds were horrible. No man seemed to come into the room with an unmangled body. The smell rose higher and higher, the bloody rags lay about the kitchen floor, torn arms, smashed legs, heads with gaping wounds, the pitiful crying and praying, the shrill voices of the delirious, Nikitin, his arms steeped in blood to the elbows, probing, cutting, digging, I myself bandaging until I did not know what my hands were doing.﻿ ﻿… Then suddenly the battle coming right back to us again, overhead now as it seemed; the cannon shaking three silly staring china dogs on the kitchen dresser, the rifle fire clattering like tumbling crockery about the walls of the cottage﻿—and through it all the white youth, crouched like a ghost on the stove, watching without pause.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

> 房间很小，但已是能弄到的最好的了。不出十分钟，它就成了一座不折不扣的屠宰场。伤员们不停地被抬进来，烛光底下，尼基京、两名卫生员和我一直干到汗水顺着脊背和胳膊成股地淌。汗从我的鼻尖滴下来，滴进嘴里，滴进眼睛里。伤势骇人。似乎没有一个人是带着囫囵的身子进这间屋的。气味越涌越高，血污的破布摊了满地，撕裂的胳膊，砸烂的腿，裂开大口子的头，凄惨的哭喊和祷告，谵妄者的尖声怪叫，尼基京两条胳膊浸血浸到肘部，探查，切割，挖掘，我自己包扎包扎到不知道自己的手在做什么。……随后战斗猛然又压回到我们头顶上来，这会儿仿佛就在正上方；炮火震得厨房碗柜上三只瞪着眼傻看的瓷狗直颤，步枪的射击像摔碎的陶器噼里啪啦砸在农舍四壁上——而透过这一切，那个白衣服的青年仍像幽灵一样蹲在炉子上，望着，一刻不停。……

===Original===

> “Ah, no, your Honour.﻿ ﻿… Ah, no!﻿ ﻿… I can’t! I can’t! Oh, oh, oh, oh!” and then sobs, the man breaking down like a child, hiding his face in his arms, his wounded leg twitching convulsively. I paused, wiped the sweat from my eyes, stood up. Nikitin looked at me.

> “Take some fresh air!” he said. “Go out with the stretcher for half an hour. I can manage here.”

> I wiped my forehead.

> “Sure you can manage?” I asked.

> “Quite,” said Nikitin. “Here, hold his back!﻿ ﻿… No, *durak*, his *back*. *Bojé moi*, can’t you get your arm under? There﻿—like that. *Horosho, golubchik, horosho*﻿ ﻿… only a minute! There! There!”

===Chinese===

> 「啊，不，长官。……啊，不！……我受不了！我受不了！哦，哦，哦，哦！」接着是呜咽，那人像孩子一样垮了下来，把脸埋进臂弯，伤腿一阵阵地痉挛抽搐。我停下手，抹去眼里的汗，站起身。尼基京望着我。

> 「出去透透气！」他说。「跟着担架出去半个钟头。这儿我照应得住。」

> 我抹了抹额头。

> 「你真照应得住？」我问。

> 「完全照应得住，」尼基京说。「来，托住他的背！……不对，*durak*（俄语：傻瓜），他的*背*。*Bojé moi*（俄语：我的上帝），你不能把胳膊伸到底下吗？喏——就这样。*Horosho, golubchik, horosho*（俄语：好了，小鸽子，好了）……就一会儿！好！好！」

===Original===

> I washed my hands and went out. The air caressed my forehead like cold water; from the little garden at the back there came scents of flowers; the moonlight was blue on the common. Eight sanitars were waiting to start. The Feldsher in charge of them did not, I thought, seem greatly pleased when he saw me, but then I am often stupidly sensitive; no one said anything and we started. We carried two stretchers and a soldier from the trenches was with us to guide us.

> I could see that the men were not happy. I heard one of them mutter to another that they should not have been sent now; that they should have waited until the attack was over﻿ ﻿… “and the full moon.﻿ ﻿… Did anyone ever see such a moon?”

> We came to crossroads and advanced very carefully.

===Chinese===

> 我洗了手，走出去。空气像凉水一样爱抚我的额头；后头的小花园里飘来花香；月光在荒地上是一片蓝。八名卫生员整装待发。他们带队的药剂医士（Feldsher）见了我，我看，似乎并不十分高兴，不过我这人常常蠢兮兮地神经过敏；谁也没说什么，我们就出发了。我们抬着两副担架，还有一名战壕里来的士兵给我们带路。

> 我看得出这些人心里不痛快。我听见其中一个跟另一个嘀咕，说不该这会儿就派他们出来；该等进攻结束再说……「还有这满月。……谁见过这样的月亮？」

> 我们来到十字路口，极其小心地往前走。

===Original===

> As we crossed the road I was conscious of great excitement. The noise around us was terrific and different from any noise that I heard before. I did not think at the time, but was informed afterwards that it was because we were almost directly under a high-wooded cliff (the actual position about whose possession the battle was being fought), that the noise was so tremendous. The echo flung everything back so that each report sounded three or four times. This certainly had the strangest effect﻿—a background as it were of rolling thunder, sometimes distant, sometimes very close and, in front of this, clapping, bellowing, stamping, and then suddenly an absolutely *smashing* effect as though someone cried: “Well, this will settle it!” In quieter intervals one heard the birdlike flight of bullets above one’s head and the irritated bad temper of the machine-guns. At every *smashing* noise the sanitars, who were, I believe, schoolmasters and little clerks, and therefore of a more sensitive head than the peasant soldier, ducked their heads, and one fat red-faced man tried to lie down flat on two occasions and was cursed heartily by the Feldsher. I myself felt no fear but only a pounding exhilarating excitement, because I was at last “really in it.” We found one wounded man very soon, lying under the hedge with the top of his head gone. Four sanitars (their relief showed very plainly in their faces) returned with him. We advanced again, skirting now a little orchard and keeping always in the shadow under the hedge. Our guide, the soldier, assured us that the wounded man was “very near﻿—quite close.” Then we came to a large barn on the edge of what seemed a silver lake but was in reality a long field under the full light of the moon. As we paused I saw, on the further side of the field, two shells burst, very quickly, one after the other.

===Chinese===

> 穿过那条路时，我心里意识到一阵巨大的兴奋。我们四周的声响大极了，同我从前听过的任何声响都不同。我当时没去想、后来才有人告诉我：那是因为我们几乎正处在一座高高的、林木繁茂的断崖底下（双方厮杀争夺的正是那处阵地），声响才那么惊天动地。回声把一切都掷回来，每一声炮响都要响上三四遍。这确乎产生了最奇异的效果——仿佛有一层滚滚的雷声做底子，时而遥远，时而迫近；在这底子前面，是劈啪声、吼叫声、跺脚声，随后突然来一下绝对的*粉碎性*巨响，仿佛有人喊道：「好，这一下就了结了！」在较安静的间隙里，能听见子弹从头顶掠过时那鸟儿扑翅般的声音，和机枪的暴躁脾气。每逢那*粉碎性*的巨响，卫生员们——我相信他们原是小学教师和小职员，脑袋比庄稼汉士兵敏感——就把头一缩，有个红脸膛的胖子有两回想整个人趴下，被药剂医士狠狠骂了一顿。我自己倒不觉得害怕，只觉得一阵擂鼓般令人振奋的兴奋，因为我终于「真正陷在其中」了。我们很快找到一个伤员，躺在树篱底下，天灵盖没了。四名卫生员（他们脸上掩不住如释重负的神色）抬着他回去了。我们继续前进，绕过一座小果园的边，始终贴着树篱的影子走。我们的向导、那名士兵向我们保证，伤员就在「很近的地方——就在跟前」。随后我们来到一座大谷仓旁，仓边那片看着像银色湖泊的，其实是一片被满月照亮的长条田野。我们在那里停住时，看见田野远侧接连两发炮弹，一前一后，炸得飞快。

===Original===

> We all stopped under the shelter of the barn.

> “Well,” said the Feldsher to the soldier, “where’s your man?”

> “Only a short way,” said the soldier. “Quite close.”

> “Across that field?” asked the Feldsher, pointing to the moonlight.

> “Yes, certainly,” said the soldier.

===Chinese===

> 我们都在谷仓的掩蔽下停住了。

> 「喂，」药剂医士对那士兵说，「你那个人在哪儿？」

> 「没多远，」士兵说。「就在跟前。」

> 「要穿过那片田？」药剂医士指着月光问。

> 「是，可不，」士兵说。

===Original===

> The Feldsher scratched his head. “We can’t go further without orders,” he said. “That’s very dangerous in front there. I’m responsible for these men. We must return and ask, your Honour,” he said, turning to me.

> “We shall be nearly an hour returning,” I said. “Is your friend badly wounded?” I asked the soldier.

> “Very,” said he.

> “You see﻿ ﻿…” I said to the Feldsher. “We can’t possibly leave him like that. It’s only a little way.”

> The Feldsher shook his head. “I can’t be responsible. I had my orders to go so far and no further. I must see that my men are safe.”

> The sanitars who were sitting in a row on their haunches under the shadow of the barn all nodded their heads.

===Chinese===

> 药剂医士挠了挠头。「没有命令我们就不能往前走了，」他说。「前面那儿非常危险。这几个人我得负责。我们得回去请示，长官，」他说着，转向我。

> 「回去一趟差不多要一个钟头，」我说。「你那位同伴伤得重吗？」我问那士兵。

> 「很重，」他说。

> 「你瞧……」我对药剂医士说。「我们总不能就把他撂在那儿。不过一点点路。」

> 药剂医士摇了摇头。「我不能担这个责任。我接到的命令是走到这里为止，不得再往前。我得保证我的人安全。」

> 蹲坐在谷仓阴影下排成一排的卫生员们全都点了点头。

===Original===

> “I didn’t know Russians were cowards,” I said fiercely.

> The Feldsher shook his head quite unmoved: “Your Honour must understand that I had my orders.” Then he added slowly: “but of course if your Honour wishes to go yourself﻿ ﻿… I would come with you. The others﻿ ﻿… they must do as they please. They are in their right to return. But I should advise that we return.”

> “I’m going on,” I said.

===Chinese===

> 「我原先可不知道俄国人竟是懦夫，」我狠狠地说。

> 药剂医士毫不动摇地又摇了摇头：「长官必须明白，我接到了命令。」随后他慢慢补了一句：「不过，长官要是自己愿意去……我陪您去。其余的人……他们愿意怎么办就怎么办。他们有权回去。但我还是劝咱们回去。」

> 「我要往前走，」我说。

===Original===

> I must say here that I felt no other sensation than a blind and quite obstinate selfishness. I had no thought of Nikitin or of the sanitars. I did not (and this I must emphasise) think, for a moment, of the wounded man. If the situation had been that by returning I should save many lives and by advancing should save only my own I should still have advanced. If the only hope for the wounded man was my instant speech with Nikitin I would not have gone back to speak with him. I was at this moment neither brave nor fearful. I repeat that I had no sensation except an absolutely selfish obstinate challenge that I, myself, was addressing to Something in space. I was saying: “At last, my chance has come. Now you shall see whether I fly from you or no. *Now* you shall do your worst and fail. I’m the hunter now, not the hunted.”

> I was conscious of nothing but this quite childish preoccupation with myself. I was, nevertheless, pleased with myself. “There, you see,” someone near me seemed to say, “he’s not quite so unpractical after all. He’s full of common sense.” I looked at the row of sanitars squatting on the ground, and felt like a schoolmaster with his children.

> “You’d better go home then,” I said scornfully. The Feldsher, who was a short stocky man, with a red face and melancholy eyes (something like a prizefighter turned poet), dismissed them. They went off in a line under the hedge.

===Chinese===

> 我必须在这里说明：我当时心里没有别的感觉，只有一种盲目的、极其顽固的自私。我没有想到尼基京，也没有想到卫生员们。我（这一点我必须强调）一刻也没有想到那个伤员。倘若当时的情况是：我回去便能救下许多条命，我前进却只能救下我自己一条命，我照样会前进。倘若那伤员唯一的生路在于我立刻回去同尼基京说一句话，我也不会回去同他说。此刻的我，既非勇敢，也非怯懦。我再说一遍：除了我向冥冥空中的某个东西发出的一股全然自私、全然顽固的挑战之外，我什么感觉都没有。我在说：「我的机会终于来了。现在你可以看看，我到底会不会从你面前逃走。*现在*你尽管使出你最狠的手段，也注定失败。如今我是猎人，不是猎物。」

> 我心里只有这份十足孩子气的自我专注，别的什么都没有。尽管如此，我对自己颇为得意。「瞧见没有，」我身旁仿佛有谁在说，「他到底也不全然不切实际。他满肚子常识呢。」我望着蹲在地上排成一列的卫生员们，觉得自己像个领着一群孩子的小学教师。

> 「那你们还是回家去吧，」我轻蔑地说。药剂医士——一个矮壮汉子，红脸膛，一双忧郁的眼睛（有几分像改行写诗的拳击手）——把他们打发了。他们排成一列，顺着树篱底下走了。

===Original===

> The man obviously thought me a tiresome prig. He had no romantic illusions about the business; he had not been a Feldsher during twenty years for nothing and knew that a wound was a wound; when a man was dead he was *dead*.

> However.﻿ ﻿… “Truly it’s not far?” he asked the soldier.

> “*Tak totchno*,” the man answered, his face quite without expression.

===Chinese===

> 这人显然把我当成了一个讨厌的自命清高之徒。他对这件事没有半点浪漫的幻想；他这二十年的药剂医士不是白当的，深知伤口就是伤口；人死了，就是*死了*。

> 不过……「真不远？」他问那士兵。

> 「*Tak totchno*，」那人答道，脸上毫无表情。

===Original===

> We crossed the moonlit field and for a brief moment silence fell, as though an audience were holding its breath watching us. On the other side were cottages, the outskirts of a tiny village. Here beside these cottages we fell into a fantastic world. That small village must in other times have been a pretty place, nestling with its gardens by the river under the hill. It seemed now to rock and rattle under the noise of the cannon. All the open spaces were like white marble in the moonlight and in these open spaces there was utter silence and emptiness. The place seemed deserted﻿—and yet, in every shadow, in long lines under the cottage wells, in little clumps and clusters round trees or ruins there were eyes staring, the gleam of muskets shone, little specks of light, dancing from wall to wall. Everywhere there were bodies, legs, boots, arms, heads, sudden caps, sudden fingers, sudden hot and streaming breaths. And over everything this infernal noise and yet no human sound. A nightmare of the true nightmare of dreams. The open silver spaces, the little gardens thick with flowers, the high moon and the starry sky, not a living soul to be seen﻿—and nevertheless watchers everywhere. “Step forward on to that little plot of grass in front of the cottage windows and you’re a dead man”﻿—the moonlight said. There were men in the body of the earth, not in trenches, but in holes﻿—my foot stepped on a head of hair and some low voice cursed me. I was, I suppose, by this time, a little delirious with my adventure. I know that I could now distinguish no separate sounds﻿—shells and bullets had vanished and in their stead were whispers and screams and shouts of triumph and bursts of laughter. Songs in chorus, somewhere miners hammering below the earth, somewhere storm at sea with the crash of waves on rocks and the shriek of wind through rigging, somewhere someone who dropped heavy loads of furniture so carelessly that I cursed him﻿—and always these little patches of moonlight, so tempting just because one was forbidden.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

> 我们穿过月光照亮田野，有一小会儿四下里一片寂静，仿佛满场的看客都屏住呼吸望着我们。田野那一边是几座农舍，一座小小村落的边缘。就在这些农舍旁边，我们跌进了一个光怪陆离的世界。那座小村子从前想必是个可爱的地方，带着它的花园偎依在小山下的河边。此刻它似乎在炮声里摇晃、震颤。所有那些空地都在月光下白得像大理石，而这些空地上又是彻底的寂静和空无。这地方看似空无一人——然而每一处阴影里、农舍水井下一长溜一长溜的地方、树旁或废墟边一小堆一小堆的人群里，都有眼睛在瞪视，火枪的幽光在闪亮，一小点一小点的光，从这面墙跳到那面墙。到处是身体、腿、靴子、胳膊、脑袋，冷不丁一顶军帽，冷不丁一根手指，冷不丁一股滚烫喷涌的呼吸。这一切之上笼着那种地狱般的喧嚣，却又没有半点人声。梦中之梦的真正噩梦。一片片开阔的银色空地，一座座花木繁茂的小花园，高悬的月亮和繁星满天的穹庐，不见一个活人——然而到处都是窥伺者。「往农舍窗前那小块草皮上跨一步，你就是死人」——月光这样说。泥土里面藏着人，不在战壕里，而是在一个个坑里——我一脚踩在一蓬头发上，有个低低的声音咒骂了我。我想，到这时，我已被自己的这场奇遇弄得有点谵妄了。我知道，我此刻已分辨不出任何单独的声响——炮弹和子弹都不见了，取而代之的是耳语、尖叫、胜利的呐喊和一阵阵大笑。有合唱的歌，什么地方的矿工在地底下锤打，什么地方海上起了风暴，浪头砸在礁石上，风穿过桅索尖啸，什么地方有谁把一件件沉重的家具摔得漫不经心，气得我直咒骂他——而始终有这一小块一小块的月光，正因为遭了禁，才那么诱人……

===Original===

> We were not popular here. Husky, breathless voices whispered to us “to be away from here, quick. We would draw the fire. What did we want here now?”

> “Have you any wounded?” we whispered in return.

> “No, no,” the answer came. “Keep away from the moonlight.” The voices came to us connected sometimes with a nose, an eye, or a leg, often enough out of the heaven itself.

> “There’s a man wounded behind the next lines,” some voice murmured.

> We stumbled on and suddenly came to a river with very steep banks and a number of narrow and slender bridges. If this *had* in reality been a nightmare this river could not have obtruded itself more often than it did. We discovered to our dismay that our soldier-guide had disappeared (exactly as in a nightmare he would have done). We crossed the river (bathed of course in moonlight), the plank bridge shaking and quivering beneath us.

===Chinese===

> 我们在这儿不受欢迎。嘶哑的、屏着气的嗓音向我们耳语，「快离开这儿。我们会把火力引来的。这会儿我们要在这儿做什么？」

> 「你们有伤员吗？」我们也耳语着回问。

> 「没有，没有，」回答传来。「离月光远点。」那些嗓音传到我们耳朵里，有时连着一只鼻子、一只眼睛或一条腿，又往往干脆是从老天爷自己那儿传下来的。

> 「下一道散兵线后头有个人受了伤，」有个声音咕哝道。

> 我们跌跌撞撞往前走，突然来到一条河前，河岸极陡，河上架着好几座又窄又细的桥。这事倘若*当真*是一场噩梦，这条河也决不可能比它实际的样子更频繁地强行拦到人面前来。我们沮丧地发现，我们那名士兵向导不见了（恰恰像噩梦里他会干的那样）。我们渡了河（自然浸在月光里），木板桥在我们脚下摇撼颤动。

===Original===

> We had then a difficult task. Here a row of cottages beneath the very edge of the bank and in the cottage shadow the soldiers were ranged in a long line. Their boots stretched to the verge of the bank, which was slippery and uncertain. We had to walk on this with our stretchers, stepping between the boots, stumbling often and slipping down towards the water.

> “Any wounded?” we whispered again and again.

> “No,” the whisper came back. “Hasten.﻿ ﻿… Take care of the moonlight.”

> And then, to my infinite relief and comfort, behind the cottages we found our wounded man. There was a dark yard here, apparently quite deserted. The Feldsher made an exclamation and stepped forward. Three bodies lay together, over one another; two men were dead and cold, the third stirred, very faintly, as we came up, opened his eyes, smiled and said:

> “Eh, *bojé moi*﻿ ﻿… at last!”

> As we moved him on to the stretcher, with a little sigh he fainted again. He had a bad stomach-wound. Before picking up the stretcher, the Feldsher wiped his forehead and crossed himself.

===Chinese===

> 接着我们的活儿难了。这里，紧贴着河岸边沿底下有一排农舍，农舍的阴影里，士兵们排成长长的一列。他们的靴子一直伸到岸沿边上，那岸沿又滑又靠不住。我们只得抬着担架从这上面走，在一只只靴子之间落脚，不时绊一下，朝水边滑下去。

> 「有伤员吗？」我们一遍又一遍地耳语。

> 「没有，」耳语传回来。「快点。……当心月光。」

> 随后，叫我无限宽慰、无限欣慰的是，在农舍后面我们找到了我们的伤员。那里有一个黑黢黢的院落，看去全然荒废无人。药剂医士惊叹一声，抢步上前。三具身体叠在一起躺着；两个已经死了，凉了；我们走近时，第三个极轻微地动了动，睁开眼睛，笑了，说：

> 「哎，*bojé moi*……可算来了！」

> 我们把他挪上担架时，他轻轻叹了口气，又晕了过去。他腹部伤得很重。抬起担架之前，药剂医士抹了抹额头，画了个十字。

===Original===

> “It’s a heavy thing for two,” he said. “He’s a big man,” looking at the soldier. There was now somewhere, apparently not very far away, hot rifle fire. The crackle sparkled in the air, as though one were living in a world in which all the electricity was loose. The other firing seemed to have drawn away, and the *Boom﻿—Boom﻿—boom* in front of us was echo from the hill.﻿ ﻿…

> We picked up the stretcher and started. It was fortunate for us that we had that difficult bit beside the river at the beginning of our journey. I don’t know how we managed it, stepping over the endless row of legs, with every side step the stretcher lurching over to the left and threatening to pitch us into the river. So slippery too was the ground that our boots refused to grip. The man on the stretcher was dreaming, making a little sound like an unceasing lullaby on two notes﻿—“Na﻿ ﻿… na! Na﻿ ﻿… na! Na﻿ ﻿… na!”

> We were compelled to cross the river twice, and the planks bent under our weight until I was assured that they would snap. My arms were beginning to ache and the sweat to trickle down my spine. My right boot had rubbed my heel. We left the river behind us and then, suddenly, my right hand began to slip off the iron handle of the stretcher.

> “We’ll have to put it down a moment,” I said. We laid it on the ground and at the same instant a bullet sang so close to my ear that I felt it as though an insect had bitten me. Then a shell, exploding, as it seemed to us, amongst the very cottages where we had just been, startled us.

> “We saved our man,” said the Feldsher, looking at the soldier, “but we’d better move on. It’s uncomfortable here.”

> We picked the thing up and started again, and at once my hand began to slip away from its hold (nightmare sensation exactly). I bent my head down, managed to lick my hand without raising it, and stiffened the muscles of my arm. We were watched, once more, by a million eyes﻿—again I stepped on a head of hair buried somewhere in the ground. Then some voice cried shrilly: “Ah! Ah!”﻿ ﻿… some man hit.

===Chinese===

> 「两个人抬太沉了，」他说。「他是个大块头，」说着望了望那士兵。这时不知什么地方，显然不远，步枪打得正热。噼啪声在空气里迸溅着火花，仿佛人正活在一个电全跑光了的世界里。别的炮火似乎已抽远了，我们面前的*嘭——嘭——嘭*是从山上传来的回声……

> 我们抬起担架，上路了。幸亏河边的这段难走的路是在行程一开头走掉的。我不知道我们是怎么走过来的，从那条没完没了的腿的长列上跨过去，每横挪一步，担架就朝左边一歪，眼看要把我们掀进河里。地面又那么滑，靴子根本咬不住地。担架上的人在说梦话，发出一种在两个音上来回的、不停歇的摇篮曲似的小声音——「呐……呐！呐……呐！呐……呐！」

> 我们不得不两次渡河，木板在我们身子底下压得弯下来，弯到我确信它们就要断了。我的胳膊开始作痛，汗顺着脊梁往下淌。右靴把我的脚后跟磨破了。我们把河甩在了身后，随后，突然，我的右手开始从担架的铁把手上滑脱。

> 「得把它放下来一会儿，」我说。我们把它放在地上，就在同一瞬间，一颗子弹贴着我耳朵唱过去，近得我感到像被虫子叮了一口。接着一发炮弹炸开了——在我们感觉里，正炸在我们刚刚待过的那些农舍中间——吓了我们一跳。

> 「咱们的人是救下来了，」药剂医士望着那士兵说，「不过咱们最好还是挪一挪。这地方待着不舒服。」

> 我们把那东西抬起来，重新上路，我的手立刻又开始从握着的把手上滑走（噩梦感分毫不差）。我低下头，手不离把手，勉强舔了舔手，把胳膊上的肌肉绷紧。我们又一次被一百万只眼睛注视着——我又一脚踩在埋在地里什么地方的哪颗长着头发的脑袋上。随后有个声音尖叫起来：「啊！啊！」……有人中弹了。

===Original===

> Every bone in my body began to ache. I was, of course, rottenly trained, without a sound muscle in my body, and my legs threatened cramp, my heel grated against my boot and sent a stab to my stomach with every movement, my shoulders seemed to pull away from the stretcher as though they would separately rebel against my orders﻿ ﻿… and my hand began again to slip. The Feldsher also began to feel the strain. Once he asked me to stop. He apologised; I could see the sweat pouring down his face: “A very big man’ ” he said.

===Chinese===

> 我全身的骨头都开始作痛。我当然是训练得一塌糊涂，身上没有一块结实的肌肉，两腿直要抽筋，脚后跟蹭着靴子，每动一下就朝我胃里扎进一刀，两个肩膀仿佛要从担架边挣脱出去，仿佛要各自单独反叛我的号令似的……而我的手又开始滑了。药剂医士也开始吃不消了。有一回他要求停下。他表示了歉意；我看得出汗正从他脸上往下泼：「真是个大块头，」他说。

===Original===

> Whether it were the echo, whether my ears had by this time been utterly deafened and confused I do not know, but now the shock and rumble of the cannon seemed to come directly from under my feet. I felt perhaps as though I were on one of those railways that I have seen in London at a fair when the ground shakes and quivers beneath you. It really would not have surprised me had the earth suddenly yawned and swallowed me. Every plague now beset me. My hand refused to hold the stretcher, my body was wet with perspiration, my face was for some reason covered with mud.﻿ ﻿… There was a snap and my braces burst. My belt was loose and my trousers, as though they had waited for their opportunity, slipped down over my knees. I felt the cold night wind on my flesh. Neither decency nor comfort mattered to me now﻿—I would have walked gladly naked through the world. The Feldsher was making a grinding noise between his teeth. I was no longer conscious of shell or bullets. I heard no noise. I was aware of neither light nor darkness. I could not have told my name had anyone asked me it. I did not recognise trees nor houses, nor was I at all aware that with a muddy face and my trousers down to my knees I was a strange figure. I was aware of one thing only﻿—that I must keep my right hand on the stretcher. My left behaved decently enough, but my right was a rebel. I felt a personal fury against it, as though I said to it: “Ah! but I’ll punish you when I get back!” I with all my mental consciousness “willed” it to remain on the handle. It slipped. I drove it back. It slipped further, it was almost gone.﻿ ﻿… With a supreme effort I drove it back again, “I *will* fall off,” said my hand. “You shall *not*,” said I. “I have!” cried my hand triumphantly. “Back!” I swore, driving it.

===Chinese===

> 也不知是回声的缘故，还是我的耳朵到这时已经彻底震聋、搅乱了，反正此刻炮火的震动与轰鸣仿佛径直从我脚底下传来。我的感觉，也许就像我在伦敦庙会上见过的那种小铁路——地面在你身子底下发抖、震颤。大地倘若突然裂开大口把我吞下去，也真不会让我吃惊。这时种种灾祸一齐来折磨我。我的手不肯握担架了，我的身子被汗浸透，我的脸不知怎么糊满了泥。……啪的一声，我的背带绷断了。腰带松了，我的裤子，仿佛早就在等这个机会，一下子褪到了膝盖底下。冰凉的夜风吹在我肉上。体面也罢，舒适也罢，此刻我全不在乎——我就是赤条条走遍世界也甘愿。药剂医士牙齿缝里磨出咯咯的响声。我已意识不到炮弹和子弹。我听不见任何声响。明也好暗也好，我一概不觉。谁若在这时问我叫什么，我也答不上来。我认不出树，也认不出房子，全然不觉得自己糊着一张泥脸、裤子褪到膝头是个多么古怪的模样。我只意识到一件事——我的右手必须留在担架上。我的左手倒还算听话，右手却是个叛徒。我对它生出一股切齿的私愤，仿佛对它说：「好啊！回去再跟你算账！」我用全部的心神「命令」它待在把手上。它滑了。我把它赶回去。它又滑远些，几乎要全滑掉了……我拼出全力再把它赶回去。「我*偏要*掉下去，」我的手说。「你*不许*，」我说。「我掉下去了！」我的手得意洋洋地喊。「回去！」我骂着，把它按回去。

===Original===

> We were now, I believe, both stumbling along, the wounded man pitching from side to side. Of the rest of our journey I have the most confused memory. The firing had no longer any effect upon me. I was thinking of my rebellious hand, my aching heel, and the irritation of my trousers clustered about my legs. Another step and I shall fall! I thought.﻿ ﻿… I shall sleep. I heard, from a great distance as it seemed, the soldier’s “Na﻿ ﻿… Na! Na﻿ ﻿… na!” I replied to him as a nurse to her child. “Na﻿ ﻿… na! Na﻿ ﻿… na!”﻿ ﻿… Then I heard Nikitin’s voice.﻿ ﻿…

> Half an hour after my adventure I was watching the dawn flood the sky from the little garden at the back of the cottage. It seemed that those stretchers are really heavy things for any two men to carry.﻿ ﻿… We had been three hours on our journey!

> Well﻿—I sat in the garden watching the sun rise. To my right were four dead men neatly laid out in a row under a tree. Their faces had not been covered but their eyes were closed, their cheeks, hands, and feet like wax. In front of them the young man who had sat on the stove in the kitchen all night and watched us at work was mowing the tall grass with a scythe. He was going to dig graves. He wore a white shirt and white trousers and had long black hair.

===Chinese===

> 我相信，这时我们俩都已跌跌撞撞，担架上的伤员从这边颠到那边。剩下的那段路，我的记忆乱作一团。炮火对我已经不起任何作用。我想着我那只造反的手，想着作痛的脚跟，想着堆在腿边碍事的裤子。再一步我就要摔倒了！我想。……我要睡着了。我听见，仿佛从极远极远的地方，传来那士兵的「呐……呐！呐……呐！」我像保姆哄孩子似的应着他。「呐……呐！呐……呐！」……随后我听见了尼基京的声音……

> 那场奇遇过后半个钟头，我已在农舍后面的小花园里，望着曙色漫上天空。看来那些担架，无论交给哪两个人抬，都实在是重东西。……我们在路上走了三个钟头！

> 唉——我坐在花园里看日出。我右边是四具死人，整整齐齐一排躺在树下。他们的脸没有蒙上，眼睛却已闭拢，面颊、双手和双脚像蜡一样。他们前面，那个在厨房炉顶上蹲了整夜、望着我们干活的青年，正挥着长柄镰割高草。他要去挖墓穴。他穿着白衬衫、白裤子，留着长长的黑发。

===Original===

> “Why didn’t they take you for a soldier?” I asked him.

> “Consumptive,” he said.

> I had washed my face, hitched up my trousers. I sat on the trunk of a tree, watched the dew on the grass and the faint blue like the colour of a bird’s egg flood the sky, staining it pale yellow. All firing had utterly ceased. There was not a sound except the birds in the trees who were beginning to sing. A soldier, a fine grave figure with a black beard, was washing in a little pool at the end of the garden. He was naked save for his white drawers. There was, I repeat, not a sound. Our cottage looked so peaceful﻿—smoke coming from the chimney. No sign of the shambles, no sign except the four dead men, all so grave and quiet. The blue in the sky grew deeper. Then the sun rose, a jolly gold ball with red clouds swinging in streamers away from it.

===Chinese===

> 「他们怎么没征你去当兵？」我问他。

> 「有痨病，」他说。

> 我洗了脸，把裤子拽了上来。我坐在一段树干上，望着草上的露水，望着那种像鸟蛋壳颜色的淡淡蓝色漫上天空，把它染成浅黄。一切炮火都彻底停歇了。除了树上开始歌唱的鸟，没有一点声响。一个士兵，一位气度堂堂、神情庄重的黑胡子汉子，正在花园尽头的一个小水洼里洗浴。他一丝不挂，只穿一条白色衬裤。我再说一遍，没有一点声响。我们的农舍看上去那么安宁——烟囱里冒着烟。屠宰场的痕迹一点也没有了，一点痕迹也没有，除了那四个死人，个个那么庄重、那么安静。天上的蓝色越来越深。随后太阳升起来了，一颗快活的金球，红色的云彩像绶带一样从它那里飘荡开去。

===Original===

> The birds sang above my head so loudly that the boy who was mowing looked up at them. The soldier finished his washing, put on his shirt. He was a Muhammadan, I perceived, because he prayed, very solemnly, his face to the sun, bowing to the ground. The grass fell before the flashing scythe, the sun flamed behind the trees, and I was happy as I had never known happiness in my life before.

> I had done only what all the soldiers are doing every day of their lives. I had been only where they always were.﻿ ﻿… But I felt that I need never be afraid again. Everyone knows how an early summer morning can give one confidence; in my happiness, God forgive me, I thought that my struggles were at an end, that I had met my enemy and defeated him﻿ ﻿… that I was worthy and able to defend Marie.

> These things may seem foolish now when one knows what followed them, but the happiness of that morning at least was real. Perhaps all over Europe there were men, at that moment, happy as I was, because they had proved something to themselves. Then Nikitin called to me, laughing.

===Chinese===

> 鸟儿在我头顶唱得那么响，连割草的那个少年都抬头望它们。那士兵洗完了，穿上衬衫。我看出他是个穆斯林，因为他祷告起来了，面朝太阳，叩拜到地，极庄重。青草在闪亮的镰刀前倒下，太阳在树后燃烧，而我快活极了，生平从不曾知道这样的快活。

> 我做的，不过是所有士兵天天都在做的事。我去的，不过是他们一直在的地方。……可我觉得，我再也不会害怕了。谁都知道，初夏的清晨能给人怎样的信心；在我的快活里，愿上帝饶恕我，我以为我的挣扎已经到头，我已同我的敌人照面并打败了他……以为我配得上、也有能力保卫玛丽了。

> 如今知道了后来发生的事，这些也许显得可笑，但那个早晨的幸福至少是真的。也许就在那一刻，全欧洲都有人同我一样快活，因为他们向自己证明了一件事。随后尼基京笑着喊我。

===Original===

> “Tea, Mr. and *bulki*3 and sausage?”

> “All right, I’m coming,” I answered. “Listen, *golubchik*,” I called to the soldier. “Bring me some water in your kettle. I’ll wash my hands.”

> He came, smiling, towards me.

> I have given the incidents of this night in great detail for my own satisfaction, because I wish to forget nothing. To others the little adventure must seem trivial, but to myself it represented the climax of a chain of events.

===Chinese===

> 「茶，先生，再来点 *bulki*〔3〕和香肠？」

> 「好，就来，」我应道。「听着，*golubchik*，」我朝那士兵喊，「用你的水壶给我打点水来。我要洗洗手。」

> 他微笑着朝我走来。

> 我把这一夜的种种情事写得如此详尽，是为我自己的满足，因为我不愿忘掉任何一点。在别人眼里，这场小小的奇遇想必显得微不足道，可在我自己，它是一连串事件的顶点。

## Part I / 第一部

## Part II / 第二部

## I Spring in the Train / 一 火车上的春天

===Original===

His was the first figure to catch my eye that evening in Petrograd; he stood under the dusky lamp in the vast gloomy Warsaw station, with exactly the expression that I was afterwards to know so well, impressed not only upon his face but also upon the awkwardness of his arms that hung stiffly at his side, upon the baggy looseness of his trousers at the knees, the unfastened straps of his long black military boots. His face, with its mild blue eyes, straggly fair moustache, expressed anxiety and pride, timidity and happiness, apprehension and confidence. He was in that first moment of my sight of him as helpless, as unpractical, and as anxious to please as any lost dog in the world—and he was also as proud as Lucifer. I knew him at once for an Englishman; his Russian uniform only accented the cathedral-town, small public-school atmosphere of his appearance. He was exactly what I had expected. He was not, however, alone, and that surprised me. By his side stood a girl, obviously Russian, wearing her Sister’s uniform with excitement and eager anticipation, her eyes turning restlessly from one part of the platform to another, listening with an impatient smile to the remarks of her companion.

From where I stood I could hear his clumsy, hesitating Russian and her swift, preoccupied replies. I came up to them.

===Chinese===

那天晚上在彼得格勒（Petrograd），头一个映入我眼帘的便是他的身影。他站在昏暗阴沉的华沙车站里一盏幽暗的灯下，带着那副我日后熟得不能再熟的神情——那神情不仅印在他脸上，也印在他两条僵硬地垂在身侧、不知往哪儿放才好的胳膊的笨拙上，印在他膝头松松垮垮的裤腿上，印在他那双黑色长军靴没有系紧的靴扣带上。他的脸，配上温和的蓝眼睛和稀稀拉拉的浅色小胡子，既流露着焦虑又流露着骄傲，既胆怯又幸福，既惴惴不安又信心十足。我第一眼望见他时，他就像世上任何一条走失的狗一样孤立无援、不通世务、一门心思只想讨人欢喜——可他同时又骄傲得赛似魔王路西法。我一眼便认出他是个英国人；他身上那套俄国军装，反倒把他那股教堂小城、小公学的气派衬托得更分明了。他同我预想的分毫不差。然而他并非独自一人，这倒叫我意外。他身边站着一个姑娘，一望便知是俄国人，穿着一身护士制服，整个人透着兴奋和急切的期待，一双眼睛不安分地从月台的这一头转到那一头，带着不耐烦的微笑听她的同伴说话。

从我站的地方，听得见他那笨拙迟疑的俄语和她飞快的、心不在焉的回答。我朝他们走了过去。

===Original===

“Mr. Trenchard?” I asked.

He blushed, stammered, held out his hand, missed mine, blushed the more, laughed nervously.

“I’m glad … I knew … I hope. …”

===Chinese===

「特伦查德（Trenchard）先生？」我问。

他脸红了，结结巴巴，伸出手来，却没碰上我的手，脸红得更厉害，又局促不安地笑起来。

「真高兴……我就知道……希望……」

===Original===

I could feel that the girl’s eyes were upon me with all the excited interest of one who is expecting that every moment of her new wonderful experience will be of a stupendous, even immortal quality.

“I am Sister Marie Ivanovna, and you are, of course, Mr. Durward,” she said. “They are all waiting for you—expecting you—you’re late, you know!” She laughed and moved forward as though she would accompany me to the group by the train. We went to the train together.

“I should tell you,” she said quickly and suddenly with nervousness, “that we are engaged, Mr. Trenchard and I—only last night. We have been working at the same hospital. … I don’t know anyone,” she continued in the same intimate, confiding whisper. “I would be frightened terribly if I were not so excited. Ah! there’s Anna Mihailovna. … I know *her*, of course. It was through, her aunt—the one who’s on Princess Soboleff’s train—that I had the chance of going with you. Oh! I’m so happy that I had the chance—if I hadn’t had it. …”

===Chinese===

我感觉得到，那姑娘的眼睛正打量着我，满怀着一种兴奋的好奇——她正指望着，这桩新奇美妙经历里的每一刻，都将是惊天动地、甚至不朽的。

「我是玛丽·伊万诺夫娜（Marie Ivanovna）护士，您呢，自然是德沃德（Durward）先生了，」她说。「大家全在等您——盼着您——您可来晚啦！」她笑着往前走，好像要陪我一同到列车旁那群人那儿去。我们便一道朝列车走去。

「有件事我该告诉您，」她忽然急促而紧张地说，「特伦查德先生和我订婚了——就在昨天晚上。我们一直在同一家医院工作……这儿我谁也不认识，」她仍旧用那种亲昵知心的耳语接着说。「要不是我兴奋成这样，早就吓坏啦。啊！那边是安娜·米哈伊洛夫娜（Anna Mihailovna）……*她*我当然认得。就是托了她姑妈的福——在索博列夫公主（Princess Soboleff）专列上服务的那一位——我才得到跟你们同行的机会。哦，我真高兴得了这个机会——要是我没得到它……」

===Original===

We were soon engulfed now. I drew a deep breath and surrendered myself. The tall, energetic figure of Anna Mihailovna, the lady to whose practical business gifts and unlimited capacity for compelling her friends to surrender their last bow and button in her service we owed the existence of our Red Cross unit, was to be seen like a splendid flag waving its followers on to glory and devotion. We *were* devoted, all of us. Even I, whose second departure to the war this was, had after the feeblest resistance surrendered myself to the drama of the occasion. I should have been no gentleman had I done otherwise.

After the waters had closed above my head for, perhaps, five minutes of strangled, half-protesting, half-willing surrender I was suddenly compelled, by what agency I know not, to struggle to the surface, to look around me, and then quite instantly to forget my immersion. The figure of Trenchard, standing exactly as I had left him, his hands uneasily at his sides, a half-anxious, half-confident smile on his lips, his eyes staring straight in front of him, absolutely compelled my attention. I had forgotten him, we had all forgotten him, his own lady had forgotten him. I withdrew from the struggling, noisy group and stepped back to his side. It was then that, as I now most clearly remember, I was conscious of something else, was aware that there was a strange faint blue light in the dark clumsy station, a faint throbbing glow, that, like the reflection of blue water on a sunlit ceiling, hovered and hung above the ugly shabbiness of the engines and trucks, the rails with scattered pieces of paper here and there, the iron arms that supported the vast glass roof, the hideous funnel that hung with its gaping mouth above the water-tank. The faint blue light was the spring evening—the spring evening that, encouraged by God knows what brave illusion, had penetrated even these desperate fastnesses. A little breeze accompanied it and the dirty pieces of paper blew to and fro; then suddenly a shaft of light quivered upon the blackness, quivered and spread like a golden fan, then flooded the huge cave with trembling ripples of light. There was even, I dare swear, at this safe distance, a smell of flowers in the air.

===Chinese===

不一会儿我们便被卷了进去。我深深吸了一口气，把自己交了出去。安娜·米哈伊洛夫娜那高大而精力充沛的身影——正是靠了这位太太务实的办事才干，靠了她那副能逼得朋友们为她效劳到交出最后一根领结、最后一粒纽扣的无穷本事，我们这支红十字队伍才得以存在——此刻正像一面壮丽的大旗，招引着她的追随者奔向荣耀与献身。我们*确乎*人人都在献身。连我——这已是我第二回开赴战场——也在象征性地稍作抵抗之后，便向这一场面的戏剧性缴了械。换个人也只好如此，否则就算不得一个体面人了。

大水没过我的头顶，也许有五分钟之久——在那半推半就、又憋又呛的沉溺里——忽然，不知被什么力量驱使，我挣扎着浮上水面，朝四下里张望，随即又把这一番沉溺忘了个干净。特伦查德的身影，仍旧分毫不差地站在我离开他时的老地方，双手不安地垂在身侧，唇边挂着半是焦急、半是自信的微笑，两眼直直地望着前方——这身影牢牢攫住了我的注意。我把他忘了，我们全都把他忘了，连他自己的意中人也把他忘了。我从那推推搡搡、喧喧嚷嚷的一群人里退出来，走回他身边。就在那时——如今我记得再清楚不过——我意识到了另一样东西，意识到这黑暗笨重的车站里有一道奇异的、淡淡的蓝光，一阵微微搏动的辉光，像日光映照的天花板上晃动的蓝色水影，悬浮在机车和车皮的丑陋寒碜之上，悬浮在四处散落着纸片的铁轨之上，悬浮在支撑着巨大玻璃棚顶的铁臂之上，悬浮在那只大张着口吊在水柜上的难看烟囱之上。这道淡淡的蓝光，是春夜——是不知受了何等勇敢的错觉的鼓舞、竟一直渗进这些绝望堡垒里来的春夜。一阵微风伴着它，肮脏的纸片被吹得来回飞舞；随后突然有一道亮光在黑暗中颤动，颤动着，展开来，像一柄金色的折扇，随即以微微战栗的光的涟漪，漫满了这个巨大的洞穴。我敢起誓，在这安全的距离上，空气中甚至还飘着一丝花香。

===Original===

“It’s a most lovely …” Trenchard said, smiling at me, “spring here … I find. …”

I was compelled by some unexpected sense of fatherly duty to be practical.

“You’ve got your things?” I said. “You’ve found your seat?”

“Well, I didn’t know …” he stammered.

“Where are they?” I asked him.

===Chinese===

「这真是个顶可爱的……」特伦查德朝我微笑着说，「春天在这儿……我觉得……」

一种出乎意料的父亲般的责任感，逼着我讲起实际来。

「您的行李都齐了？」我说。「座位找着了吗？」

「呃，我原先不知道……」他结结巴巴地说。

「东西在哪儿？」我问他。

===Original===

He was not quite sure where they were. He stood, waving his hands, whilst the golden sunlight rippled over his face. I was suddenly irritated.

“But please,” I said, “there isn’t much time. Four of us men have a compartment together. Just show me where your things are and then I’ll introduce you.” He seemed reluctant to move, as though the spot that he had chosen was the only safe one in the whole station; but I forced him forward, found his bags, had them placed in their carriage, then turned to introduce him to his companions.

Anna Mihailovna had said to me: “This detachment will be older than the last. Doctor Nikitin—he’ll take that other doctor’s place, the one who had typhus—and Andrey Vassilievitch—you’ve known him for years. He talks a great deal but he’s sympathetic and such a good business man. He’ll be useful. Then there’s an Englishman; I don’t know much about him, except that he’s been working for three months at the English Hospital. He’s not a correspondent, never written a line in his life. I only saw him for a moment, but he seemed sympathetic. …”

===Chinese===

他自己也说不清行李在哪儿。他站在那里挥动着双手，金色的阳光在他脸上流漾。我突然来了一股火气。

「可是拜托，」我说，「时间不多了。我们四个男人合用一个包厢。您只消指给我东西在哪儿，回头我好把您引见给大家。」他似乎不愿挪动，仿佛他选中的那块地方是全车站唯一安全的一角；可我硬推着他往前走，找到了他的行李，叫人安置到车厢里，然后转身把他引见给他的同伴们。

安娜·米哈伊洛夫娜先前对我说过：「这一批人要比上一批老成些。尼基京（Nikitin）医生——他来顶替原先那位医生，就是得了斑疹伤寒的那位——还有安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇（Andrey Vassilievitch）——您认识他好些年了。他话多，为人却热络，又是个顶呱呱的生意人。会派得上用场的。另外还有个英国人；我不大了解他，只知道他在英国医院干了三个月。他不是记者，这辈子一行字也没写过。我只同他照过一面，不过人看上去挺热络的……」

===Original===

Anna Mihailovna, as is well known to all of us, finds everyone sympathetic simply because she has so much to do and so many people to see that she has no time to go deeply into things. If you have no time for judging character you must have some good common rule to go by. I had known little Andrey Vassilievitch for some years and had found him tiresome. Finally, I did not care about the possibility of an Englishman. Perhaps I had wished (through pride) to remain the only Englishman in our *Otriad*. I had made friends with them all, I was at home with them. Another Englishman might transplant me in their affections. Russians transfer, with the greatest ease, their emotions from one place to another; or he might be a failure and so damage my country’s reputation. Some such vain and stupid prejudice I had. I know that I looked upon our new additions with disfavour.

There, at any rate, Dr. Nikitin and little Andrey Vassilievitch were, and a strange contrast they made. Nikitin’s size would have compelled attention anywhere, even in Russia, which is, of course, a country of big men. It was not only that he was tall and broad; the carriage of his head, the deep blackness of his beard, his eyebrows, his eyes, the sure independence with which he held himself, as though he were indifferent to the whole world (and that I know that he was), must anywhere have made him remarked and remembered. He looked now immensely fine in his uniform, which admirably suited him. He stood, without his greatcoat, his hand on his sword, his eyes half-closed as though he were almost asleep, and a faint half-smile on his face as though he were amused at his thoughts. I remember that my first impression of him was that he was so completely beneath the domination of some idea or remembrance that, at that moment, no human being could touch him. When I took Trenchard up to him I was so conscious of his remoteness that I was embarrassed and apologetic.

===Chinese===

众所周知，安娜·米哈伊洛夫娜看谁都热络，无非因为她要办的事太多，要见的人太多，根本没有工夫往深里探究。既然没工夫品评人的品性，就得有一条可靠的大致规矩可循。我认识小个子安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇已有几年，觉得他有点烦人。再说，添一个英国人的可能，我也不稀罕。或许我（出于虚荣）巴不得自己仍旧是我们*医疗支队*（Otriad）里唯一的英国人。我同他们大家都交上了朋友，在他们中间如鱼得水。再来一个英国人，说不定会在他们的感情里把我挤走。俄国人的感情最容易从一个地方挪到另一个地方；再不然，他也许是个不中用的，反倒败坏我祖国的名声。我心里揣着的就是这么一种虚荣而愚蠢的偏见。我知道，我是带着不痛快看待我们队里这几张新面孔的。

那边，尼基京医生和小个子安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇好歹是到了，两人往一处一站，对比再奇特不过。尼基京的那副身量，无论在哪里都逼得人非注目不可，即便在俄国——那自然是个多彪形大汉的国度——也不例外。况且还不仅仅是他高大宽阔；他昂首的姿态、胡须眉毛眼睛的浓黑、他立身处世时那种笃定的独立劲儿，仿佛全世界的兴衰都与他无干（这一点我知道他真是如此）——无论在哪里，这些都必然使他引人注目、教人过目不忘。这会儿他穿上军装，气派极了，那身军装又衬得他好。他站着，没穿大衣，一只手按在佩剑上，两眼半阖，仿佛快要睡着，脸上挂着一丝若有若无的浅笑，仿佛想什么想得有趣。我记得我对他的第一印象是：他整个儿被某个念头或某段回忆辖制着，此时此刻，世上没有任何人能触碰得了他。我领着特伦查德走到他跟前时，深深觉出他的遥不可及，竟弄得我又窘迫又赔小心。

===Original===

And if I was aware of Nikitin’s remoteness I was equally conscious of Andrey Vassilievitch’s proximity. He was a little man of a round plump figure; he wore a little imperial and sharp, inquisitive moustaches; his hair was light brown and he was immensely proud of it. In Petrograd he was always very smartly dressed. He bought his clothes in London and his plump hands had a movement familiar to all his friends, a flicker of his hands to his coat, his waistcoat, his trousers, to brush off some imaginary speck of dust. It was obvious now that he had given very much thought to his uniform. It fitted him perfectly, his epaulettes glittered, his boots shone, his sword was magnificent, but he looked, in spite of all his efforts, exactly what he was, a rich successful merchant; never was there anyone less military. He had dressed up, one might suppose, for some fancy-dress ball.

I could see at once that he was ill at ease, anxious as ever to please everyone, to like everyone, to be liked in return, but unable, because of some thought that troubled him, to give his whole attention to this business of pleasing.

He greeted me with a warmth that was really genuine although he bestowed it upon his merest acquaintances. His great dream in life was a universal popularity—that everyone should love him. At any rate at that time I thought that to be his dream—I know now that there was something else.

===Chinese===

如果说尼基京的遥不可及我看在眼里，那么安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇的近在眼前我同样感觉得到。他是个圆滚滚、胖墩墩的小个子；下巴蓄一撮山羊胡，唇上两撇尖尖的、爱打探的小胡子；头发是浅棕色的，他为之得意得不得了。在彼得格勒，他一向穿得十分入时。他的衣裳都在伦敦置办；他那一双胖手有个动作，朋友们个个熟悉——手往大衣上、背心上、裤子上飞快地一拂，掸去一粒子虚乌有的灰尘。眼下这身军装，他显然是下了大工夫琢磨的。军装合身极了，肩章锃亮，皮靴闪光，佩剑富丽堂皇，可是任凭他怎么费尽心机，他看上去还是他那副本相——一个发家致富的阔商人；天下再没有比他更不像军人的了。人家还当他是装扮起来去赴什么化装舞会呢。

我一眼便看得出他心神不宁。他一如既往地急于讨好每一个人、喜欢每一个人、也受人喜欢，可是因为有桩心事搅着他，没法把全副心思放在讨好这门功课上。

他同我寒暄，热络得发自肺腑——尽管这份热络，他对泛泛之交也一概奉送。他人生的大梦是人见人爱——盼着人人都爱他。至少那时候我以为这就是他的梦——如今我知道，他心里还另有蹊跷。

===Original===

“But Ivan Petrovitch! … This is delightful! Here we all are! What pleasure! Thank God, we’re all here, no delays, nothing unfortunate. An Englishman? … Indeed, I am very glad! Your friend speaks Russian? Not very much, but enough? … You know Vladimir Stepanovitch? Dr. Nikitin … my friend Meester Durward. Also Meester? … ah, I beg your pardon, Tronsart. Two Englishmen in our Otriad … the alliance, yes, delightful!”

Nikitin slowly opened his eyes, shook hands with me and with Trenchard, said that he was glad to see us and was silent again. Trenchard stammered and blushed, said something in very bad Russian, then glanced anxiously, with an eager light in his mild blue eyes, in the direction of the excited crowd that chattered and stirred about the train. There was something, in that look of his, that both touched and irritated me. What does he come for? I thought to myself. With his bad Russian and his English prejudices. Of course he’ll be lonely and then he’ll be in everyone’s way.

I could remember, readily enough, some of the loneliness of those first months of my own, when both war and the Russians had differed so from my expectations. This fellow looked just the figure for high romantic pictures. He had, doubtless, seen Russia in the colours of the pleasant superficial books of travel that have of late, in England, been so popular, books that see in the Russian a blessed sort of idiot unable to read or write but vitally conscious of God, and in Russia a land of snow, icons, mushrooms and pilgrims. Yes, he would be disappointed, unhappy, and tiresome. Upon myself would fall the chief burden of his trouble—I should have enough upon my shoulders without him.

===Chinese===

「哎呀，伊万·彼得罗维奇（Ivan Petrovitch）！……这可太好啦！我们全到齐啦！多开心！谢天谢地，人都在，一个没误，一件倒霉事儿也没有。一位英国人？……哎呀，我真高兴！您这位朋友会讲俄语？讲得不多，可也够用了？……您认识弗拉基米尔·斯捷潘诺维奇（Vladimir Stepanovitch）吧？尼基京医生……我的朋友米斯特·德沃德。也是米斯特？……啊，恕我失言，特龙萨尔（Tronsart）。我们支队里两位英国人……同盟嘛，是啊，妙极啦！」

尼基京慢慢睁开眼睛，同我、同特伦查德握了握手，说了句见到我们很高兴，便又不作声了。特伦查德结结巴巴，红着脸，用很蹩脚的俄语说了句什么，随即不安地朝列车四周那群叽叽喳喳、挤来挤去的兴奋人群望了一眼，温和的蓝眼睛里透出热切的光。他那一眼里的什么东西，既叫我心软，又叫我恼火。他来干什么呢？我心里想。凭他那蹩脚的俄语，凭他那套英国人的偏见。往后自然是孤孤单单，然后到处碍人的事。

我不难想起自己开头那几个月的孤寂，那时无论战争还是俄国人，都同我的预料大相径庭。这家伙活脱脱是给浪漫巨幅画作当模特的料。毫无疑问，他是照着近来在英国大为流行的那些轻松肤浅的游记来想象俄国的——那些书把俄国人看成一种目不识丁、心里却活泼泼感知着上帝的有福的白痴，把俄国看成一片白雪、圣像、蘑菇和朝圣者之乡。是的，他会失望，会痛苦，会讨人嫌。他的烦恼这副重担，大头准得落在我身上——没有他，我肩上的担子也已经够重了。

===Original===

The golden fan had vanished from the station walls. A dim pale glow, with sparkles as of gold dust shining here and there upon that grimy world, faltered and trembled before the rattle and roar that threatened it. Nevertheless, Spring was with us at our departure. As the bells rang, as the ladies of our Committee screamed and laughed, as Anna Mihailovna showered directions and advice upon us, as we crowded backwards into our compartment before the first jolt of the departing train, Spring was with us … but of course we were all of us too busy to be aware of it.

Nikitin, I remember, reduced us very quickly, for all practical purposes, to a company of three. He lowered one of the upper beds, climbed into it, stretched himself out and lay in silence staring at the carriage-roof. His body was a shadow in the half-light, touched once and again by the gesture of the swinging lamp, that swept him out of darkness and back into it again. The remaining three of us did not during either that evening or the next day make much progress. At times there would of course be tea, and then the two Sisters who were in a compartment close at hand joined us.

===Chinese===

那柄金扇已从车站的墙上消失了。一道昏白的余晖，带着几星金尘似的闪光，星星点点地洒在这个油腻腻的世界上，在威胁着它的轰隆喧嚣前颤颤地摇曳。尽管如此，启程之时，春天仍与我们同在。铃声响起，我们委员会的太太们又叫又笑，安娜·米哈伊洛夫娜把嘱咐和忠告一阵阵泼洒在我们身上，我们在列车启动的头一下颠簸之前倒退着挤进包厢——春天与我们同在……只是我们大家都忙得无暇察觉罢了。

我记得，尼基京很快就把我们实际上削减成了三个人的伙。他放下一张上铺，爬了上去，直挺挺躺开，默不作声地躺着，眼睛盯着车顶。他的身躯在半明半暗里成了一道影子，随着摇晃的挂灯一摆一荡，时而把他从黑暗里扫出来，时而又扫回去。剩下我们三个，当晚也好，第二天也好，都没谈出什么名堂。间或当然要喝茶，这时住在紧邻包厢的两位护士便过来加入我们。

===Original===

Marie Ivanovna, Trenchard’s lady, was quieter than she had been before. Her face, which now seemed younger than ever, wore a look of important seriousness as though she were conscious of the indecency of her earlier excitement. She spoke very little, but no one could be in her presence without feeling the force of her vitality like some hammer, silent but of immense power, beating relentlessly upon the atmosphere. Its effect was the stronger in that one realised how utterly at present she was unable to deal with it. Her very helplessness was half of her power—half of her danger too. She was most certainly not beautiful; her nose was too short, her mouth too large, her forehead, from which her black hair was brushed straight back, too high. Her complexion was pale and when she was confused, excited, or pleased, the colour came into her face in a faint flush that ebbed and flowed but never reached its full glow. Her hands were thin and pale. It was her eyes that made her so young; they were so large and round and credulous, scornful sometimes with the scorn of the very young for all the things in the world that they have not experienced—but young especially in all their urgent capacity for life, in their confidence of carrying through all the demands that the High Gods might make upon them. I knew as I looked at her that at present her eagerness for experience was stronger, by far, than her eagerness for any single human being. I wondered whether Trenchard knew that. He was, beyond discussion, most desperately in love; the love of a shy man who has for so many years wondered and dreamed and finds, when the reality comes to him, that it is more, far more, than he had expected. When she came in to us he sat very quietly by her side and talked, if he talked at all, to the other Sister, a stout comfortable woman with no illusions, no expectations, immense capacity and an intensely serious attitude to food and drink.

===Chinese===

特伦查德的意中人玛丽·伊万诺夫娜，比先前安静了。她那张如今显得比任何时候都年轻的脸，摆出一副郑重其事的严肃神情，仿佛她已意识到先前那阵兴奋有失体统。她话极少，可是只要她在场，谁都会感到她那生命力的重压——像一柄大锤，无声却力道千钧，毫不容情地捶打着四周的空气。这效果所以格外强烈，是因为人们看得出，眼下她全然拿它没办法。她这份无能为力，正是她力量的一半——也是她危险的一半。她肯定算不上漂亮；鼻子太短，嘴太大，那头黑发直直向后拢去的额头又太高。她肤色苍白，一当慌乱、兴奋或是欢喜，脸上便泛起一阵淡淡的红晕，涨了又退，退了又涨，却总也涨不到十足。她的手又瘦又白。使她显得那样年轻的，是她的眼睛；那双眼睛又大又圆，轻信于人，有时又带着极年轻的人对世上一切他们未曾经历之事的轻蔑——但尤其年轻的，是那双眼睛里急于拥抱生活的全部渴望，是那种自信无论诸天神明提出怎样的要求都担当得起的气概。我望着她，心里明白：此刻她对经历的渴求，远比对任何一个人的渴求都要强烈。我不知道特伦查德明不明白这一点。毫无疑问，他正爱得死去活来；那是一个腼腆人的爱情：多年来一直猜想、一直梦想，待到现实真正临头，却发现它比自己所期待的多得多、多得多。她到我们这里来的时候，他安安静静坐在她身旁，要说也是同另一位护士说话——那是个富态安详的女人，没有幻想，不抱期待，能耐极大，对吃吃喝喝抱着极其严肃的态度。

===Original===

Trenchard let his eyes rest upon his lady’s face whenever she was unaware, but I could see that he was desperately anxious not to offend her. His attitude to all women, even to Anna Petrovna, the motherly Sister, was that of a man who has always blundered in their company, who has been mocked, perhaps, for his mistakes. I could see, however, that his pride in his new possession, his pride and his happiness, carried with it an absolute assurance of his security. He had no doubts at all. He seemed, in this, even younger than she.

Through all that long Spring day we wandered on—wandering it seemed as the train picked its way through the fields under a sky of blue thin and fine like glass; through a world so quiet and still that birds and children sang and called as though to reassure themselves that they were not alone. Nothing of the war in all this. At the stations there were officers eating *ztchee* soup and veal and drinking glasses of weak tea, there were endless mountains of hot meat pies; the icons in the restaurants looked down with benignancy and indifference upon the food and the soldiers and beyond the station the light green trees blowing in the little wind; the choruses of the soldiers came from their trains as though it were the very voice of Spring itself. It sounded in the distance like—

> Barinisha Barinisha—Pop.

 Barinisha—Pop.

 So—la, la—la …

 Bar … inisha la.

The bell rang, officers with meat pies in their hands came running across the platform. We swung on again through the green golden day.

===Chinese===

只要她不留神，特伦查德便让目光流连在他意中人的脸上，可我看得出，他提心吊胆，生怕冒犯了她。他对所有女人的态度——连那位母亲般慈爱的护士安娜·彼得罗夫娜（Anna Petrovna）也不例外——是一个在同她们相处时屡屡出丑、说不定还因差错挨过嘲笑的人的态度。然而我看得出，他对自己新到手的这份珍宝的骄傲，他的骄傲和他的幸福，自带着一种万无一失的安全感。他心里没有一丝怀疑。在这一点上，他甚至比她还显得年轻。

整整那漫长的一春日，我们一路信步而行——说是信步，其实是列车在玻璃般纤薄澄澈的蓝天底下的田野间拣着道儿走；穿行在一个如此安宁静谧的世界里，鸟儿和孩子们又唱又叫，像是要让自己确信，他们并不是孤零零的。这一切里没有半点战争的影子。沿路各站，军官们吃着菜汤（*ztchee*）和炖小牛肉，喝着一杯杯淡茶；热气腾腾的肉馅饼堆成了一座座望不到头的山；车站食堂里的圣像，慈祥而漠然地俯视着吃食和士兵们；车站外头，浅绿的树在微风中摇曳；士兵们的合唱声从他们的军车里传出来，仿佛那就是春天本身的声音。远远听去，它像是——

> 巴里尼沙 巴里尼沙——波普。

巴里尼沙——波普。

索——啦，啦——啦……

巴……里尼沙，啦。

铃声响了，手里攥着肉馅饼的军官们一路小跑着穿过月台。我们又摇荡着驶进那绿里泛金的一天里去了。

===Original===

Andrey Vassilievitch of course chattered to us all. It was his way, and after a very brief experience of it one trained oneself to regard it as an inevitable background, like the jerking and smoke of the train, the dust, the shrill Russian voices in the next compartment, the blowing of paper to and fro in the corridor. I very quickly discovered that he was intensely conscious of Nikitin, who scarcely throughout the day moved from his upper bunk. Andrey Vassilievitch handed him his tea, brought his meat pies and sandwiches from the station, and offered him newspapers. He did not, however, speak to him and I was aware that throughout that long day he was never once unconscious of him. His chatter, which was always the most irrepressible thing in the world, had, perhaps, today some direction behind it. For the first time in my long acquaintance with Andrey Vassilievitch he interested me. The little man was distressed by the heat and dirt; his fingers were always flickering about his clothes. He was intensely polite to everyone, especially to Trenchard, paying him many compliments about England and the English. The English were the only “sportsmen” in the world. He had been once in London for a week; it had rained very much, but one afternoon it had been fine, and then what clothes he had seen! But the City! He had been down into the City and was lost in admiration; he had also been lost in practical earnest and had appealed to one of the splendid policemen as to the way to Holborn Viaduct, a name that he was quite unable to pronounce. This incident he told us several times. Meanwhile … he hoped he might ask without offence … what was our Navy doing? Why weren’t our submarines as active as the German submarines? And in France … how many soldiers had we now? He did hope that he was not offending. … He spoke rapidly and indistinctly and much of his conversation Trenchard did not understand; he made some rather stupid replies and Marie Ivanovna laughed.

She spoke English very well, with an accent that was charming. She had had, she said, an English nurse, and then an English governess.

===Chinese===

安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇自然跟我们大家闲扯个不停。他就是这个脾气，稍加领教，人们便学会了把他的絮叨当作一种免不了的背景音，好比列车的颠簸和煤烟、尘土、隔壁包厢里尖利的俄语嗓门、走廊里吹来吹去的纸片。我很快便发现，他对尼基京留神极了——尼基京几乎一整天都没挪过他那格上铺。安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇给他递茶，从车站给他买肉馅饼和三明治，给他递报纸。然而他不同他说话，而我察觉，那漫长的一整天里，他没有一刻忘掉过他。他那天下第一、按捺不住的絮叨，今天背后或许另有一番用意。在我同安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇多年的交往里，他头一回叫我觉得有趣。这小个子给暑热和肮脏折磨得苦不堪言；他的手指头总在衣裳上飞快地拂来拂去。他对人人都客气极了，对特伦查德尤其如此，把英格兰和英国人着实夸了个够。英国人是世界上独一无二的「运动家」。他去过一趟伦敦，待了一个星期；雨下得厉害，可有一个下午天气晴了，那时候他见着的衣裳哟，了不得！还有伦敦城！他下去逛过伦敦城，叹赏得魂儿都没了；他也在实打实的意义上迷了路，只好向一位威风的警察打听去霍尔本高架桥（Holborn Viaduct）的路——那个名字他怎么也念不上来。这段插曲他给我们讲了好几遍。话又说回来……他但愿问一句不算失礼……我们的海军如今在干什么呀？为什么我们的潜艇不像德国潜艇那样活跃？还有法国……我们现在有多少兵？他实在但愿自己没有失礼……他说话又快又含混，他的话特伦查德有大半听不懂；他答了几句相当蠢的话，逗得玛丽·伊万诺夫娜笑起来。

她英语说得很好，带着一种迷人的口音。她说她先后有过一位英国保姆，后来又有一位英国家庭教师。

===Original===

Of course they asked me many questions about the future. Would we be close to the Front? How many versts? Would there be plenty of work, and would we *really* see things? We wanted to be useful, no use going if we were not to be useful. How many Sisters were there then already? Were they “sympathetic”? Was Molozov, the head of the Otriad, an agreeable man? Was he kind, or would he be angry about simply nothing? Who would bandage and who would feed the villagers and who would bathe the soldiers? Were the officers of the Ninth Army pleasant to us? Where? Who? When? The day slipped away, the colours were drawn from the sky, the fields, the hills, the stars came out in their myriads, thickly clustered in ropes, and lakes and coils of light; the air was scented with flowers. The second night passed.

===Chinese===

他们当然拿许多关于将来的问题问过我。我们会离前线很近吗？有多少俄里（verst）？活儿会多吗？我们*当真*能见世面吗？我们想派上用场，若派不上用场，去就没有意思了。那边眼下已经有多少位护士了？她们「热络」吗？支队长官莫洛佐夫（Molozov）是个好相处的人吗？他脾气好吗，还是会无缘无故发火？谁管包扎，谁管给村民们分饭，谁管给士兵们洗澡？第九集团军的军官们对我们友好吗？在哪儿？是谁？什么时候？一天溜了过去，色彩从天空、田野和山峦上一层层抽尽了，星星出来了，千颗万颗，密密簇簇，结成一绳绳、一湖湖、一盘盘的光；空气里浮着花香。第二个夜晚过去了。

===Original===

The greater part of the next day was spent in H——, a snug town with a little park like a clean handkerchief, streets with coloured shops, neat and fresh-painted like toys from a toyshop, little blue trains, statues of bewigged eighteenth-century kings and dukes, and a restaurant, painted Watteau-fashion with bright green groves, ladies in hoops and powder, and long-legged sheep. Here we wandered, five of us. Nikitin told us that he would meet us at the station that evening. He had his own business in the place. The little town was delivered over to the Russian army but seemed happy enough in its deliverance. I have never realised in any place more completely the spirit of bright cheerfulness, and the soldiers who thronged the little streets were as far from alarm and thunder as the painted sheep in the restaurant. Marie Ivanovna was as excited as though she had never been in a town before. She bought a number of things in the little expensive shops—eau de cologne, sweets, an electric lamp, a wristwatch, and some preserved fruit. Trenchard made her presents; she thanked him with a gratitude that made him so happy that he stumbled over his sword more than ever, blushing and pushing his cap back from his head. There are some who might have laughed at him, carrying her parcels, his face flushed, his legs knocking against one another, but it was here, at H——, that, for the first time, I positively began to like him. By the evening when we were assembled in the station again as I looked at him standing, waiting for directions, smiling, hot, untidy and awkward, I knew that I liked him very much indeed. …

===Chinese===

第二天的大半天是在 H—— 度过的。那是座安逸的小城，带着一方手帕般干净的小公园，街上是一爿爿彩色的铺子，齐整洁净，漆色新亮，像玩具店里拿出来的玩具；有小小的蓝色火车，有戴假发的十八世纪国王和公爵们的雕像；还有一家饭馆，墙上画着华托（Watteau）风格的画：鲜绿的树丛、穿裙撑敷粉的贵妇，和长腿的羊。我们五个就在这里闲逛。尼基京告诉我们，他晚上到车站同我们会合。他在此地自有他自己的事。这座小城已整个儿交给俄国军队接管，但对自己的易手倒好像颇为自得。我在任何地方都不曾像在这里这样透彻地领教那股明亮欢快的精神；挤满小街的士兵们，同饭馆里画上的那些羊一样，离惊惶与雷霆有十万八千里。玛丽·伊万诺夫娜兴奋得好像从前从来没有进过城。她在那些价钱昂贵的小铺子里买了一堆东西——花露水、糖果、一只电灯、一块手表，还有些糖渍水果。特伦查德送了她礼物；她向他道谢，那份感激劲儿把他乐得绊在剑上摔跤摔得比先前更勤了，红着脸，把军帽往后脑勺上一推。他替她抱着包裹，脸膛通红，两条腿磕磕绊绊，有些人怕是要笑话他的；可是正是在这里，在 H——，我当真开始喜欢上了他。到了傍晚，当我们重新在车站聚齐，我望着他站在那里等着吩咐，笑着，热汗涔涔，衣衫凌乱，笨手笨脚，我知道自己实在是非常喜欢他了……

===Original===

Our new train overflowed: with the greatest difficulty we secured a small wooden compartment with seats sharp and narrow and a smell of cabbage, bad tobacco, and dirty clothes. The floor was littered with sunflower seeds and the paper wrappings of cheap sweets. The air came in hot stale gusts down the corridor, met the yet closer air of our carriage, battled with it and retired defeated. We flung open the windows and a cloud of dust rose gaily to meet us. The whole of the Russian army seemed to be surging upon the platform; orderlies were searching for their masters, officers shouting for their orderlies, soldiers staggering along under bundles of clothes and rugs and pillows; here a group standing patiently, each man with his blue-painted kettle and on his face that expression of happy, half-amused, half-inquisitive, wholly amiable tolerance which reveals the Russian soldier’s favourite attitude to the world. Two priests with wide dirty black hats, long hair, and soiled grey gowns slowly found their way through the crowd. A bunch of Austrian prisoners in their blue-grey uniform made a strange splash of colour in a corner of the platform, where, very contentedly, they were drinking their tea; someone in the invisible distance was playing the balalaika and every now and then some church bell in the town rang clearly and sharply above the tumult. The thin films of dust, yellow in the evening sun, hovered like golden smoke under the station roof. At last with a reluctant jerk and shiver the train was slowly persuaded to totter into the evening air; the evening scents were again around us, the balalaika, now upon the train, hummed behind us, as we pushed out upon her last night’s journey.

The two Sisters had the seats by the windows; Nikitin curled up his great length in another corner and Andrey Vassilievitch settled himself with much grunting and many exclamations beside him. I and Trenchard sat stiffly on the other side.

===Chinese===

我们的新车挤得满满当当：我们费了九牛二虎之力才占到一间小小的木包厢，座位又硬又窄，一股白菜、劣质烟草和脏衣裳的气味。地板上撒满了葵花子壳和便宜糖果的纸包装。走廊里一阵阵热烘烘的浊气涌进来，同我们车厢里更加浑浊的空气相遇，厮杀一场，败退下去。我们推开窗，一团尘土兴高采烈地迎面扑来。整个俄国军队仿佛都在往月台上涌；勤务兵找他们的长官，军官们喊他们的勤务兵，士兵们抱着一捆捆衣裳、毯子和枕头，踉踉跄跄地走；这里还有一伙人耐心地站着，每人提着他的蓝漆水壶，脸上挂着那种快活的、半是好笑半是好奇、全然和气的宽容神情——那是俄国兵面对世界时最心爱的态度。两位头戴宽大黑帽、长发披散、灰袍污损的神父慢慢从人群中挤过去。一小群穿蓝灰色军装的奥地利俘虏，在月台一角泼出一块古怪的色斑，他们正在那里心满意足地喝着茶；看不见的远处有人在弹巴拉莱卡琴（balalaika），城里哪座教堂的钟不时清脆尖亮地在喧闹之上响起来。傍晚的太阳照得黄蒙蒙的薄薄尘幕，像金色的烟在车站棚顶下浮动。列车终于老大不情愿地一哆嗦、一震颤，被慢慢劝进了傍晚的空气里；夜晚的芬芳又在我们四周了，此刻已上了车的巴拉莱卡琴在我们身后嗡嗡地响，载着我们踏上它最后一夜的旅程。

两位护士坐了靠窗的位子；尼基京把他那大高个儿蜷在另一个角落里，安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇哼哼唧唧、哎呀连声地在旁边安顿下来。我和特伦查德直挺挺地坐在另一边。

===Original===

I had, long ago, accustomed myself to sleep in any position on any occasion, however sudden it might be, and I fancied that I should now, in a moment, be asleep, although I had never, in my long travelling experience, known greater discomfort. I looked at the dim lamp, at the square patch beyond the windows, at Nikitin’s long body, which seemed nevertheless so perfectly comfortable, and at Andrey Vassilievitch’s short fat one, which was so obviously miserably uncomfortable; I smelt the cabbage, the dust, the sunflower seeds; first one bone then another ached, in the centre of my back there was an intolerable irritation; above all, there was in my brain some strange insistent compulsion, as though someone were forcing me to remember something that I had forgotten, or as though again someone were forewarning me of some peril or complication. I had, very distinctly, that impression, so familiar to all of us, of passing through some experience already known: I had seen already the dim lamp, the square patch of evening sky, Nikitin, Andrey Vassilievitch. … I knew that in a moment Trenchard. … He did. … He touched my arm.

“Can you sleep?” he whispered.

“No,” I answered.

“It’s terribly hot, close—smell. … Are you going to sleep?”

“No,” I whispered back again.

“Let us move into the corridor. It will be cooler there.”

There seemed to me quite a new sound of determination and resolve in his voice. His nervousness had left him with the daylight. He led the way out of the carriage, turned down the little seats in the corridor, provided cigarettes.

===Chinese===

我早就练出了本事，无论什么场合、无论多么突然，什么姿势都能睡着。我满以为自己这会儿转眼就能入睡，尽管在我漫长的旅行经历中还从不曾领教过比这更难受的滋味。我望着昏暗的灯，望着窗外那一方块夜空，望着尼基京的长身子——瞧那样子却舒服得很——又望着安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇的短胖身子，一望便知其苦不堪言；我闻着白菜味、尘土味、葵花子味；这根骨头疼过，那根骨头又疼起来，脊梁正中有一处说不出的难受；最要命的是，我脑子里有一种奇怪的、纠缠不休的强迫感，仿佛有人在逼我想起一件忘掉的事，又仿佛有人在预先警告我提防什么祸事或乱子。我清清楚楚地有一种我们人人都熟悉的印象：正经历一件从前已经经历过的事；这盏昏暗的灯、这一方块傍晚的天空、尼基京、安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇，我从前都见过……我知道，转眼就该轮到特伦查德了……果然。……他碰了碰我的胳膊。

「您睡得着吗？」他悄声问。

「睡不着。」我答。

「热得吓人，又闷——还有股味儿……您要睡了吗？」

「不睡。」我又悄声回答。

「我们到走廊去吧。那儿凉快些。」

我觉得他的嗓音里有一种全然新鲜的果断和决绝。他的紧张劲儿已随着白昼一同离开了他。他在头里引路出了包厢，翻下走廊里的小折座，又张罗着递烟。

===Original===

“It isn’t much better here, but we’ll have the window open. It’ll get better. This is really war, isn’t it, being so uncomfortable as this? I feel as though things were really beginning.”

“Well, we shall be there tomorrow night,” I answered him. “I hope you’re not going to be disappointed.”

“Disappointed in what?” His voice was quite sharp as he spoke to me, “You don’t know what I want.”

“I suppose you’re like the rest of us. You want to see what war really is. You want to do some good if you can. You want to be seriously occupied in it to prevent your thinking too much about it. Then, because you’re English, you want to see what the Russians are really like. You’re curious and sympathetic, inquisitive and, perhaps, a little sentimental about it. … Am I right?”

“No, not quite—there are other things. I’d like to tell you. Do you mind,” he said suddenly looking up straight into my face with a confiding smile that was especially his own, “if I talk, if I tell you why I’ve come? I’ve no right, I don’t know you—but I’m so happy tonight that I *must* talk—I’m so happy that I feel as though I shall never get through the night alive.”

===Chinese===

「这儿也好不到哪儿去，不过我们可以把窗子敞开。会好起来的。这才真正是打仗的样子，是不是——受这样的罪？我觉着，一切好像真的要开始了。」

「嗯，明天夜里我们就到了，」我回答他。「我希望您不会失望。」

「失望什么？」他同我说话时嗓音相当尖利，「您不知道我要的是什么。」

「我猜您同我们其余的人一样。您想看看战争到底是什么样子。您想尽力做点好事。您想一门心思扎在里面忙，免得想得太多。再呢，因为您是英国人，您想看看俄国人究竟是什么样的人。您好奇而热心，爱打听，兴许还有点多愁善感……我说得对吗？」

「不，不全是——还有别的原因。我很想告诉您。您介意吗，」他突然抬起头，直直地望着我的脸，带着他特有的那种推心置腹的微笑，「介意我说说话，告诉您我为什么要来吗？我没有这个权利，我同您素不相识——可是今晚我太幸福了，*非*说不可——我幸福得只觉得，自己怕是活不过今夜了。」

===Original===

Of our conversation after this, or rather of *his* talk, excited, eager, intimate and shy, old and wise and very, very young, I remember now, I think, every word with especial vividness. After events were to fix it all in my brain with peculiar accuracy, but his narration had that night of itself its own individual quality. His was no ordinary personality, or, at any rate, the especial circumstances of the time drove it into no ordinary shape, and I believe that never before in all his days had he spoken freely and eagerly to anyone. It was simply tonight his exultation and happiness that impelled him, perhaps also some sense of high adventure that his romantic character would, most inevitably, extract from our expedition and its purposes.

At any rate, I listened, saying a word now and then, whilst the hour grew dark, lit only by the stars, then trembled into a pale dawn overladen with grey dense clouds, which again broke, rolled away, before another shining, glittering morning. I remember that it was broad daylight when we, at last, left the corridor.

“I’m thirty-three,” he said. “I don’t feel it, of course; I seem to be now only just beginning life. I’m a very unpractical person and in that way, perhaps, I’m younger than my age.”

I remember that I said something to him about his, most certainly, appearing younger.

===Chinese===

关于这以后我们的谈话——不如说是*他的*独白，兴奋急切，又亲昵又羞怯，老成睿智，又非常非常年轻——如今我想我还能一字一句、清清楚楚地记得。后来的种种事件，把这一切以异样的精确钉进了我的脑子；可是他那晚的叙述，本身就自有一种独特的气质。他本来就不是个寻常的人，或者无论如何，那个时代的特殊境遇把他揉成了一个不寻常的形状；我相信，他一辈子从来不曾对任何人这样自由而急切地倾吐过。今夜驱使他的，无非是他的狂喜和幸福，或许还有几分崇高历险之感——以他那浪漫的天性，注定要从我们这趟远征及其使命中提炼出这种味道来。

总之，我听着，不时插上一句；其间天色沉黑，只有星光照明，随后又颤巍巍地透出一个灰云密布的惨白黎明，云层重又崩开、滚散，让位给另一个亮闪闪、明晃晃的早晨。我记得，当我们终于离开走廊时，天已大亮。

「我三十三了，」他说。「当然自己不觉得；我好像现在才刚刚开始生活。我这个人非常不切实际，就这一点说，也许我比我的年纪还要年轻。」

我记得我应了他一句，说他看上去确实显年轻。

===Original===

“Most certainly I do. I’m just the same as when I went up to Cambridge and I was then as when I first went to Rugby. Nothing seems to have had any effect upon me—except, perhaps, these last two days. Do you know Glebeshire?” he asked me abruptly.

I said that I had spent one summer there with a reading party.

“Ah,” he answered, smiling, “I can tell, by the way you say that, that you don’t really know it at all. To us Glebeshire people it’s impossible to speak of it so easily. There are Trenchards all over Glebeshire, you know, lots of them. In Polchester, our cathedral town, where I was born, there are at least four Trenchard families. Then in Truxe, at Garth, at Rasselas, at Clinton—but why should I bother you with all this? It’s only to tell you that the Trenchards are simply Glebeshire forever and ever. To a Trenchard, anywhere in the world, Glebeshire is hearth and home.”

“I believe I’ve met,” I said, “your Trenchards of Garth. George Trenchard. … She was a Faunder. They have a house in Westminster. There’s a charming Miss Trenchard with whom I danced.”

“Yes, those are the George Trenchards,” he answered with eagerness and delight, as though I had formed a new link with him. “Fancy your knowing them! How small the world is! My father was a cousin, a first cousin, of George Trenchard’s. The girl—you must mean Millie—is delightful. Katherine, the elder sister, is married now. She too is charming, but in a different, graver way.”

He spoke of them all with a serious lingering pleasure, as though he were summoning them all into the dusty, stuffy corridor, carrying them with him into these strange countries and perilous adventures.

===Chinese===

「确实显年轻。我同上剑桥（Cambridge）那会儿一模一样，而那时又同我初进拉格比（Rugby）公学时一模一样。什么事好像都没在我身上起过作用——也许，除了这两天。您知道格利比郡（Glebeshire）吗？」他突兀地问我。

我说我有一年夏天，曾同一个读书小组一起在那儿住过。

「啊，」他笑着回答，「听您说这话的口气，我就知道您其实根本不了解它。对我们格利比郡人来说，不可能这样轻飘飘地谈论它。您知道，整个格利比郡到处都是特伦查德家的人，多着呢。在波尔切斯特（Polchester），我们的教堂城，我出生的地方——至少就有四家特伦查德。还有特鲁克斯（Truxe）、加斯（Garth）、拉塞拉斯（Rasselas）、克林顿（Clinton）……可我干吗拿这些来烦您呢？我只是想告诉您，特伦查德家世世代代就是格利比郡的人。对任何一个特伦查德来说，无论身在世界的哪个角落，格利比郡都是炉火与家。」

「我想我见过，」我说，「你们加斯的特伦查德家。乔治·特伦查德（George Trenchard）……他太太娘家姓方德（Faunder）吧。他们在威斯敏斯特（Westminster）有一所宅子。家里有位挺可爱的特伦查德小姐，我同她跳过舞。」

「对，那就是乔治·特伦查德家，」他急切而欣喜地回答，仿佛我这一下同他结上了一条新的纽带。「想不到您认识他们！这世界真小啊！我父亲是乔治·特伦查德的堂兄弟，嫡亲的堂兄弟。那位姑娘——您说的一定是米莉（Millie）——可爱极了。她姐姐凯瑟琳（Katherine）如今出嫁了。她也很可爱，只是可爱得不一样，更端庄些。」

他说起他们大家，带着一种郑重而缠绵的欢喜，仿佛正把他们一个个召唤进这条尘土飞扬、闷热的走廊，带着他们同他一起进入这些陌生的国度、凶险的历险。

===Original===

“They always laughed at me—Millie especially; I’ve stayed sometimes with them at Garth. But I didn’t mean really to talk about *them*—I only wanted to show you how deeply Glebeshire matters to the Trenchards, and whatever happens, wherever a Trenchard goes, he always really takes Glebeshire with him. I was born in Polchester, as I said. My father had a little property there, but we always lived in a little round bow-windowed house in the Cathedral Close. I was simply brought up on the Cathedral. From my bedroom windows I looked on the whole of it. In our drawing-room you could hear the booming of the organ. I was always watching the canons crossing the cathedral green, counting the strokes of the cathedral bell, listening to the cawing of the cathedral rooks, smelling the cathedral smell of cold stone, wet umbrellas and dusty hassocks, looking up at the high tower and wondering whether anywhere in the world there was anything so grand and fine. My moral world, too, was built on the cathedral—on the cathedral ‘don’ts’ and ‘musts,’ on the cathedral hours and the cathedral prayers, and the cathedral ambitions and disappointments. My father’s great passion was golf. He was not a religious man. But my mother believed in the cathedral with a passion that was almost a disease. She died looking at it. Her spirit is somewhere round it now, I do believe.”

===Chinese===

「他们老是笑话我——米莉尤其厉害；我有时到加斯他们家住些日子。不过我原不是真要谈*他们*——我只是想让您看看，格利比郡在特伦查德家人的心里扎的根有多深；无论出了什么事，无论一个特伦查德走到哪里，他其实总是把格利比郡随身带走的。我生在波尔切斯特，刚才说过。我父亲在那儿有点产业，可我们一直住在教堂区（Cathedral Close）里一幢带圆弓形凸窗的小房子里。我简直是大教堂给喂大的。从我卧室的窗口望出去，整座教堂尽收眼底。在我们的客厅里，听得见管风琴的嗡嗡声。我总是望着教士们走过教堂草坪，数着大教堂的钟敲了几响，听着教堂白嘴鸦的啼叫，闻着大教堂那股冷石头、湿雨伞和积灰跪垫的气味，仰起头望着那座高塔，心里纳闷世上还有没有这么雄伟、这么美好的东西。我的道德世界也是建在大教堂上的——建在大教堂的『不可』与『务须』上，建在大教堂的时辰与祷文上，建在大教堂式的抱负与失意上。我父亲最大的嗜好是打高尔夫。他不是个虔诚的人。可我母亲信大教堂信得几乎成了一种病。她是望着它咽气的。我相信，她的魂灵此刻就在它近旁什么地方。」

===Original===

He paused, then went on:

“It was the cathedral that made me so unpractical, I suppose. I who am an only child—I believed implicitly in what I was told and it always was my mother who told me everything.”

He was, I thought, the very simplest person to whom I had ever listened. The irritation that I had already felt on several occasions in his company again returned. “My father’s great passion was golf” would surely in the mouth of another have had some tinge of irony.

In Trenchard’s mild blue eyes irony was an incredible element. I could fancy what he would have to say to the very gentlest of cynics; some of the sympathy I had felt for him during the afternoon had left me.

He’s very little short of an idiot, I thought. He’s going to be dreadfully in the way.

===Chinese===

他顿了顿，又接着说：

「我想，正是大教堂把我弄得这样不切实际。我是个独生子——人家告诉我什么，我就一概笃信不疑，而告诉我一切的，从来都是我母亲。」

我想，他是我平生听人说话里头一个顶顶简单的人。先前几次同他相处时起过的那股火气，又回来了。「我父亲最大的嗜好是打高尔夫」这句话，若出自身一个人之口，准会带上几分讥诮。

在特伦查德温和的蓝眼睛里，讥诮是一种不可思议的成分。我简直可以想见，他面对最最温和的玩世不恭者会说些什么；下午我对他的那点好感，已经跑掉几分了。

他简直同白痴相差无几，我心里想。往后他可要大大地碍事了。

===Original===

“I was the only child, you see,” he continued. “Of course I was a great deal to my mother and she to me. We were always together. I don’t think that even when I was very young I believed all that she told me. She seemed to me always to take everything for granted. Heaven to me was so mysterious and she had such definite knowledge. I always liked things to be indefinite … I do still.” He laughed, paused for a moment, but was plainly now off on his fine white horse, charging the air, to be stopped by no mortal challenge. I had for a moment the thought that I would slip from my seat and leave him; I didn’t believe that he would have noticed my absence; but the thought of that small stuffy carriage held me.

But he *was* conscious of me; like the Ancient Mariner he fixed upon my arm his hand and stared into my eyes:

===Chinese===

「您知道，我是独生子，」他接着说。「当然，我母亲拿我当命根子，我也拿她当命根子。我们总是在一起。我想，即便在我很小的时候，我也不全信她告诉我的一切。在我看来，她总是把样样事情想成理所当然。天堂在我眼里那么神秘，她却有那样确凿的知识。我一向喜欢凡事含含糊糊……如今还是这样。」他笑了，停了片刻，可这会儿分明已跨上他那匹神骏的白马，对着空气冲杀起来，任凭什么凡人的诘问也拦他不住了。有一瞬间我想从座位上溜走，撇下他不管；我想他多半不会发觉我不在；可一想到那间又小又闷的车厢，我便留住了。

可他*倒是*知道有我这个人；像柯勒律治笔下的老水手一样，他一只手攥住我的胳膊，直勾勾地盯着我的眼睛：

===Original===

“There were other things that puzzled me. There was, for instance, the chief doctor in our town. He was a large, fat, jolly red-faced man, clean-shaven, with white hair. He was considered the best doctor in the place—all the old maids went to him. He was immensely jolly, you could hear his laugh from one end of the street to the other. He was married, had a delightful little house, where his wife gave charming dinners. He was stupid and self-satisfied. Even at his own work he was stupid, reading nothing, careless and forgetful, thinking about golf and food only all his days. He was a snob too and would give up anyone for the people at the Castle. Even when I was a small boy I somehow knew all this about him. My father thought the world of him and loved to play golf with him. … He was completely happy and successful and popular. Then there was another man, an old canon who taught me Latin before I went to Rugby, an old, untidy, dirty man, whose sermons were dull and his manners bad. He was a failure in life—and he was a failure to himself; dissatisfied with what he used to call his ‘bundle of rotten twigs,’ his life and habits and thoughts. But he thought that somewhere there was something he would find that would save him—somewhere, sometime … not God merely—‘like a key that will open all the doors in the house.’ To me he was fascinating. He knew so much, he was so humble, so kind, so amusing. Nobody liked him, of course. They tried to turn him out of the place, gave him a little living at last, and he married his cook. Was she his key? She may have been … I never saw him again. But I used to wonder. Why was the doctor so happy and the little canon so unhappy, the doctor so successful, the canon so unsuccessful? I decided that the great thing was to be satisfied with oneself. I determined that I would be satisfied with myself. Well, of course I never was—never have been. Something wouldn’t let me alone. The key to the door, perhaps … everything was shut up inside me, and at last I began to wonder whether there was anything there at all. When at nineteen I went to Cambridge I was very unhappy. Whilst I was there my mother died. I came back to the little bow-windowed house and lived with my father. I was quite alone in the world.”

===Chinese===

「还有别的事叫我困惑。比如说，我们城里的那位主任医生。他是个肥肥胖胖、红光满面、嘻嘻哈哈的大个子，脸刮得干干净净，一头白发。人家算他是本地最好的医生——全城的老姑娘都找他看病。他快活极了，他的笑声从街这头一直听到街那头。他成了家，有一所可爱的小宅子，他太太在那儿设宴请客，风雅得很。他又蠢又自满。连自己的本行他也做得蠢，什么都不读，马马虎虎，丢三落四，一辈子光想着高尔夫和吃食。他还是个势利眼，为了巴结城堡里的人家，谁都可以牺牲。我还是个小孩子的时候，就不知怎么把这些全看透了。我父亲把他捧上了天，最爱同他打高尔夫……他完完全全是个幸福、成功、得人心的人。另外还有一个人，一位老教士，我上拉格比之前他教我拉丁文。一个邋里邋遢、脏兮兮的老头儿，讲道沉闷，举止粗鲁。他这辈子一事无成——对他自己也交不了账；他总不满意他自己常说的那『一捆朽枝』——他的生活、习惯和思想。可他相信在什么地方有一样东西，他找着了就能得救——在什么地方，什么时候……又不仅仅是上帝——『像一把钥匙，能打开这宅子里所有的门。』在我眼里他迷人得很。他懂得那么多，那么谦卑，那么和善，那么有趣。当然，谁也不喜欢他。他们千方百计要撵他走，末了给了他一份小小的圣俸，他娶了自己的厨娘。她是他那把钥匙吗？也许是……我再没见过他。可我从前总琢磨。为什么医生那么幸福，小教士那么不幸；医生那么成功，教士那么失败？我断定，顶要紧的是对自己心满意足。我打定主意，要对自己心满意足。唉，当然我从来也没有满意过——至今也没有。有个什么东西总不肯放过我。也许就是那把开门的钥匙……一切都关在我心里，到头来我竟开始怀疑，那里头到底有没有东西。十九岁上剑桥的时候，我非常不快乐。在那儿念书时，我母亲去世了。我回到那幢带弓形凸窗的小房子，同父亲一起生活。我在这个世界上就孑然一身了。」

===Original===

In spite of myself I had a little movement of impatience.

“How self-centred the man is! As though his case were at all peculiar! Wants shaking up and knocking about.”

He seemed to know my thought.

“You must think me self-centred! I was. For thirteen whole years I thought of nothing but myself, my miserable self, all shut up in that little town. I talked to no one. I did not even read—I used to sit in the dark of the cathedral nave and listen to the organ. I’d walk in the orchards and the woods. I would wonder, wonder, wonder about people and I grew more and more frightened of talking, of meeting people, of little local dinner-parties. It was as though I were on one side of the river and they were all on the other. I would think sometimes how splendid it would be if I could cross—but I couldn’t cross. Every year it became more impossible!”

“You wanted someone to take you out of yourself,” I said, and then shuddered at my own banality. But he took me very seriously.

“I did. Of course,” he answered. “But who would bother? They all thought me impossible. The girls all laughed at me—my own cousins. Sometimes people tried to help me. They never went far enough. They gave me up too soon.”

===Chinese===

我不由自主地动了动，有些不耐烦。

「这个人多么以自我为中心！好像他的情形有什么特别似的！真该好好摇一摇、磕打磕打。」

他好像知道我的心事。

「您一定觉得我自我中心！我确实是。整整十三年，我心里想的无非是我自己，我那可怜的自己，整个儿关在那座小城里。我不同任何人说话。我连书也不读——我惯常坐在大教堂中堂的暗处，听管风琴。我在果园和树林里散步。我对人心啊人性啊，猜了又猜，想了又想，越来越怕同人说话，怕见人，怕本地那些小小的晚宴。就好像我站在河的这一边，他们全在河的那一边。我有时想，倘若我能过河去，那该多好——可我过不去。一年比一年过不去！」

「您是想有个人把您从自我里拉出来，」我说，说完又为自己这话的陈腐打了个寒噤。可他却拿它当了正经。

「我是想。当然，」他回答。「可谁肯费这个心呢？他们全都觉得我无可救药。姑娘们个个笑话我——我自己的表姐妹们。有时也有人想帮帮我。可他们从来帮不到底，太早就把我撂开了。」

===Original===

He evidently thinks he was worth a lot of trouble, I thought irritably. But suddenly he laughed.

“That same doctor one day spoke of me, not knowing that I was near him; or perhaps he knew and thought it would be good for me. ‘Oh, Trenchard,’ he said. ‘He ought to be in a nunnery … and he’d be quite safe, too. *He’d* never cause a scandal!’ They thought of me as something not quite human. My father was very old now. Just before he died, he said: ‘I’d like to have had a son!’ He never noticed me at his bedside when he died. I was a great disappointment to him.”

“Well,” I said at last to break a long pause that followed his last words, “what did you think about all that time you were alone?”

===Chinese===

他显然以为自己很值得人家费一番手脚呢，我烦躁地想。可他忽然笑了。

「就是那位医生，有一天说起我来，不知道我就在他近旁；也许他知道，觉得这话对我有好处。『哦，特伦查德呀，』他说，『他该送进女修道院去……在那儿还保险得很。*他*一辈子也闯不出丑闻来！』他们把我看作一样不大算人的东西。我父亲这时已经很老了。临终前不久，他说：『我本想有个儿子！』他咽气的时候，压根儿没注意到守在床边的我。我实在叫他太失望了。」

「那么，」他最后一句话之后是长长的沉默，我终于开口打破它，「您孤身一人的那些年月里，都在想些什么呢？」

===Original===

“I used to think always about two things,” he said very solemnly. “One was love. I used to think how splendid it would be if only there would be someone to whom I could dedicate my devotion. I didn’t care if I got much in return or no, but they must be willing to have it ready for me to devote myself altogether. I used to watch the ladies in our town and select them, one after another. Of course they never knew and they would only have laughed had they known. But I felt quite desperate sometimes. I had so much in me to give to someone and the years were all slipping by and it became, every day, more difficult. There *was* a girl … something seemed to begin between us. She was the daughter of one of the canons, dark-haired, and she used to wear a lilac-coloured dress. She was very kind; once when we were walking through the town I began to talk to her. I believe she understood, because she was very, very young—only about eighteen—and hadn’t begun to laugh at me yet. She had a dimple in one cheek, very charming—but some man from London came to stay at the Castle and she was engaged to him. Then there were Katherine and Millie Trenchard, of whom we were talking. Katherine never laughed at me; she was serious and helped her mother about all the household things and the village where they lived. Afterwards she ran away with a young man and was married in London—very strange because she was so serious. There was a great deal of talk about it at the time. Millie too was charming. She laughed at me, of course, but she laughed at everyone. At any rate she was only cousinly to me; she would not have cared for my devotion.”

As he spoke I had a picture in my mind of poor Trenchard searching the countryside for someone to whom he might be devoted, tongue-tied, clumsy, stumbling and stuttering, a village Don Quixote with a stammer and without a Dulcinea.

“They must have been difficult years,” I said, and again cursed myself for my banality.

“They were,” he answered very gravely. “Very difficult.”

“And your other thoughts?” I asked him.

===Chinese===
