Nikitin simply told me that a furious battle was raging some ten versts on the other side of the river, that we were to go at once to form a temporary hospital behind the lines in the Forest; that the nurses and the rest of the Otriad would remain in Mittövo to wait for the main tide of the wounded, but that we were to go forward to help the army doctors. He spoke very quietly. We said nothing of Marie Ivanovna.

I dressed quickly and on going out found the wagons waiting, some fifteen or twenty sanitars and Trenchard and Andrey Vassilievitch. The four of us climbed into one of the wagons and set off. I did not see Semyonov. Trenchard was pale, there were heavy black lines under his eyes﻿—but he seemed calm, and he stared in front of him as though he were absorbed by some concentrated self-control. For the first time in my experience of him he seemed to me a strong independent character.

===Chinese===

玛丽·伊万诺夫娜（Marie Ivanovna）去世的那天夜里，我睡了一场沉甸甸的无梦的酣睡。第二天早晨六七点钟之间，尼基京（Nikitin）把我唤醒，告诉我，他同特伦查德（Trenchard）、安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇（Andrey Vassilievitch）还有我，必须立刻返回森林。我立刻觉察到大事临头时空气里那种难以形容的震颤。房子里静极了，夏日的清晨非常清新明澈，可空气里压着某种危机。萦绕在我们心头的，不只是玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的死，更是一种告诉我们此刻任何个人的生死都已无足轻重的东西……个人，个性，都被一场大结局席卷一切的急流吞没了。尼基京只简单告诉我，河对岸十俄里开外，一场恶战正打得火热；我们要即刻前往，在森林里战线后方设立一所临时医院；护士们和医疗支队（Otriad）其余的人留在米托沃（Mittövo）等候伤员的大股人流，而我们几个要顶上前去协助军医。他说得非常平静。关于玛丽·伊万诺夫娜，我们一个字也没有提。

我匆匆穿好衣服，出门时只见大车已在等候，有十五到二十名卫生员（sanitar），还有特伦查德和安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇。我们四个爬上其中一辆大车，出发了。我没有见到谢苗诺夫（Semyonov）。特伦查德脸色苍白，眼睛底下压着浓重的黑影——但他似乎很平静，直勾勾地望着前面，仿佛整个人沉浸在某种凝紧的自制里。以我对他的阅历而论，这是头一回，他在我眼里成了一个坚强而独立的人物。

===Original===

We did not speak at all. I could see that Andrey Vassilievitch was nervous: his eyes were anxious and now and then he moistened his lips with his tongue. When we had crossed the river and began to climb the hill I knew that I *hated* the Forest. It was looking beautiful under the early morning sun, its green so delicate and clear, its soft shadows so cool, its birds singing so carelessly, the silver birches, lines of light against the dark spaces; but this was all to me now as though it had been arranged by some ironic hand. It knew well enough who had died there yesterday and it was preparing now, behind its black recesses, a rich harvest for its malicious spirit. We passed through the cholera village and reached the white house of yesterday at about ten o’clock. As we clattered up to the door I for a moment closed my eyes. I felt as though I could not face the horrible place, then summoning my control I boldly challenged it, surveying its long broken windows, its high doorway, its sunny, insulting garden. We were met by the stout doctor, whom I had seen before. As he is of some importance in the events that followed I will mention his name﻿—Konstantine Feodorovitch Kryllow. He was large and stout, a true Russian type, with a merry laughing face. He had the true Russian spirit of unconquerable irrational merriment. He laughed at everything with the gaiety of a man who finds life too preposterous for words. He had all the Russian untidyness, kindness of heart, gay, ironical pessimism. “Tomorrow” was a word unknown to him: nothing was sacred to him, and yet at times it seemed as though life were so holy, so mysterious, that the only way to keep it from careless eyes was by laughing at it. He had no principles, no plans, no prejudices, no reverences. If he wished to sleep for a week he would do so, if he wished to eat for a week he would do so. If he died tomorrow he did not care﻿ ﻿… it was all so absurd that it was not worth while to give it any attention. He would grow very fat, he would die﻿—he would love women, play cards, drink, quarrel, give his life for a sentimental moment, pour every farthing of his possessions into the lap of a friend, incur debts which he would not pay, quarrel wildly with a man about a rouble, remember things that you would expect him to forget, forget everything that he should remember﻿—a pagan, a saint, a blackguard, a hero﻿—anything you please so long as you do not take it seriously.

This morning he was dirty and looked as though he had slept for many nights without taking off his clothes﻿—unshaven, his shirt open showing his hairy chest, his eyes blinking in the light.

===Chinese===

我们一路一言不发。我看出安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇心神不定：他的眼神焦灼，时不时伸舌头舔一舔嘴唇。渡过河、开始爬坡的时候，我知道自己是*恨*这森林的。清晨的太阳底下它显得那样美，绿意那样纤柔明净，柔软的阴影那样清凉，鸟儿唱得那样漫不经心，银白的桦树像一道道光线，衬着幽暗的空间；可这一切如今在我眼里，竟像是出自某只讥诮之手的安排。它清楚地知道昨天是谁死在了那里，此刻正在它黑黢黢的腹地后面，为它那恶毒的性灵备下一场丰盛的收获。我们穿过霍乱村，十点钟左右到了昨天那座白房子跟前。当我们的车子辚辚驶近门口时，我有一瞬间闭上了眼睛。我觉得自己简直无法面对这个可怕的地方，随后鼓起全部自制，昂然向它挑战，打量它那一长排破败的窗子、那高高的门洞、那洒满阳光、咄咄逼人的园子。迎接我们的是那位胖医生，我从前见过他。他在后来的事件里颇有几分要紧，因此我要写下他的名字——康斯坦丁·费奥多罗维奇·克雷洛夫（Konstantine Feodorovitch Kryllow）。他高大肥胖，地道的俄国人类型，一张快活的笑脸。他身上有俄国人那种不可战胜的、无理性的欢乐精神。他样样事情都笑，笑得像一个人觉得生活荒唐得一言难尽。俄国式的不修边幅、心地的善良、快活而讥诮的悲观主义，他样样俱全。「明天」这个词他一无所知：没有任何东西在他眼里是神圣的，然而有时又仿佛，生活是那样神圣、那样神秘，唯一能使它免遭轻慢目光的办法，就是拿它打趣。他没有原则，没有计划，没有偏见，没有敬畏。他想睡上一个星期，他就睡；他想吃上一个星期，他就吃。明天就是死了，他也不在乎……这一切都荒唐透顶，不值得为它操一点心。他会越来越胖，他会死——他会爱女人，打牌，喝酒，吵架，为一时感伤献出性命，把家财的每一个子儿都倒进朋友的腰包，欠下永不偿还的债，为一个卢布同人吵得天翻地覆，记住你料定他会忘掉的事，忘掉他该记住的一切——一个异教徒，一个圣徒，一个无赖，一个英雄——随你怎么说都行，只要你别当真。

这天早晨他一身肮脏，看样子像有许多个夜里和衣而睡——胡子没刮，衬衫敞开着，露出毛茸茸的胸膛，眼睛在光里直眨。

===Original===

“That’s good,” he said, seeing us. “I’ve got to be off, leaving the place to you.﻿ ﻿… Fearful time they’re having over there,” pointing across the garden. “Yes, five versts away. Plenty of work in a minute. Brought food with you? Very little here.” Then I heard him begin, as he walked into the house with Nikitin, “Terrible thing, Doctor, about your Sister yesterday.﻿ ﻿… Terrible.﻿ ﻿… I﻿—”

I remember that my great desire was that I should not be left alone with Trenchard. I clung to Andrey Vassilievitch, and a poor resource he was, watching with nervous eyes the building and the glimmering forest, dusting his clothes and beginning sentences which he did not finish, Trenchard was quite silent. We entered the horrible room of yesterday. The dirty plate and the sardine-tin were still there with the flies about them: the highly coloured German supplement watched us from its rakish position on the wall, the treatise on New Mexico was lying on the table. I picked up the book and it opened naturally at a place where the last reader had turned down the corner of the page. The same page happens to be quoted exactly in Trenchard’s diary on an occasion about which afterwards I shall have to speak. There is an account of the year’s work of some New Mexican school and it runs:

===Chinese===

「这就好了，」他一见我们便说。「我得走了，这地方交给你们。……他们那边正够受的呢，」他朝园子那头一指。「是啊，五俄里外。一忽儿就有的是活儿。带吃食来了吗？这儿少得可怜。」随后我听见他同尼基京往屋里走去时开了口：「你们那位护士昨天的事，大夫，真是太惨了。……太惨了。……我——」

我记得我当时最大的心愿，就是别让我同特伦查德单独待在一处。我缠住安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇不放，而他实在是个不顶事的依靠——只见他神色不安地望着那座房子和微光闪烁的森林，掸着自己的衣裳，一句话开了头又结不了尾；特伦查德则完全沉默。我们走进昨天那间可怕的屋子。脏盘子和沙丁鱼罐头还在原处，周围尽是苍蝇：那张色彩浓艳的德国杂志附页，仍以它那副放肆的角度挂在墙上望着我们，那部新墨西哥报告摊在桌上。我拿起那本书，它自然而然翻开在上一位读者折过角的那一页。恰好就是这一页，后来一字不差地引在特伦查德的日记里，那件事我往后还得谈到。上面记着某所新墨西哥学校一年的工作，全文如下：

===Original===

> “Besides the regular class work there have been other features of special merit, programmes of which we append:

> “Lectures: Rev. H. W. Ruffner, Titles and Degrees; Mr. Fred A. Bush, What the Community owes the Newspaper and what the Newspaper owes the Community; Dr. E. H. Woods, Tuberculosis; Rev. I. R. Glass, Fools; Mr. Eugene Warren, Blood of the Nation; Dr. L. M. Strong, Orthopedics; Hon. S. M. Ashenfelter, Freedom of Effort; Hon. W. T. Cessna, Don’t Pay too dearly for the Whistle; Dr. O. S. Westlake, The Physician and the Laity; Prof. Wellington Putman, Rip Van Winkle; Rev. E. S. Hanshaw, The Mind’s Picture Gallery; Hon. R. M. Turner, Opportunities.

> “*Othello*. For the first time the normal students presented for the class-day exercise a Shakespearian play, *Othello*. Cast of characters: Othello, E. F. Dunlavey; Iago, Douglas Giffard; Duke of Venice, Charles Harper; Brabantio, Eugene Cosgrove; Cassio, Arnold Rosenfeld; Roderigo, Erwin Moore; Montano, Wilson Portherfield; Lodovico, Henry Geitz; Gratiano, William Fleming; Desdemona, Carrie Whitehill; Emilia, Gussie Rodgers; Bianca, Florence Otter; senators, officers, messengers and attendants.

> “*Graduating Programme.* Music: the Anglo-Saxon in History, Douglas Giffard; the Anglo-Saxon in Science, Florence Otter; the Anglo-Saxon in Literature, Gussie Rodgers; Music; annual address, Hon. R. M. Turner; Music; presentation of diplomas.

> “Doubtless among the most interesting and most profitable events of the institution was the annual society contest between the two societies, the Literati and the Lyceum. The Silver City Commercial Club offered a costly cup to the winning society and it was won by the Lyceum. The contest was in oration, elocution, debate, parliamentary usage and athletics.

> “The inside adornment of the hall has not been neglected. A number of portraits and a large number of carbon prints of celebrated paintings have been added, the class picture being the most important and costing in the neighbourhood of $100; this is the hunting scene of Ruysdael. Some of the others are *The Parthenon*, *The Immaculate Conception* by Murillo, and *The Allegorie du Printemps* by Botticelli. Many valuable specimens have been added to the museum: among these are minerals, animals and vegetable products, and manufactured articles from abroad illustrative of the habits and customs of foreigners.”

===Chinese===

> 「除常规课业外，尚有其他颇具特色的项目，其节目单附录如下：

> 「演讲：H·W·拉夫纳牧师，《头衔与学位》；弗雷德·A·布什先生，《社会亏欠报纸什么，报纸又亏欠社会什么》；E·H·伍兹博士，《结核病》；I·R·格拉斯牧师，《痴人》；尤金·沃伦先生，《民族的血脉》；L·M·斯特朗博士，《矫形外科学》；S·M·阿申费尔特议员，《努力的自由》；W·T·塞斯纳议员，《别为哨子付出太高的代价》；O·S·韦斯特莱克博士，《医生与常人》；惠灵顿·普特南教授，《瑞普·凡·温克尔》；E·S·汉肖牧师，《心灵的画廊》；R·M·特纳议员，《机遇》。

> 「《奥赛罗》。师范生首次为班级日演出搬演了一部莎剧，即《奥赛罗》。演员表：奥赛罗，E·F·邓拉维；伊阿古，道格拉斯·吉法德；威尼斯公爵，查尔斯·哈珀；勃拉班修，尤金·科斯格罗夫；凯西奥，阿诺德·罗森菲尔德；罗德利哥，欧文·摩尔；蒙塔诺，威尔逊·波特菲尔德；洛多维科，亨利·盖茨；格拉提亚诺，威廉·弗莱明；苔丝狄蒙娜，卡丽·怀特希尔；爱米利娅，格西·罗杰斯；比恩卡，弗洛伦斯·奥特；另有元老、军官、使者及侍从若干。

> 「《毕业典礼节目单》。音乐；历史中的盎格鲁-撒克逊人，道格拉斯·吉法德；科学中的盎格鲁-撒克逊人，弗洛伦斯·奥特；文学中的盎格鲁-撒克逊人，格西·罗杰斯；音乐；年度演讲，R·M·特纳议员；音乐；颁授文凭。

> 「本校历届最有趣、最有益的活动之中，无疑当数两个社团——文学社（Literati）与学园社（Lyceum）——之间的年度社团竞赛。银城商会（Silver City Commercial Club）为优胜社团提供了一座价值不菲的奖杯，奖杯由学园社夺得。竞赛项目为演说、朗诵、辩论、议事规程及田径。

> 「礼堂内部的装点亦未尝疏忽。新增了若干肖像画和大量名画的碳素印片，其中以班级合影最为重要，造价约 100 美元；此即雷斯达尔的狩猎图。其余诸品中，有《帕特农神庙》、穆里略的《圣母无染原罪》，以及波提切利的《春之寓意》（Allegorie du Printemps）。博物馆亦新增许多珍贵标本：其中有矿物、动物与植物产品，以及来自外国、用以展示外邦人风俗习惯的制成品。」

===Original===

I give this page in full because it was afterwards to have importance, though at the time I glanced at it only carelessly. But I remember that I speculated on the lecture by the Rev. I. R. Glass about “Fools,” that I admired a contest so widely extended as to embrace oration, parliamentary usage and athletics, that I liked very much the “class Ruysdael,” “costing in the neighbourhood of $100,” and the “manufactured articles from abroad, illustrative of the habits and customs of foreigners.”

Nikitin came up to me. “Will you please set off at once with Mr. to Vulatch?” he said. “Find there Colonel Maximoff and get direct orders from him. Return as soon as possible. They say we’re not likely to have wounded until late this afternoon﻿—a good thing as a lot wants doing to this place. Hasten, Ivan Andreievitch. No time to lose.”

===Chinese===

我把这整页照录于此，是因为它后来竟有了干系，尽管当时我只漫不经心地扫过一眼。但我记得，我曾对I·R·格拉斯牧师那篇论「痴人」的演讲悠然神往了一番，曾赞叹一场竞赛竟能扩及演说、议事规程与田径如此之广，还非常喜欢那幅「班级合影的雷斯达尔」、那「造价约 100 美元」，以及那些「来自外国、用以展示外邦人风俗习惯的制成品」。

尼基京走到我跟前。「请您即刻同特伦查德先生动身去武拉奇（Vulatch），好吗？」他说。「到那里找到马克西莫夫上校（Colonel Maximoff），直接向他领受命令。尽快回来。他们说，伤员恐怕要到今天傍晚才来得了——这倒是好事，这地方有好多事等着收拾呢。快去吧，伊万·安德烈耶维奇（Ivan Andreievitch）。一刻也不能耽搁。」

===Original===

Vulatch was a little town situated ten versts to our right in the Forest. I had heard of its strange position before, quite a town and yet lying in the very heart of the Forest, as though it had been the settlement of some early colonists. It had running through it a good high road, but otherwise was far removed from the outer world. It had during the war been twice bombarded and was now, I believed, ruined and deserted. For the moment it was the headquarters of the Sixty-Fifth Staff. I was frankly frightened of going alone with Trenchard﻿—frightened both of myself and of him. I told him and without a word he went with me. When we started off in the wagon I looked at him. He was sitting on the straw, very quietly, his hands folded, looking in front of him. He seemed older: the sentimental naivete that had been always in his face seemed now entirely to have left him. He had always looked before as though he wanted someone to help him out of a position that was too difficult for him; now he was alone in a world where no one could reach him. During the whole drive to Vulatch we exchanged no word. The sound of the cannon was distant but incessant, and strangely, as it seemed to me, we were alone. Once and again soldiers passed us, sometimes wagons with kitchens or provisions met us on the road, sometimes groups of men were waiting by the roadside, once we saw them setting up telegraph wires, once a desolate band of Austrian prisoners crossed our path, twice wagons with wounded rumbled along﻿—but for the most part we were alone. We were out of the main track of the battle. It was as though the Forest had arranged this that it might the more impress us. Our road, although it was the high road, was rough and uneven and we advanced slowly: with every step that the horses took I was the more conscious of a sinister and malign influence. I know how easily one’s nerves can lend atmosphere to something that is in itself innocent and harmless enough, but it must be remembered that (at this time), in spite of what had happened yesterday, neither Trenchard’s nerves nor mine were strained. My sensation must, I think, have closely resembled the feelings of a diver who, for the first time, descends below the water. I had never felt anything like this before and there was quite definitely about my eyes, my nose, my mouth, a feeling of suffocation. I can only say that it was exactly as though I were breathing in an atmosphere that was strange to me. This may have been partly the effect of the sun that was beating down very strongly upon us, but it was also, curiously enough, the result of some dimness that obscured the direct path of one’s vision. On every side of our rough forest road there were black cavernous spaces set here and there like caves between sheets of burning sunlight. Into these caves one’s gaze simply could not penetrate, and the light and darkness shifted about one with exactly the effect of stirring, swaying water. Although the way was quite clear and the road broad I felt as though at any moment our advance would be stopped by an impenetrable barrier, a barrier of bristled thickets, of an iron wall, of a sudden, fathomless precipice. Of course to both Trenchard and myself there were, during this drive, thoughts of his dream. We both recognized, although at this time we did not speak of it, that this was the very place that had now grown so vivid to us. “Ah, this is how it looks in sunlight!” I would think to myself, having seen it always in the early morning and cold. Behind me the long white house, the hunters, the dogs.﻿ ﻿… No, they were not here in the burning suffocating sunlight, but they would come﻿—they would come!

===Chinese===

武拉奇是森林里位于我们右手十俄里处的一个小镇。它那奇特的地理位置我早有耳闻——好端端一座镇子，却端坐在森林的最深处，仿佛是某一拨早期殖民者的聚居地。一条像样的大路穿镇而过，除此之外，它与外面的世界远远隔绝。战争期间它挨过两次炮击，如今，我相信，已经倾颓荒废。眼下它是第六十五师（Sixty-Fifth Staff）师部的驻地。要我同特伦查德单独同行，我说不出的心怯——既怕我自己，也怕他。我对他一说，他一言不发便随我走了。坐上大车出发时，我端详着他。他坐在干草上，安安静静，双手交叠，望着前面。他显得老了些：他脸上一直带着的那股多愁善感的天真，如今似乎荡然无存。从前他总像是在巴望什么人搭救他脱出一个他应付不了的处境；如今他独自待在一个谁也够不着他的世界里。去武拉奇的一路，我们没有交谈一句。炮声遥远，却不曾间断，而说来奇怪，我们是孤独的。间或有士兵打旁边经过，有时载着行军厨房或给养的大车在路上同我们相遇，有时成群的人守在路旁，有一次我们看见他们在架设电线，有一次一小队落落寡欢的奥军战俘横穿过我们的去路，有两次载着伤员的大车辚辚驶过——但大半时候，只有我们两个。我们处在战斗干道的外围。仿佛是森林特意作了这样的安排，好叫它给我们的印象更加深刻。我们走的虽是大路，却坎坷不平，车子走得慢：马每迈出一步，我都更深切地觉出一种阴险而恶毒的影响。我知道，人的神经多么容易给一件本身全然无辜、全然无害的东西敷染上氛围；但必须记住，（在这个时候）尽管昨天出了那样的事，特伦查德的神经和我的神经都还不曾绷到极限。我想，我的感觉必定极像一个头一回潜入水下的潜水人。这样的事我从前从来没有体会过，而且我的眼睛周围、鼻子周围、嘴巴周围，确确实实有一种窒息之感。我只能这样说：那完全像是我正在呼吸一种对我而言全然陌生的空气。这一半或许是烈日当头直晒我们的缘故，但说来奇怪，它也是某种迷蒙的结果——那迷蒙遮蔽了人视线笔直的去路。在我们坎坷的林间道路两侧，到处都是黑洞洞的巨穴，像一幅幅燃烧的阳光之间凿出的洞窟。人的目光根本无法透进这些洞穴，光明与黑暗在人四周移动变幻，那效果恰似搅动着的、摇漾的水。虽然道路完全畅通，路面宽阔，我却觉得我们的前进随时会被一道穿不过去的屏障挡住——一道荆棘丛生的屏障，一道铁墙，一道突兀的无底深渊。当然，在这一路上，特伦查德和我心里都想着他的那个梦。我们俩都认出来了——尽管当时彼此没有说破——这正是那个如今在我们眼里已变得如此鲜明的地方。「啊，原来它在阳光下是这个模样！」我心里这样想，因为从前见到它，总是在清晨，总是天寒地冷。在我背后，那长长的白房子，那些猎人，那些狗。……不，在这灼人的、令人窒息的阳光里，他们不在，可他们会来的——他们会来的！

===Original===

The monotony of the place emphasised its vastness. It was not, I suppose, a great Forest, but today it seemed as though we were winding further and further, through labyrinth after labyrinth of clouding obscurity, winding towards some destination from which we could never again escape. *Pum﻿—pum﻿—pum*, whispered the cannon; *Whirr﻿—whirr﻿—whirr*, the shadowy trembling background echoed. Then with a sudden lifting of the curtain Vulatch was revealed to us. Ruined towns and villages were, by this time, no new sight to me, but this place was different from anything that I had ever seen before. From the bend of the little hill we looked down upon it and the sight of it made me shudder. It was the deadest place, the *deadest* place in the world﻿—all white under the sun it lay there like the bleached bones of some animal picked clean long ago by the birds.

Not a sound came from it, not a movement could be discerned in it. I could see, standing out straight from the heart of it, what must have been once a fine church. It had had four green turrets perched like little green bubbles on white towers; three of these were still there, and between them stood the white husk of the place; from where we watched we could see little fires of blue light sparkling like jewels between the holes. Over it all was a strange metallic glitter as though we were seeing through glass, glass shaded very faintly green. Under this green shadow, which seemed very gently to stain the air, the town was indeed like a lost city beneath the sea. Catching our breaths we plunged down into the fantastic depths.﻿ ﻿…

As we descended the hill we were surprised by the silence﻿—not a soul to be seen. We had expected to find the place filled with the soldiers of the Sixty-Fifth Division. Our driver on this day was the man Nikolai whom I have mentioned before as attaching himself from the very beginning to Trenchard’s service. He had been Trenchard’s unofficial servant now for a long time, saying very little, always succeeding, in some quiet fashion of his own, in accompanying Trenchard on his expeditions. Nikolai was one of the quietest human beings I have ever known. His charming ugly face was in repose a little gloomy, not thoughtful so much as expectant, dreamy perhaps but also very practical and unidealistic. His smile changed all that; in a moment his face was merry, even good-humouredly malicious, suspicious, and a little ironical. He had the thick stolid body of the Russian peasant who is trained to any endurance, any misfortune that God might choose to send it. His attachment to Trenchard had been so unobtrusive that Molozov had officially permitted it without realising that he had permitted anything. It was so unobtrusive that I myself had not, during these last weeks, noticed it. Today I saw Nikolai glance many times at Trenchard. His eyes were anxious and inquiring; he looked at him rather as a dog may look at his master, although there was here no dumb submission, nor any sentimental weakness.﻿ ﻿… I should rather say that Nikolai looked at Trenchard as one free man may look at another. “What is the matter with you?” his eyes seemed to say. “But I know﻿ ﻿… a terrible thing has happened to you. At any rate I am here to be of any use that I can.”

===Chinese===

这地方的单调反倒凸显了它的浩大。我想它算不得一片大森林，可是今天，我们仿佛越绕越远，穿过一层又一层阴翳迷蒙的迷宫，朝着某个一旦到达便再也无法脱身的目的地绕行。*嘣——嘣——嘣*，大炮低语着；*呼——呼——呼*，那影影绰绰、微微颤动的背景应和着。随后，仿佛幕布倏然掀起，武拉奇袒露在我们眼前。倾颓的城镇村庄，此时于我已不是什么新鲜的景致，可这地方不同于我平生见过的任何一处。从小山的拐弯处我们俯瞰着它，那景象叫我不寒而栗。这是全世界最死寂的地方，最*死寂*的地方——它白晃晃地躺在太阳底下，像一头很久以前就被飞鸟剔食净尽、只余白骨的兽。

镇里没有一丝声响，也辨不出一点动静。我看得见从镇子正中笔直挺立出来的，想必从前是一座漂亮的教堂。它原有四座绿色的小尖塔，像白塔上顶着的小小绿泡；其中三座还在，塔与塔之间立着这地方的白色空壳；从我们瞭望的地方，能看见点点蓝色的小火光在窟窿之间宝石似的闪烁。这一切之上罩着一层奇异的金属般的光泽，仿佛我们正隔着一层玻璃在看，一层微微泛绿的玻璃。在这片仿佛把空气轻轻浸染了的绿荫底下，这镇子真像一座沉落海底的迷失的城。我们屏住呼吸，一头扎进这奇幻的深处。……

下坡进镇时，那片寂静叫我们诧异——一个人影也看不见。我们原以为这地方准是挤满了第六十五师的士兵。这天给我们赶车的是尼古拉（Nikolai），就是我先前提过的那个打从一开始便自顾自贴上特伦查德当差的人。他给特伦查德当非正式的仆人已有好一阵子，话极少，却总能用他自己那种悄没声儿的办法，跟着特伦查德一起出门办事。尼古拉是我平生所见最安静的人之一。他那张讨人喜欢的丑脸静下来时带点阴郁，与其说是若有所思，不如说是有所期待，也许近于出神，却又非常实际，绝不多情善感。他一笑，这一切就全变了；一转眼他的脸就快活起来，甚至快活得带点坏意，狐疑，还带几分讥诮。他有一副俄国庄稼汉敦敦实实的身板——那种磨炼得受得住任何忍耐、受得上帝高兴降下的任何灾殃的身板。他对特伦查德的依恋毫不惹眼，以致莫洛佐夫（Molozov）公事公办地准许了他，却根本不曾意识到自己准许了什么。也因为它毫不惹眼，连我自己在这几个星期里也不曾留意。今天我看见尼古拉一次又一次地瞟特伦查德。他的眼神焦灼而探询；他望着他的样子，倒像狗望着主人，虽说这里头没有一点哑忍的顺从，也没有一丝感伤的软弱。……我宁可这样说：尼古拉望特伦查德，像一个自由人望着另一个自由人。「您这是怎么了？」他的眼睛仿佛在说。「可我知道……一件可怕的事落在您头上了。不管怎样，我在这儿，能派什么用场就派什么用场。」

===Original===

“Nikolai,” I said, “why is there no one here?”

“*Ne mogoo znat*, your Honour.”

“Well, the first soldier you see you must ask.”

“*Tak totchno.*”

“Who said you were to drive us?”

“Vladimir Stepanovitch, your Honour.”

“Are you going to remain with us?”

“*Tak totchno.*”

His eyes rested for a moment on Trenchard, then he turned to his horses.

===Chinese===

「尼古拉，」我说，「这儿怎么一个人也没有？」

「*Ne mogoo znat*（俄语：无从知道），长官。」

「那么，见到第一个士兵，你务必问一问。」

「*Tak totchno*（俄语：一点不错）。」

「是谁派你来给我们赶车的？」

「弗拉基米尔·斯捷潘诺维奇（Vladimir Stepanovitch），长官。」

「你要留在我们这儿吗？」

「*Tak totchno*。」

他的目光在特伦查德身上停了片刻，随后便转向他的马。

===Original===

We were entering the town now and it did, indeed, present to us a scene of desperate desolation. The place had been originally built in rising tiers on the side of the valley, and the principal street had leading out of it, up the hill, steps rising to balconied houses that commanded a view of the opposite hill. Almost every house in this street was in ruins; sometimes the ruins were complete﻿—only an isolated chimney of broken stone wall remaining, sometimes the shell was standing, the windows boarded up with wood, sometimes almost the whole building was there, a gaping space in the roof the only sign of desolation. And there remained the ironical signs of its earlier life. Many of the buildings had their titles still upon them. In one place I saw the blackened and almost illegible plate of a lawyer, in another a large still fresh-looking advertisement of a dentist, here there was the large lettering “Tobacconist,” there upon a trembling wall the tattered remains of an announcement of a sale of furniture. Once, most ironical of all, a gaping and smoke-stained building showed the half-torn remnant of a cinematograph picture, a fat gentleman in a bowler hat entering with a lady on either arm a gaily painted restaurant. Over this, in big letters, the word “**farce**.”

Although we saw no soldiers we were not entirely alone. In and out of the sunny caverns, appearing outlined against the darkness, vanishing in a sudden blaze of light, were shadows of the citizens of Vulatch. They seemed to me, without exception, to be Jews. From most of the Galician towns and villages the Jews had been expelled﻿—here they only, apparently, had been left. Of women I saw scarcely any﻿—old men, with long dirty black or grizzled beards, yellow skins, peaked black caps, and filthy black gowns clutched about their thin bodies. They watched us, silently, ominously, maliciously. They crept from door to door, stole up the stone steps and vanished, appeared, as it seemed, right beneath our horses’ feet and disappeared. If we caught them with our eyes they bowed with a loathsome, trembling subservience. There were many little Jewish children, with glittering eyes, naked feet, bare scrubby heads and white faces. Nikolai at length caught an old man and asked him where the soldiers were. The old man replied in very tolerable Russian that all the soldiers had gone last night﻿—not one of them remained﻿—but he believed that some more were shortly to arrive. They were always coming and going, he said.

We stayed where we were, under the blazing sun, and held council. In every doorway, in every shadow, there were eyes watching us. The whole town was overweighted, overwhelmed by the brooding Forest. From where we stood I could see it rising on every side of us like a trembling, threatening green wave; in the furious heat of the sun the white ruins seemed to jump and leap.

===Chinese===

这时我们正在进镇，它呈现在我们面前的，确确实实是一片惨绝的荒凉。这地方原是顺着谷坡一级级往上盖的，主街尽头有台阶引上山去，接到一带带阳台的房子，从那里可以望见对面的山。这条街上几乎家家户户都成了废墟；有的毁得干干净净——只剩一段孤零零的烟囱或半堵破石墙；有的还立着空壳，窗子用木板钉死；也有的几乎整座俱在，屋顶上一个豁开的大洞是唯一的荒凉印记。而它先前生活的种种讥诮的痕迹还在。许多房子上还挂着字号。一处我看到律师的黑牌子，烟熏火燎，字迹几乎漫漶难辨；另一处是一大幅还颇新鲜的牙医广告；这边是大字招牌「烟草店」，那边一面摇摇欲坠的墙上，还贴着家具大拍卖告示的残片。有一处最是讥诮入骨：一栋门窗洞开、烟熏火燎的房子上，露出半幅撕破的电影海报——一位戴圆顶礼帽的胖绅士，左右各挽着一位女士，正走进一家油漆得花红柳绿的餐馆。海报上方，大字写着「**滑稽戏**」。

我们虽然见不到士兵，却并非全然孤独。在那一个个阳光普照的洞穴里进进出出的，是武拉奇市民们的影子——时而衬着黑暗显出轮廓，时而在骤然迸发的亮光里消隐。在我眼里，他们无一例外全是犹太人。加利西亚（Galicia）的城镇村庄，犹太人大都被驱逐了——唯独在这里，他们显然被留了下来。女人我几乎一个也没有见到——尽是老头儿，蓄着长长的、肮脏的黑胡须或花白胡须，蜡黄的皮肤，尖顶黑帽，污黑的长袍紧紧裹住瘦削的身子。他们望着我们，默不作声，不祥而恶毒。他们从一道门蹭到另一道门，蹑手蹑脚登上石阶便不见了，仿佛就出现在我们马蹄底下，一晃又没了。我们的目光若逮住他们，他们便躬身行礼，那份卑躬屈膝叫人厌恶，还哆哆嗦嗦。犹太小孩子很多，眼睛亮晶晶的，光着脚，剃得短短的头发，小脸苍白。尼古拉终于逮住一个老头儿，问他士兵们都上哪儿去了。老头儿用相当过得去的俄语回答，士兵们昨天夜里全走了——一个也不剩——不过他相信，过不多久还会有一批开到。他们总是来了又走，他说。

我们停在烈日底下，就地商议对策。每一道门口，每一片阴影里，都有眼睛在窥伺我们。整座镇子被那阴沉沉郁结酝酿着的森林压得不堪重负、压得垮塌了。从我们站立的地方，我看得见它在我们四面八方涌起，像一道颤动的、威逼而来的绿浪；在烈日狂暴的炙烤下，白色的废墟仿佛在蹦跳。

===Original===

“Well,” I said to Trenchard, “what’s to be done?”

He pulled himself back from his thoughts.

He had been sitting in the cart, quite motionless, his face white and hidden, as though he slept. He raised his tired, heavy eyes to my face.

“Do?” he said.

“Yes,” I answered impatiently. “Didn’t you hear what Nikolai said? There are no soldiers here. We can’t find Maximoff because he isn’t here. We must go back, I suppose.”

“Very well,” he answered indifferently.

“I’m not going back,” I said, “until I’ve had something to drink﻿—tea or coffee. I wonder whether there’s anything here﻿—any place we could go to.”

Nikolai inquired. Old Shylock pointed with his bony finger down the street.

===Chinese===

「喂，」我对特伦查德说，「怎么办？」

他从自己的思绪里挣了回来。

他一直坐在车里，纹丝不动，脸色白白的、遮掩着，仿佛睡着了。此刻他抬起那双疲惫沉重的眼睛，望着我的脸。

「怎么办？」他说。

「是啊，」我不耐烦地答道，「你没听见尼古拉的话吗？这儿没有士兵。我们找不到马克西莫夫，因为他根本不在这儿。我看，我们只好回去。」

「好吧，」他淡漠地答道。

「我可不想这就回去，」我说，「总得先弄点喝的——茶也行，咖啡也行。不知道这儿能不能找到什么——有没有个去得的去处。」

尼古拉去打听。老夏洛克用他皮包骨的手指朝街那头一指。

===Original===

“Very fine restaurant there,” he said.

“Will you come and see?” I asked Trenchard.

“Very well,” said Trenchard.

I told Nikolai to stay there and wait for us. I walked down the street, followed by Trenchard. I found on my left, at the top of a little flight of steps, a house that was for the most part untouched by the general havoc around and about it. The lower windows were cracked and the door open and gaping, but there stood, quite bravely with new paint, the word “Restoration” on the lintel and there were even curtains about the upper windows. Passing through the door we found a room decently clean, and behind the little bar a stout red-faced Galician in white shirt and grey trousers, a citizen of the normal world. We were just then his only customers. We asked him for tea and sat down at a little table in the corner of the room. He did not talk to us but stood in his place humming cheerfully to himself and cleaning glasses. He was a rogue, I thought, looking at his little eyes, but at any rate a merry rogue; he certainly had kept off from him the general death and desolation that had overwhelmed his neighbours. I sat opposite to Trenchard and wondered what to say to him. His expression had never varied. As I looked at him I could not but think of the strength of his eyes, of his mouth, the quiet concentration of his hands﻿ ﻿… a different figure from the smiling uncertain man on the Petrograd station﻿—how many years ago?

===Chinese===

「那边有家上好的馆子。」他说。

「你去看看吗？」我问特伦查德。

「好吧。」特伦查德说。

我吩咐尼古拉留在原地等我们。我沿街走去，特伦查德跟在后面。在左手边，一小段台阶顶上，我发现一座房子，四邻八舍尽遭浩劫，它却大体温完好。楼下的窗玻璃裂了，门敞着大口，可门楣上竟还相当神气地留着新漆的「Restoration」（法语：餐馆）字样，楼上几扇窗子甚至都还挂着窗帘。穿过门口，里面是一间还算干净的屋子，小柜台后头站着一个红脸膛的胖子，加利西亚人，白衬衫，灰裤子，一个正常世界里的公民。那时节我们是他仅有的两位客人。我们向他要了茶，在屋角一张小桌旁坐下。他不跟我们搭话，只站在原处快活地自哼自唱，擦拭着玻璃杯。他是个滑头，我看他那一双小眼睛便知道，但无论如何是个快活的滑头；吞没了左邻右舍的那场普遍的死亡与荒凉，他肯定硬是给挡开了。我坐在特伦查德对面，琢磨着该对他说点什么。他的神情从来没有变过。我望着他，不能不想起他眼睛的力量、他嘴巴的力量、他那双手安静专注的样子……同彼得格勒（Petrograd）车站上那个满面笑容、捉摸不定的人相比，判若两人——那是多少年以前的事了？

===Original===

Our tea was brought to us. Then quite suddenly Trenchard said to me:

“Did she say anything before she died?”

“No,” I answered quietly. “She died instantly, they told me.”

“How exactly was she killed?”

His eyes watched my face without falter, clearly, gravely, steadfastly.

“She was killed by a bullet. Stepped out from behind her shelter and it happened at once. She can have suffered nothing.”

===Chinese===

茶给我们端上来了。这时特伦查德突然对我说：

「她死之前说过什么吗？」

「没有，」我平静地答道。「他们告诉我，她当场就死了。」

「她究竟是怎么死的？」

他的眼睛望着我的脸，不动摇，清亮、庄重而坚定。

「她是中了一颗子弹死的。刚从掩体后面跨出来，事情就发生了。她不可能受什么苦。」

===Original===

“And Semyonov *let* her?”

“He could not have prevented it. It might have happened to anyone.”

“I would have prevented it,” he said, nodding his head gravely.

He was silent for a little; then with a sudden jerk he said:

“Where has she gone?”

===Chinese===

「谢苗诺夫就那么*由着*她？」

「他没法阻止。这种事可能落到任何人头上。」

「换了我就会拦住她，」他说，郑重地点了点头。

他沉默了一小会儿；随后猛地一扬声说：

「她到哪儿去了？」

===Original===

“Gone?” I repeated stupidly after him.

“Yes﻿—that’s not death﻿—to go like that. She must be somewhere still﻿—somewhere in this beastly forest. What﻿—afterwards﻿—when you saw her﻿—what?﻿ ﻿… her face?﻿ ﻿…”

“She looked very peaceful﻿—quite happy.”

“No restlessness in her face? No anxiety?”

“None.”

“But all that life﻿—that energy. It can’t have stopped. Quite suddenly. It *can’t*. She can’t have wanted *not* to know all those things that she was so eager about before.” He was suddenly voluble, excited, leaning forward, staring at me. “You know how she was. You must have seen it numbers of times﻿—how she never looked at any of us really, how we were none of us﻿—no, not even Semyonov﻿—anything to her *really*; always staring past us, wanting to know the answer to questions that *we* couldn’t solve for her. She wouldn’t give it all up simply for nothing, simply for a bullet﻿ ﻿…” he broke off.

===Chinese===

「去了？」我傻乎乎地跟着他重复。

「是啊——那不是死——不能就这样一去了之。她一定还在什么地方——在这片可恶的森林里的什么地方。后来——你见到她的时候——她是什么样子？……她的脸？……」

「她看上去非常安详——很平静，很幸福。」

「脸上没有不安？没有焦虑？」

「没有。」

「可是那整副生命——那股精力。它不可能就那么停了。说停就停。它*不可能*。她不会甘心*不再*知道她从前那样热切想知道的一切。」他忽然滔滔不绝，兴奋起来，身子前倾，直盯着我。「你知道她的为人。你一定见过许多回——她其实从来不正眼看我们中的任何一个；我们谁——不，连谢苗诺夫也在内——对她来说都算不得什么，*真正*算不得什么；她总是望着我们身后的地方，想知道那些*我们*替她解不开的问题的答案。她不会把这一切白白撒手，就为了一颗子弹……」他说不下去了。

===Original===

“Look here, Trenchard,” I said, “try not to think of her just now more than you can help, *just now*. We’re in for a stiff time, I believe. This will be our last easy afternoon, I fancy, and even now we ought to be back helping Nikitin. You’ve got to work all you know. One’s nerves get wrong easily enough in a place like this﻿—and after what has happened I feel this damned Forest already. But we mustn’t *let* our nerves go. We’ve simply got to work and think about nothing at all﻿—*think about nothing at all*.”

I don’t believe that he heard me.

“Semyonov?” he said slowly. “What did he do?”

“He was very quiet,” I answered. “He didn’t say anything. He looked awful.”

“Yes. She snapped her fingers at *him* anyway. *He* couldn’t keep her for all his bullying.”

===Chinese===

「我说，特伦查德，」我说，「眼下能不想她，就尽量别想，*就说眼下*。我看苦日子正等着我们呢。今天这个下午，恐怕是我们最后一个松快的下午了，就是现在，我们也早该回去帮尼基京的忙了。你得把浑身解数都使出来干活。在这种地方，人的神经说坏就坏——出了昨天那样的事，我已经觉出这该死的森林的滋味了。可我们千万不能*由着*神经垮下去。我们只管埋头干活，什么都别想——*什么都别想*。」

我相信他根本没有听进去。

「谢苗诺夫？」他慢慢地说。「他怎么样？」

「他非常安静，」我回答。「什么也没说。他脸色难看极了。」

「是啊。不管怎么说，她对他是不屑一顾的。*他*再怎么霸道，也留不住她。」

===Original===

“It pretty well killed him,” I said rather fiercely. “Look here, Trenchard. Don’t think of yourself﻿—or of her. Everyone’s in it now. There isn’t any personality about it. We’ve simply got to do our best and not think about it. It’s thinking that beats one if one lets it.”

“Semyonov﻿ ﻿… Semyonov,” he repeated to himself, smiling. “No, *he* had not power over her.” Then looking at me very calmly, he remarked: “This Death, you know, Durward.﻿ ﻿… It simply doesn’t exist. It can’t stop *her*. It can’t stop *anyone* if they’re determined. I’ll find her before Semyonov does, too.”

Then, as though he had waked from sleep, he said to me, his voice trembling a little: “Am I talking queerly, Durward? If I am, don’t think anything of it. It’s this heat﻿—and this place. Let’s get back.” He only spoke once more. He said: “Do you remember that first drive﻿—ages ago, when we saw the trenches and heard the frogs and I thought there was someone there?”

“Yes,” I said. “I remember.”

“Well, it’s rather like that now, isn’t it?”

===Chinese===

「这件事差不多把他整个人毁了，」我说得相当狠。「我说，特伦查德。别想你自己的事——也别想她的事。如今人人都卷在里头。这里头再没有什么个人不个人。我们只有尽力干，别去多想。想，才是压垮人的东西，你要是由着它去想。」

「谢苗诺夫……谢苗诺夫，」他自言自语地念着，微笑起来。「不，*他*没有辖制她的力量。」随后他十分平静地望着我，说道：「这死亡啊，德沃德（Durward），您是知道的。……它压根儿不存在。它拦不住*她*。人只要打定了主意，它就拦不住*任何人*。我还会赶在谢苗诺夫前头找到她呢。」

随后，仿佛从酣睡中醒来似的，他对我说，声音微微发颤：「我是不是说话很古怪，德沃德？要是古怪，可别放在心上。是这暑热——还有这地方。我们回去吧。」此后他只又开过一次口。他说：「你还记得头一回坐车出去吗——好久好久以前，我们看见战壕、听见蛙鸣、我觉得那里面有个人的那一次？」

「记得，」我说。「我记得。」

「嗯，眼下这光景，跟那一次颇有点相像，是不是？」

===Original===

A pretty girl, twenty-two or twenty-three years of age, obviously the daughter of the red-faced proprietor, came up to us and asked us if we would like any more tea. She would be stout later on, her red cheeks were plump and her black hair arranged coquettishly in little shining curls. She smiled on us.

“No more tea?” she said.

“No more,” I answered.

“You will not be staying here?”

“Not tonight.”

“We have a nice room here.”

“No, thank you.”

“Perhaps one of you﻿—”

===Chinese===

一个漂亮的姑娘走上前来，问我们还添不添茶。她二十二三岁年纪，一望便知是那位红脸膛店主的女儿。往后她准会发胖，她的红脸颊圆鼓鼓的，黑头发俏皮地梳成一个个亮亮的小卷。她朝我们微笑。

「不再来点茶吗？」她说。

「不要了。」我回答。

「你们不在这儿住下吗？」

「今晚不住。」

「我们这儿有个好房间。」

「不用了，谢谢。」

「也许二位哪位——」

===Original===

“No. We are returning tonight.”

“Perhaps, for an hour or two.” Then smiling at me and laughing a little, “I have known many officers﻿ ﻿… very many.”

“No, thank you,” I said sternly.

“I have a sister,” she said. She turned, crying: “Marie, Marie!”

A little girl, who could not have been more than fourteen years of age, appeared from the background. She also was red-cheeked and plump; her hair also was arranged in black, shining curls. She stood looking at us, half smiling, half defiant, sucking her finger.

“She also has known officers,” said the girl. “She would be very glad, if you cared﻿—”

I heard their father behind the bar humming to himself.

“Come out of this!” I said to Trenchard. “Come away!”

===Chinese===

「不。我们今晚就回去。」

「哪怕待一两个钟头也好呢。」随后她朝我微笑，轻轻笑了两声，「我认识过许多军官……许许多多。」

「不用了，谢谢。」我板着脸说。

「我有个妹妹，」她说。她转过身去喊：「玛丽，玛丽！」

一个小姑娘从后面走了出来，至多不过十四岁。她也生得红脸颊、胖乎乎；头发也同样梳成黑亮的小卷。她站在那儿望着我们，半带笑意，半带挑衅，嘬着手指头。

「她也认识过军官，」姑娘说。「要是二位有意思，她会非常乐意的——」

我听见柜台后面她们的父亲还在自哼自唱。

「快别待在这儿了！」我对特伦查德说。「走吧！」

===Original===

He followed me quietly, bowing very politely to the staring sisters.﻿ ﻿…

“Go on,” I said to Nikolai. “Drive on. No time to waste. We’ve got work to do.”

On our return we found that the press of work was not as yet severe. Half the building belonged to us, the remaining half being used by the officers of the battery. Nikitin had arranged a large room, that must I think have been a dining-room in happier days, with beds; to the right was the operating-room, overhead were our bedrooms and the room where originally I had sat with Marie Ivanovna was a general meeting place. The officers of the battery, two middle-aged and two very young indeed, were extremely courteous and begged us to make use of them in any way possible. They were living in the raggedest fashion, a week’s growth of beard on their chins, their beds unmade, the floor littered with ends of cigarettes, pieces of paper, journals.

“Been here weeks,” they apologetically explained to us. “Come in and have a meal with us whenever you like.” They resembled animals in a cave. When they were not on duty they played chemin-de-fer and slept. Meanwhile for three days and nights our work was slight. The battle drew further away into the Forest. Wagons with wounded came to us only at long intervals.

===Chinese===

他悄没声儿地跟我走了出去，朝瞪着眼望我们的姐妹俩极有礼貌地鞠了一躬。……

「走吧，」我对尼古拉说。「赶车吧。没工夫耽搁了。我们还有活儿要干。」

我们回来后发现，活计的压力还不大。半座房子归我们使用，其余一半住着炮队的军官。尼基京布置好了一个大房间——我想在太平年月里准是间饭厅——摆上了床；右手边是手术室，楼上是我们的卧室，而当初我同玛丽·伊万诺夫娜坐过的那个房间，成了大家聚会的场所。炮队的军官们，两位人到中年，两位着实非常年轻，客气极了，恳请我们无论何事随时使唤他们。他们过得邋遢至极：下巴上一个星期的胡子茬，床铺从不整理，地板上散满烟头、碎纸和报纸。

「在这儿待了几个星期了，」他们带着歉意对我们解释。「随时请过来同我们一起用饭。」他们像穴居的野兽。不值班的时候，他们就打铁路牌（chemin-de-fer），睡觉。与此同时，一连三天三夜，我们的活计都算轻省。战斗朝森林深处退去。载着伤员的大车隔很久才来一辆。

===Original===

The result of these three days was a strange new intimacy between the four of us. I have never in all my life seen anything more charming than the behaviour of Nikitin and Andrey Vassilievitch to Trenchard. There is something about Russian kindness that is both simpler and more tactful than any other kindness in the world. Tact is too often another name for insincerity, but Russian kindheartedness is the most honest impulse in the Russian soul, the quality that comes first, before anger, before injustice, before prejudice, before slander, before disloyalty, and overrides them all. They were, of course, conscious that Trenchard’s case was worse than their own. Marie Ivanovna’s death had shocked them, but she had been outside their lives and already she was fading from them. Trenchard was another matter. Nikitin seemed to me for the first time in my knowledge of him to come down from his idealistic dreaming. He cared for Trenchard like a child, but never obtrusively. Trenchard seemed to appreciate it, but there was something about him that I did not like. His nerves were tensely strained, he did his work with his eyes fixed upon some impossible distance, he often did not hear us when we spoke to him.

And so the three of us formed a kind of hedge about him to protect him, a hedge of which he was perfectly unconscious. He was very silent and I would have given a great deal to hear again one of those Glebeshire stories that I had once found so tiresome. That some plan or purpose was in his head one could not doubt.

===Chinese===

这三天的结果，是我们四人之间生出了一种陌生的新亲密。尼基京和安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇待特伦查德的样子，是我平生所见最动人的。俄国人的好意有一种东西，比世上任何别的好意都更单纯，也更体贴。体贴往往是不诚恳的代名词，可俄国人的好心肠是俄国人灵魂里最诚实的冲动，是排在第一位的品性——排在怒气之前，不义之前，偏见之前，诽谤之前，背信之前——并且压倒这一切。他们当然明白，特伦查德的处境比他们自己的更糟。玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的死震动过他们，但她本在他们生活之外，如今已在他们心头渐渐淡去。特伦查德却是另一回事。以我对尼基京的了解，他这是头一回从他理想主义的迷梦里走下来。他照料特伦查德像个孩子，却从不显山露水。特伦查德似乎领受这份情，可他身上有一样东西我不喜欢。他的神经绷得紧紧的，干活时眼睛总定定地望着某种遥不可及的远处，我们同他说话，他常常听不见。

于是我们三个围着他筑起一道篱笆保护他，一道他全然不觉的篱笆。他话极少，而我情愿付出很大的代价，再听一回那些我曾觉得那么腻烦的格利比郡（Glebeshire）故事。他脑袋里揣着什么计划或目的，这是谁也不能怀疑的。

===Original===

We had, all of us, much in common in our characters. We liked the sentimental easy coloured view of life. We suddenly felt a strange freedom here in this place. For myself, on the third day, I found that Marie Ivanovna was most strangely present with me, and on the afternoon of that day, our wounded quiet on their beds, our wagons sent into the tent with no prospect of their return for several hours, we sat together, Nikitin, Andrey Vassilievitch and I, looking out through a break in the garden towards the Forest, and talked about her. The weather was now very heavy﻿—certainly a thunderstorm was coming. I was also weighted down by an intense desire for sleep, at the same time knowing that if I were to fling myself on my bed sleep would not come to me. This is an experience that is not unusual at the Front, and officers have told me that in the middle of a battle when there comes a sudden lull, their longing for sleep has been so overpowering that no imminent danger could lift it from their eyes.

We sat there then and talked in low voices of Marie Ivanovna. I was aware of the buzzing of the flies, of the dull yellow light beyond the windows, of the Forest crouching a little as it seemed to me like a creature who expects a blow. We were all half asleep perhaps, the room dark behind us, and we talked of her as we might talk of a picture, a book, an experience ended and dismissed﻿—something outside our present affairs. And yet I knew that for me at any rate she was not outside them. I felt as though at any moment she might enter the room. We discussed her aloofness, her sudden happiness and her sudden distress, her intimacies and withdrawals, Nikitin and Andrey Vassilievitch slowly elaborating her into a high romantic figure. Behind her, behind all our thoughts of her, there was the presence of Semyonov. Nothing was stranger during our time here than the way that Semyonov had always kept us company.

Our consciousness of relief from him had begun it. We had been more under his influence than any of us had cared to confess and, in his presence, had checked our natural impulses. I also was strongly aware of him through Trenchard. Trenchard seemed now to have a horror of him that could be explained only by the fact that he held him responsible for Marie Ivanovna’s death. It’s a good thing, I thought to myself, that Semyonov’s not here.

===Chinese===

我们几个的性子彼此都有许多相通处。我们都偏爱那种多情的、随和的、着了彩色的生活观。在这个地方，我们忽然感到一种陌生的自由。就我自己而言，到了第三天，我发现玛丽·伊万诺夫娜以一种最奇异的方式伴随在我身边；那天下午，我们的伤员在床上安安静静，我们的大车开进帐篷里去，几个钟头内没有回来的指望，我们便坐在一起——我、尼基京和安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇——透过园子里的一处缺口望着森林，谈起她来。天气这时非常沉闷——肯定有一场雷雨要来。我还被一股强烈的睡意压得发沉，同时又知道，倘若扑到床上去，睡意偏又不会来。这种经历在前线并不罕见，有军官告诉我，战斗正酣时忽然出现一个间歇，他们对睡的渴求会凶猛到任何迫在眉睫的危险都无法把它从眼皮上掀开。

我们就这样坐着，低声谈论玛丽·伊万诺夫娜。我意识到苍蝇的嗡嗡声，意识到窗外那昏黄的天光，意识到森林微微蹲伏着——在我看，它像一头等着挨一记的畜生。我们也许都半睡着了，身后的房间黑黢黢的，我们谈着她，就像谈一幅画、一本书、一段完结了又搁下了的经历——一件置身于我们眼前事务之外的东西。然而我知道，至少对我而言，她并不在外面。我觉得仿佛她随时都会走进屋来。我们议论她的孤冷，她突如其来的欢喜和突如其来的悲苦，她的亲昵与疏远，尼基京和安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇慢慢地把她敷演成一位高贵的传奇人物。在她身后，在我们关于她的一切念头身后，是谢苗诺夫的影子。我们待在此地的日子里，再没有比谢苗诺夫始终陪伴着我们这件事更奇怪的了。

这一切，是从我们意识到摆脱了他之后的那份轻松开始的。我们受他的影响，其实比我们哪一个肯承认的都深，当着他面的时候，我们都敛着自己的天性。我也通过特伦查德强烈地意识到他。特伦查德如今对他怀有一种恐怖，那只能用一件事解释：他把玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的死记在了他账上。真是好事，我心里想，幸亏谢苗诺夫不在这儿。

===Original===

These hours of waiting, when there was nothing to do, was bad for all our nerves. Upon this afternoon I remember that after a time silence fell between us. We were all staring in front of us, seeing pictures of other places and other people. I was aware, as I always was, of the Forest, seeing it shine with its sinister green haze, seeing the white bleached town, the huddled villagers waiting for their food, but seeing yet more vividly the deep silences, the dark hollows, the silent avenues of silver birch. Against this were the figures of the people who were dear to me. It is strange how war selects and brings forward as one’s eternal company the one or two souls who have been of importance in one’s life. One knows then, in those long, long threatening pauses, when the battle seems to gather itself together before it thunders its next smashing blow, those who are one’s true companions. Certain English figures were now with me outlined against the Forest﻿—and joined together with them Marie Ivanovna as I had last seen her, turning round to me by the door and smiling upon me. I did truthfully feel, as Trenchard had said to me, that she was not dead; I sat, staring before me, conjuring her to appear. The others also sat there, staring in front of them. Were they also summoning some figure? I knew, as though Andrey Vassilievitch had told me, that he was thinking of his wife. And Nikitin?﻿ ﻿…

He sat there, lying back on the old sofa that Marie had used, his black beard, his long limbs, his dark eyes giving him the colour of some Eastern magician. He did indeed, with his intense, absorbed gaze, seem to be casting a spell. As I looked Andrey Vassilievitch caught his glance﻿—they exchanged the strangest flash﻿—something that was intimate and yet foreign, something appealing and yet hostile. It was as though Andrey Vassilievitch had said: “I know you are thinking of her. Leave her to me,” and Nikitin had replied: “My poor friend. What can you do?﻿ ﻿… I do as I please.”

I know at least that I saw Andrey Vassilievitch frown, make as though he would get up and leave the room, then think better of it, and sink back into his chair.

===Chinese===

这些无所事事的等待时辰，对我们大家的神经都没有好处。我记得那天下午，过了一阵，我们之间沉寂下来。我们都直望前方，看见别处旁人的种种图景。我像往常一样意识到森林，看见它闪着不祥的绿霭，看见那白得褪了色的镇子，看见蜷缩着等候吃食的村民，可我看见得更鲜明的，是那深深的静谧、幽暗的洼地、银白桦树下幽寂无声的甬道。衬着这一切的，是那些我所亲爱的人们的身影。战争竟会拣选出、推到人跟前，作为人永恒的伴侣的，恰恰是一个人一生中最要紧的那一两个灵魂，这真是奇怪。于是，在那漫长的、漫长的、步步威逼的间歇里——当战斗仿佛攒足了劲、就要轰出下一记粉碎性打击的时候——人就认清了谁是自己真正的同伴。几幅英国人的身影此刻正衬着森林陪着我——同他们连在一起的，是玛丽·伊万诺夫娜，我最后见到她的样子：在门边回过头来，朝我微笑。我确实真心感到——正如特伦查德对我说过的——她没有死；我坐着，凝望前方，念咒似的唤她显形。其余的人也都坐在那里，凝望前方。他们是不是也在召唤什么身影？我知道——仿佛安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇亲口告诉过我似的——他想的是他的妻子。那么尼基京呢？……

他坐在那里，仰靠在玛丽用过的那张旧沙发上，黑胡须，长手足，一双黑眼睛，给他添上几分东方魔术师的颜色。他那种热烈入迷的凝视，确实像在施行法术。我望着的时候，安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇恰巧接住了他的目光——两人交换了一个最古怪的眼神——一种又亲昵又隔膜的东西，一种又恳求又敌对的东西。仿佛安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇在说：「我知道你在想她。把她留给我。」而尼基京回答：「我可怜的朋友。你能做什么呢？……我行我素。」

我至少看见安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇皱起了眉头，做出一副要起身离房的样子，随即又打消了主意，重新跌坐回他的椅子里。

===Original===

I remember that just at that moment Trenchard entered. He joined us and sat on the sofa near Nikitin without speaking, staring in front of him like the rest of us. His face was tired and old, his cheeks hollow.

I waited and the silence began to get on my nerves. Then there came an interruption. The door opened quite silently: we all turned our eyes towards it without moving our heads. In the doorway stood Semyonov.

We were startled as though by a ghost. I remember that Andrey Vassilievitch jumped to his feet, crying. Trenchard never moved. Semyonov with his usual stolid self-possession came towards us, greeted us, then turning to me said:

===Chinese===

我记得恰在那时特伦查德走了进来。他加入我们，挨着尼基京在沙发上坐下，一言不发，像我们大家一样望着前面。他的脸疲惫而苍老，两颊凹陷。

我等着等着，那沉默渐渐磨起我的神经来。这时来了一个岔子。门悄没声儿地开了：我们都把眼睛转向门口，头却没有动。门口立着谢苗诺夫。

我们吓了一跳，仿佛见了鬼。我记得安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇跳起身来，叫出了声。特伦查德一动不动。谢苗诺夫带着他惯常那种木然的自若朝我们走来，向我们道了好，随后转过身来对我说：

===Original===

“I’ve come to take your place, Ivan Andreievitch.”

“My place?” I stammered.

“Yes. You’re wanted there. You’re to return at once in the *britchka*.﻿ ﻿… In half an hour, if you don’t mind.”

“And you’ll stay?”

“And I’ll stay.”

===Chinese===

「我是来接您的班的，伊万·安德烈耶维奇。」

「我的班？」我结结巴巴地说。

「是的。那边要您回去。您要立刻乘*britchka*（俄语：轻便四轮马车）回去。……要是您不介意，半个钟头之内就走。」

「那您留下？」

「我留下。」

===Original===

No one else said anything. I remember that I had some half-intention of protesting, of begging to be allowed to remain. But I was no match for Semyonov. I could fancy the futility of my saying: “But really, Alexei Petrovitch, we don’t want you here. It’s much better to leave me. You’ll upset them all. It’s a nervous place, this.” I said nothing, except: “All right. I’ll go.” He watched me. He watched us all. I fancy that he smiled.

Outside I had a desperate absurd thought that I would return and ask him to be kind to Trenchard. As I turned away someone seemed to whisper in my ear:

“He’s come, you know, to find Marie Ivanovna.”

===Chinese===

别的人谁也没有说什么。我记得我当时多少有点想抗议，想求他准许我留下。可我斗不过谢苗诺夫。我设想着自己这么说会有多么徒劳：「可是说真的，阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇（Alexei Petrovitch），我们这儿不需要您。还是留我下来的好。您会把他们全都搅乱的。这地方闹神经，这地方。」我什么也没说，只说了一句：「好吧。我去。」他望着我。他望着我们大家。我猜想他笑了。

走到外面，我起了一个绝望而荒唐的念头：我要回去，求他待特伦查德客气些。我转身走开时，仿佛有谁在我耳边低语：

「您可知道，他来，是为了找玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的。」

## III The Invisible Battle / 三 看不见的战斗

===Original===

On the evening of the following day Trenchard, Andrey Vassilievitch and I were sent with sanitars and wagons to the little hamlet of M﻿—﻿—, five versts only from the Position. It was night when we arrived there; no sound of cannon, only on the high hills (the first lines of the Carpathians) that faced us the scattered watchfires of our own Sixty-Fifth Division, and in the little village street a line of cavalry moving silently, without a spoken word, on to the high road beyond. After much difficulty (the village was filled with the officers of the Sixty-Fifth) we found a kitchen in which we might sleep. Upon the rough earth floor our mattresses were spread, my feet under the huge black oven, my head beneath a gilt picture of the Virgin and Child that in the candlelight bowed and smiled, in company with eight other pictures of Virgins and Children, to give us confidence and encouragement.

It was a terrible night. On a high pillared bed set into the farther wall, an old Galician woman, her head bound up in a red handkerchief, knelt all night and prayed aloud. Her daughter crouched against the wall, sleeping, perhaps, but nevertheless rocking ceaselessly a wooden cradle that hung from a black bar in the ceiling. In this cradle lay her son, aged one or two, and once and again he cried for half an hour or so, protesting, I suppose, against our invasion. There was a smell in the kitchen of sour bread, mice, and bad water. The heat was terrible but the old lady told us that the grandchild was ill and would certainly die were the window opened. The candle we blew out but there remained a little burning lamp under the picture of the Virgin immediately over the old lady’s bed. I slept, but for how long I do not know. I was only aware that suddenly I was awake, staring through the tiny diamond-paned window, at the faint white light now breaking in the sky. I could see from my mattress only a thin strip of this light above the heavy mass of dark forest on the mountainside.

===Chinese===

第二天傍晚，特伦查德、安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇和我带着卫生员和大车，被派往小村 M——，离阵地只有五俄里。我们到那儿时已是夜里；没有炮声，只有面对我们的高冈上（喀尔巴阡山脉的头几重山峦）我们自己的第六十五师（Sixty-Fifth Division）散落的一堆堆篝火，还有小村街上，一队骑兵默默无言、一声不响地朝前头的大路开去。费了好大劲（村里住满了第六十五师的军官），我们才找到一间可以过夜的厨房。粗糙的泥地上铺开我们的褥垫，我的脚伸在大黑炉子底下，头枕在一幅镀金的圣母子画像下面；烛光里，那画像同另外八幅圣母子画像一起朝我们颔首微笑，给我们壮胆打气。

那是一个可怕的夜晚。靠里墙嵌着一张高柱床，一个上了年纪的加利西亚老太婆，头上包着红头巾，整夜跪在床上大声祷告。她女儿蜷在墙边，也许睡着了，却仍一刻不停地摇着一只吊在天花板黑木梁上的木摇篮。摇篮里躺着她的儿子，一两岁模样，时不时哭上半个钟头，我想，是在抗议我们这些入侵者。厨房里有一股酸面包、老鼠和坏水的气味。热得要命，可老太婆告诉我们，孙子病着，一开窗准死。蜡烛我们吹灭了，但老太婆床头正上方圣母像底下，还亮着一盏小小的长明灯。我睡着了，睡了多久不知道。只觉得忽然就醒了，透过小小的菱形格窗户，望着天边正在破晓的一线白光。从褥垫上望出去，我只看得见山坡上黑压压的森林之上露出的一窄条光。

===Original===

I must have been still only half-awake because I could not clearly divide, before my eyes, the true from the false. I could see quite plainly in the dim white shadow the face of Trenchard; he was not asleep, but was leaning on his elbow staring in front of him. I could see the old woman with her red handkerchief kneeling in front of her lamp and her prayer came like the turning of a wheel, harsh and incessant. The cradle creaked, in the air was the heavy smell, and suddenly, beyond the window, a cock crowed. These things were real. But also I seemed to be in some place much vaster than the stuffy kitchen of the night before. Under the light that was with every minute growing stronger, I could fancy that many figures were moving in the shadows; it seemed to me as though I were in some place where great preparations were being made. I fancied then that I could discern Marie Ivanovna’s figure, then Nikitin, then Semyonov, then Molozov.﻿ ﻿… There was a great silence but I felt that everyone was busily occupied in making ready for some affair. This was with half my consciousness﻿—with the other half I was perfectly aware of the actual room, of Trenchard, the creaking cradle and the rest.

Then the forest that had been on the hills seemed to draw closer to the house. I felt that it had invaded the garden and that its very branches were rubbing against the windows. With all of this I was aware that I was imagining some occurrence that I had already seen, that was not, in any way, new to me, I was assured of the next event. When we, all of us, Marie Ivanovna, Semyonov, Nikitin and the rest, were ready we should move out into the forest, would stand, a vast company, with our dogs and horses.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

想必我当时还没全醒，因为眼前的一切，我分不清哪是真、哪是幻。朦胧的白影里，我能清清楚楚看见特伦查德的脸；他没有睡，支着胳膊肘，怔怔地望着前面。我看见包红头巾的老太婆跪在她的灯前，祷告声像转轮子似的，又刺耳又不停。摇篮吱呀作响，空气里是那股浓浊的气味，忽然，窗外有公鸡打鸣。这些都是真的。可同时，我仿佛又置身在一个远比昨夜那间憋闷的厨房广阔得多的地方。在每一分钟都在增强的光线下，我恍惚觉得许多人影在暗处走动；仿佛这里正在进行什么盛大的准备。我恍惚认出玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的身影，接着是尼基京，接着是谢苗诺夫，接着是莫洛佐夫。……四下里一片大静默，可我觉出人人都忙着张罗一件事。这是我半边意识的所见——另半边，我对这间实实在在的屋子、对特伦查德、对吱呀作响的摇篮和其余一切，都清清楚楚。

随后，原在山坡上的森林仿佛朝房子逼近过来。我觉得它已侵入花园，枝条正擦着窗户。而在这一切之中，我意识到自己想象的是一桩我早已见过的事，对我毫不新鲜，下一步是什么我有十足的把握。等我们大家——玛丽·伊万诺夫娜、谢苗诺夫、尼基京和其余的人——都齐备了，我们就该开进森林，好大一群人，带着我们的狗和马，站在那儿。……

===Original===

Why, it was Trenchard’s dream that I was seeing! I was merely repeating to myself his own imaginations﻿—and with that I had suddenly, as though someone had hypnotised me, fallen back into a heavy dreamless sleep. It was already midday when I was wakened by little Andrey Vassilievitch, who, sitting on my bed and evidently in a state of the very greatest excitement, informed me that Dr. Semyonov and the Sisters Marie Ivanovna and Anna Petrovna had arrived from —﻿—﻿—, and that we might be off at any moment. I was aware, as he spoke, of a great stir beyond the window and saw, passing up through the valley, a flood of soldiers, infantry, cavalry, kitchens with clumsy black funnels bobbing on their unsteady wheels, cannon, hundreds of carts; the soldiers came up through our own garden treading down the cabbages, stopping at the well near our door and filling their tin kettles, tramping up the road, spreading, like smoke, in the far distance, up the high road that led into the furthest forest.

===Chinese===

哎呀，我看见的正是特伦查德的梦！我不过是在心里复演他自己的想象罢了——这么一想，我忽然像被人催了眠似的，又沉沉睡去，无梦无痕。再醒来已是晌午，小个子安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇坐在我床上叫醒了我，他显然兴奋到了极点，告诉我谢苗诺夫医生和玛丽·伊万诺夫娜、安娜·彼得罗夫娜两位护士已经从————到了，我们随时可能出发。他说话时，我听见窗外一片大骚动，看见谷地里正涌过一股军队的洪流：步兵、骑兵、驮着大锅的炊事车——笨重的黑烟囱在晃晃悠悠的车轮上颠簸——还有大炮、成百上千的大车；士兵们打我们自家的菜园里穿过来，踩塌了卷心菜，在我们门口的水井边停下，灌满他们的白铁水壶，又顺着大路嘈嘈往上走，像烟一样在远处散开，漫上通往森林最深处的大道。

===Original===

“They say﻿—tonight﻿—for certain,” said Andrey Vassilievitch, his fat hand trembling on my bed. He began to talk, his voice shaking with excitement. “Do you know, Ivan Andreievitch, I am continually surprised at myself: ‘Here you are, Andrey Vassilievitch, here, at the war. What do you make of it? I say to myself. Just consider.﻿ ﻿… No, but seriously, Ivan Andreievitch, of course I must seem to all of you something of a comic figure. When my wife was alive﻿—how I wish that you could have known her! Such a remarkable woman; everyone who met her was struck by her fine character﻿—when my wife was alive I had my position to support. That I should have been a comic figure would have distressed her. But now, who cares? Nobody, you may very truly say.﻿ ﻿… Well, well. But the point is that this evening we shall really be in the thick of it. And﻿—may I tell you something, Ivan Andreievitch? Only for yourself, because you are an Englishman and can be trusted: to speak quite truthfully I’m frightened. I say to myself that one is at the war and that one must be frightened at nothing, and still I remain frightened.﻿ ﻿… Frightened of what?﻿ ﻿… I really cannot tell you. Death, perhaps? But no, I should not be sorry to die﻿—there are reasons.﻿ ﻿…

“And yet although I should not be sorry to die, I remain frightened﻿—all night I was awake﻿—I do my utmost to control it, but there is something stronger than I﻿—something. I feel as though if I once discovered what that something was I should not be frightened any longer. Something definite that you could meet and say to yourself: ‘There, Andrey Vassilievitch, you’re not frightened of *that*, are you? What is there to be frightened of?﻿ ﻿… Why then, you know, I don’t believe I should be frightened any more!’ ”

===Chinese===

「他们说——今夜——准没错，」安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇说，他的胖手在我床上直哆嗦。他打开了话匣子，声音兴奋得发颤。「您可知道，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，我老是对自己感到奇怪：『你居然到了这儿，安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇，到了这儿，在战场上。你对此作何感想？』我对自己说。您想想。……不，说正经的，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，在你们大家眼里，我当然少不了是个滑稽角色。我妻子在世时——我多希望您认识过她！那样一位不同寻常的女人；凡是见过她的人，无不为她的人品折服——她还在世时，我是有身份地位要撑着的。我竟成了个滑稽角色，这会让她难过的。可如今呢，谁在乎？您尽可以说：没一个人在乎。……好吧，好吧。可要紧的是，今天晚上我们可就真刀真枪上场了。还有——我能告诉您一件事吗，伊万·安德烈耶维奇？只给您一个人，因为您是英国人，信得过：说老实话，我害怕。我对自己说，人到了战场上，就什么也不许怕，可我还是怕。……怕什么呢？……我实在说不上来。也许是死？不，倒不是，我死而无憾——这里头自有缘故。……

「可话虽这么说，我虽不怕死，却还是害怕——我一夜没合眼——我拼命想管住它，可有一样东西比我强——一样什么东西。我总觉得，只要让我弄明白那东西究竟是什么，我就再也不怕了。一样确确实实的东西，你能跟它照面，对自己说：『喏，安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇，*这*你总不怕了吧？这有什么可怕的呢？……那样一来，您知道，我看我就再也不会害怕了！』」

===Original===

I remember that he then explained to me that he wished Nikitin had been sent instead of Semyonov. Nikitin was much more sympathetic.

“You seem very fond of Nikitin,” I said.

“We are friends﻿ ﻿… we have been friends for many years. My wife was very fond of him. I am a lonely man, Ivan Andreievitch, since the death of my wife, and to be with anyone who knew her is a great happiness﻿ ﻿… yes, a great happiness.”

“And Semyonov?” I asked.

“I have nothing to say against Alexei Petrovitch,” he answered stiffly.

===Chinese===

我记得他随后向我解释，他宁愿派来的是尼基京，而不是谢苗诺夫。尼基京这人热络得多。

「您好像很喜欢尼基京，」我说。

「我们是朋友……多年的朋友了。我妻子生前很喜欢他。我是个孤单的人啊，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，自打我妻子去世以后；能同认识她的人待在一起，就是天大的幸福……是的，天大的幸福。」

「那谢苗诺夫呢？」我问。

「对阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇，我无可指摘，」他生硬地回答。

===Original===

When later I joined the others at the cottage higher up the road taken by the doctors of the Division, I discovered Trenchard in an ecstasy of happiness. He did not speak to me but his shining eyes, the eagerness with which standing back from the group he watched us all, told me everything. Marie Ivanovna had been kind to him, and when I found her in the centre of them, her whole body alert with excitement, I forgot my anger at her earlier unkindness or, if I remembered it, laid it to the charge of my own imagination or Trenchard’s sensitiveness.

Indeed we were all excited. How could we fail to be! There was some big business toward, and in it we were to have our share. We were, perhaps this very day, to penetrate into the reality of the thing that for nine months now we had been watching. All of us, with our little private histories like bundles on our backs, are venturing out to try our fortune.﻿ ﻿… What are we going to find?

===Chinese===

随后我到路上头一处农舍去和大家会合——师里的医生们占了那地方——发现特伦查德喜不自胜。他没有跟我说话，可他那双发亮的眼睛，他站在人群外注视我们大家的那股急切劲儿，把什么都告诉我了。玛丽·伊万诺夫娜对他和气了；我在人群当中找到她时，她浑身上下都绷着兴奋，便把她先前那番冷淡惹起的恼怒忘掉了——或者即便记得，也归咎于我自己的瞎想，或是特伦查德的过敏。

的确，我们人人都兴奋。怎能不兴奋呢！一场大事情正在酝酿，而我们在里头有我们的一份。也许就在今天，我们就要钻进那桩我们旁观了九个月的事情的内核里去。我们这些人，个个背着自家那点小小的私史，像背着包袱，冒险出来碰运气。……我们会撞见什么呢？

===Original===

I remember indeed that early on that afternoon I felt the drama of the whole affair so heavily that I saw in every soldier who passed me a messenger of fate. They called me to a meal. Eat! Now! How absurd it seemed! Semyonov watched me cynically:

“Eat and then sleep,” he said, “or you’ll be no use to anyone.”

Afterwards I went back to the kitchen and slept. That sleep was the end of my melodrama. I was awakened by a rough hand on my shoulder to find it dark beyond the windows and Semyonov watching me impatiently:

===Chinese===

我确实记得，那天下午刚开头，整桩事情的戏剧性压得我那么沉重，以致从身边走过的每一个士兵，在我眼里都是命运的信使。他们唤我吃饭。吃！现在就吃！这显得多么荒唐！谢苗诺夫冷眼瞧着我：

「吃了睡，」他说，「不然你对谁都没用处。」

后来我回到厨房睡了。这一觉，给我的情节剧画上了句号。一只粗糙的手摇醒我，窗外已经黑了，谢苗诺夫不耐烦地看着我：

===Original===

“Come, get up! It’s time for us to start,” and then moved out. I was conscious that I was cold and irritable. I looked back with surprised contempt to my earlier dramatic emotions. I was hungry; I put on my overcoat, shivered, came out into the evening, saw the line of wagons silhouetted against the sky, listened to the perfect quiet on every side of me, yawned and was vexed to find Trenchard at my side.

Why this is actually dull! I thought to myself. It is as though I were going to some dinner that I know beforehand will be exceedingly tiresome﻿—only then I should get some food.

I was disappointed at the lack of drama in the affair. I looked at my watch﻿—it was ten o’clock. Semyonov was arranging everything with a masterly disregard of personal feelings. He swore fine Russian oaths, abused the sanitars, always in his cold rather satirical voice, his heavy figure moving up and down the road with a practical vivid alertness that stirred my envy and also my annoyance. I felt utterly useless. He ordered me on to my wagon in a manner that, in my present half-sleepy, half-surly mood seemed to me abominably abrupt. Trenchard climbed up, very clumsily, after me.

I leaned back on the straw, let my arms fall and lay there, flat on my back, staring straight into the sky.﻿ ﻿… With that my mood suddenly changed. I was at peace with the whole world. Tonight was again thick with a heavy burden of stars that seemed to weigh like the silver lid of some mighty box heavily down, down upon us, until trees and hills and the dim Carpathians were bent flat beneath the pressure. I lying upon my back, seeing only that sheet of stars, in my nostrils the smell of the straw, rocked by the slow dreamy motion of the wagon, was filled with an exquisite ease and lethargy. I was going into battle, was I? I was to have tonight the supreme experience of my life? It might be that tonight I should die﻿—only last week two members of the Red Cross﻿—a nurse and a doctor﻿—had been killed. It might be that these stars, this straw, this quiet night were round me for the last time. It did not matter to me﻿—nothing could touch me. My soul was somewhere far away, upon some business of its own, and how happy was my body without the soul, how contented, how undisturbed! I could fancy that I should go, thus rocking, into battle and there die before my soul had time to return to me. What would my soul do then? Find some other body, or go wandering, searching for me? A star, a flash of light like a cry of happiness or of glad surprise, fell through heaven and the other stars trembled at the sight.

===Chinese===

「来，起来！该出发了，」说完便走了出去。我自觉又冷又烦躁。我回想先前那些戏剧性的激动，只觉得惊讶又不屑。我饿了；穿上大衣，打了个寒噤，走进暮色里，看见一列大车的剪影衬着天空，听着四下里一片死寂，打了个哈欠，发现特伦查德就在身边，心里直冒火。

怎么，这居然是乏味的！我暗自想。这就像去赴一个我预先就知道必定腻味透顶的宴会——只不过赴宴总还能落着点吃的。

这桩事情毫无戏剧性，我很失望。我看看表——十点钟了。谢苗诺夫安排着一切，全然不顾个人情绪，手段高明。他骂出漂亮的俄国脏话，训斥卫生员，声音照例是那种冷冷的、带几分讥诮的调子；他沉重的身影在大路上来来回回，那股务实、麻利、鲜活的劲头，既惹我眼红，又惹我气恼。我自觉完全没用。他吩咐我上我那辆车，那副腔调，在我此刻半睡半醒、半愠半怒的心境里，粗暴得可恶。特伦查德跟着我爬上来，笨手笨脚。

我仰面躺在干草上，胳膊摊开，直直地望着天空。……这么一躺，我的心境忽然变了。我同全世界讲和了。今夜的星空又密密地压着沉沉的星，像一只巨箱的银盖，沉甸甸地压下来，压下来，压在我们身上，直压得树木、山冈和朦朦胧胧的喀尔巴阡山都给压伏在地。我仰面躺着，眼里只有那一整幅星斗，鼻孔里是干草的气息，身子随大车缓慢的、催眠般的晃动摇荡着，心里充满一种精微的安适和倦怠。我要上战场了，是吗？今夜我将经历平生最了不起的体验？也许今夜我就会死——就在上礼拜，红十字会还死了两个人——一个护士，一个医生。也许这些星星、这干草、这静静的夜，是最后一次围着我了。我不在乎——什么都挨不着我。我的灵魂在很远的地方，办它自己的事去了；没了灵魂的肉体多么快活，多么安逸，多么不受搅扰！我可以想象自己就这样摇摇晃晃上了战场，还没等灵魂赶回我身上，就死在了那儿。到那时我的灵魂可怎么办呢？另找一副躯壳，还是四处流浪，寻找我？一颗星，像一声欢乐或惊喜的呼喊似的闪出一道光，划过天幕坠落下去，其余的星星见了都颤动起来。

===Original===

My wagon stopped with a jerk. Some voice asked: what the devil were we doing filling the road with our carts at the exact moment that such-and-such a Division wished to move.

I heard Semyonov’s voice, very cold, official and polite. Then again: “Well, in God’s name, hurry then!﻿ ﻿… taking up the road!﻿ ﻿… hurry, I tell you!”

On we jogged again. Trenchard’s voice came to me: he had been, it might be, talking for some time.

“And so I’m not surprised, Durward, that you thought me a terrible fool to show my feelings as I’ve done this last fortnight. But you don’t know what it is to me﻿—to have something at last in your hands that you’ve dreamed of all your life and never dared to hope for: to have it and feel that at any moment it may slip away and leave you in a worse state than you were before. I’d been wishing, these last weeks, that I’d never met her, that I’d simply come to the war by myself. But now﻿—today﻿—when she spoke to me as she did, asked me to forgive her for what happened last night, my God, Durward! *I* to forgive *her*!﻿ ﻿… But I’ll show her this very night what I can do﻿—this very night! They’ll give me a chance, won’t they? It would be terrible if they didn’t. Semyonov won’t give me a chance if he can help it. What have I done to Semyonov that he should hate me? What have.﻿ ﻿…”

But I didn’t answer Trenchard. That part of me that had any concern with him and his affairs was far away. But his voice had stirred some more active life in me. I thought to myself now: Will there be some concrete definite moment in this affair when I shall say to myself: “Ah, there it is! There’s the heart of this whole business! There’s the enemy! Slay him and you have settled the matter!” or, perhaps, “Ah, now I’ve seen the secret. Now I’ve hunted the animal to his lair. This is war, this thing here. Now all my days I remain quiet. There is nothing more to fear”﻿—or would it be perhaps that I should face something and be filled, then, with ungovernable terror so that I should run for my life, run, hide me in the hills, cover up my days so that no one shall ever find me again?﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

我的车猛地停住了。有个声音质问：某师正要开进，我们凭什么偏在这节骨眼上拿大车堵住大路。

我听见谢苗诺夫的声音，非常冷淡、公事公办而又客气。接着又是一句：「行，看在上帝份上，那就快点！……占着大路！……快点，听见没有！」

我们又摇摇晃晃往前走。特伦查德的声音传到我耳朵里：说不定他已经说了半天了。

「所以说，德沃德，您这两个礼拜看我那样流露感情，把我当成天大的傻瓜，我一点也不奇怪。可您不知道这对我意味着什么——一辈子梦寐以求、从不敢存指望的东西，终于到了手里：拿到了它，却又时时觉得它随时会溜走，把你撂在比先前更糟的境地。这几个礼拜，我一直巴不得从没遇见过她，巴不得干脆一个人来打仗算了。可是如今——今天——当她那样同我说话，求我原谅她昨夜的所作所为时，我的上帝，德沃德！*我*来原谅*她*！……可就在今夜，我要让她瞧瞧我的能耐——就在今夜！他们会给我机会的，是不是？不给我机会就太可怕了。只要躲得开，谢苗诺夫是不会给我机会的。我到底对谢苗诺夫做了什么，他要这样恨我？我到底……」

可我没有答理特伦查德。我心里那点同他和他的事相干的部分，正在很远的地方。但他的声音搅起了我心里某种更活跃的东西。此刻我暗自想：这一遭里，会不会有某个确确实实的时刻，让我对自己说：「啊，就是它！这就是整桩事情的心脏！这就是敌人！杀死他，事情就了结了！」或者也许：「啊，如今我看见秘密了。我把这头畜生追到老巢了。这就是战争，就是眼前这个东西。往后我一辈子都心安了。再没有什么可怕的了」——又或者，我会面对什么东西，随即被一股不可遏制的恐怖攫住，于是没命地逃，逃进山里躲起来，把余生的日子掩埋掉，叫谁也再找不着我？……

===Original===

I raised myself on my elbow and looked at the country. We jolted over a little brook, brushed through a thicket of trees, came on to a path running at the forest’s foot, and saw on our left a little wooden house, a high wood fire burning in front of it. I looked at my watch. It was one o’clock. Already a very faint glow throbbed in the sky. Out of the forest, at long intervals, came a dull booming sound like the shutting of a heavy iron door.

The wagons drew up. We had arrived at our destination.

“We shall be here,” I heard Semyonov say, “some five hours or so. You’d better sleep if you can.”

===Chinese===

我支起胳膊肘，望着四野。我们颠过一条小溪，擦过一片树丛，上了林子脚下的小径，看见左边有座小木屋，屋前燃着一堆高高的篝火。我看看表。一点钟了。天边已有一线极淡的红光在搏动。森林深处，隔上好一阵，传来一声沉闷的轰响，像一扇沉重的铁门关上了。

大车停住了。我们到了目的地。

「我们要在这儿待，」我听见谢苗诺夫说，「大约五个钟头。能睡就睡吧。」

===Original===

A group of soldiers round the wood fire were motionless, their faces glowing, their bodies dark. Our wagons, drawn up together, resembled in the twilight strange beasts; the two Sisters lay down on one wagon, Semyonov, Andrey Vassilievitch, Trenchard and I on another. My irritated mood had returned. I had been the last to climb on to the straw and the others had so settled themselves that I had no room to lie flat. Semyonov’s big body occupied half the wagon, Andrey Vassilievitch’s boots touched my head and at intervals his whole body gave nervous jerks. It was also quite bitterly cold, which was curious enough after the warmth of the earlier nights. And always, at what seemed to be regular intervals, there came, from the forest, the banging of the iron door.

I felt a passionate irritation against Andrey Vassilievitch. Why could he not keep quiet? What, after all, was he doing here? I could hear that he was dreaming. He muttered some woman’s name:

“Sasha﻿ ﻿… Sasha﻿ ﻿… Sasha.﻿ ﻿…”

“Can’t you keep still?” I whispered to him, but in the cold I myself was trembling. The dawn came at last with reluctance, flushing the air with colour, then withdrawing into cold grey clouds, then stealing out once more behind the forest in scattered strips of pale green gold, then suddenly sending up into the heaven a flock of pink clouds like a flight of birds, that spread in extending lines to the horizon, covering at last a sky now faintly blue, with rosy bars. The flame of the soldiers’ fire grew faint, white mists rose in the fields, the cannon in the forest ceased and the birds began.

===Chinese===

篝火四周一圈士兵一动不动，脸膛发亮，身子乌黑。我们的大车聚拢在一处，在暮色里像几头怪兽；两位护士在一辆车上躺下，谢苗诺夫、安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇、特伦查德和我在另一辆上。我的烦躁又上来了。我最末一个爬上干草，别人都占好了地方，弄得我没法躺平。谢苗诺夫的大个子占了半个车，安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇的靴子抵着我的脑袋，他浑身上下隔一阵就神经质地抽动一下。夜里还冷得刺骨，先前几夜那么暖和，这倒也怪。而森林里那扇铁门，总是仿佛按着均匀的间隔，一记一记地撞响。

我对安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇涌起一股按捺不住的火气。他就不能安生点？说到底，他到这儿来干什么？我听得出他在做梦。他嘟哝着一个女人的名字：

「萨莎（Sasha）……萨莎……萨莎……」

「你就不能一动不动？」我低声对他说，可在寒气里我自己也在发抖。黎明终于迟迟疑疑地来了，先给空气染上一层红晕，随即又缩回冰冷的灰云里，接着又从森林背后偷偷溜出来，化作几道浅绿带金的微光，然后忽然朝天上放出一群粉红的云，像一群飞鸟，排成越来越宽的行列铺向天边，终于给这已微微发蓝的天空盖上一道道玫瑰色的彩带。士兵们篝火的火苗暗下去，田野里升起白雾，森林里的炮声停了，鸟儿叫起来了。

===Original===

I sat up on the cart, looked at my sleeping companions, and thought how unpleasant they looked. Semyonov like a dead man, Andrey Vassilievitch like a happy pig, Trenchard like a child who slept after a scolding. I felt intense loneliness. I wanted someone to comfort me, to reassure me against life which seemed to me suddenly now perilous and remorseless; moreover someone seemed to be reviewing my life for me and displaying it to me, laying bare all its uselessness and insignificance.

“But I’m in no way a fine fellow,” I could fancy myself crying. “I’m sleepy and cold and hungry. If you’ll remove Andrey Vassilievitch’s boots for me I’ll lie flat on this wagon and you can let loose every shrapnel in the world over my head and I’ll never stir. I thought I was interested in your war, and I’m not.﻿ ﻿… I thought no discomfort mattered to me, but I find that I dislike so much being cold and hungry that it outweighs all heroism, all sense of danger﻿ ﻿… let me alone!”

===Chinese===

我在车上坐起来，看看熟睡的同伴们，觉得他们一个个面目可憎。谢苗诺夫像具死尸，安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇像头快活的猪，特伦查德像个挨了骂才睡的孩子。我感到一阵剧烈的孤独。我需要有人安慰我，替我壮胆，好抵挡这突然显得凶险无情的人生；而且仿佛有什么人正在替我检阅我的一生，摊开给我看，把它的一切无用、一切微不足道，全亮了出来。

「可我哪里算得上什么好汉，」我恍惚听见自己在哭诉。「我又困又冷又饿。你们要是肯替我把安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇的靴子挪开，我就在这车上躺平，随你们把天下所有的榴霰弹都朝我头顶上放，我保证一动不动。我原以为我对你们这场战争有兴趣，其实并没有。……我原以为吃什么苦我都不在乎，可如今我发现，又冷又饿这滋味实在难受，难受得盖过了一切英雄气概、一切危险感……别来缠我！」

===Original===

Then something occurred. Looking down over the side of the cart I saw, to my great surprise, Marie Ivanovna.

“You!” I whispered.

“Hush!” she answered. “Come down.”

I let myself down and at once she put her hand into mine.

“Walk with me just a little way,” she whispered, “to those trees and back.” I had noticed at once that her voice trembled; now I perceived that her whole body was shaking; her hand gave little startled quivers under mine.

“You’re cold,” I said.

“No, I’m not cold,” she answered still in a whisper, although we were now some way from the wagons. “I’m frightened, Mr. Durward, that’s what’s the matter﻿—desperately frightened.”

===Chinese===

随后出了一件事。我从车帮上探头往下望，大大地吃了一惊：玛丽·伊万诺夫娜。

「您！」我低声道。

「嘘！」她答道。「下来。」

我翻身下车，她立刻把手伸进我手里。

「陪我稍稍走一走，」她低声说，「到那排树那儿，就回来。」我一下就听出她的声音在抖；现在我看清她整个人都在发抖；她的手在我掌心里一阵阵惊跳。

「您是冷的吧，」我说。

「不，我不是冷，」她还低声答道，尽管我们已经离大车有一段路了。「我是害怕，德沃德先生，就是这么回事——怕得要命。」

===Original===

“Nonsense,” I answered her. “You! Frightened! Never!”

“But I am. I’ve been terribly fr-frightened all night; and that Sister Anna Petrovna, he (she sometimes confused her pronouns) sleeps like a log. How can he? I’ve never slept, not for a moment, and I’ve been so cold and every time the cannon sounded I wanted to run away.﻿ ﻿… Oh, Mr. Durward, I’m so ashamed!”

Then, suddenly, desperately clutching my hand:

“Mr. Durward, you’ll never tell anyone, anyone never.﻿ ﻿… Promise!”

“Never a soul,” I answered. “It’s only because you’re cold and hungry and sleepy that you think you’re frightened. You’re not frightened really. But wouldn’t you like me to wake Trenchard and get him to come to you.﻿ ﻿… He’d be so happy?﻿ ﻿…”

She started fiercely from me. “Never! Never! Why, what *can* you think! You must never tell, most of all you must never tell him.﻿ ﻿… He must *never* know﻿—nothing﻿—”

===Chinese===

「瞎说，」我回答她。「您！害怕！哪会呢！」

「可我就是怕。我一整夜都怕、怕、怕得厉害；还有那位安娜·彼得罗夫娜护士，他（她有时会弄混人称代词）睡得像根木头。他怎么能睡得着？我是一刻也没合过眼，一直冷得要命，炮一响我就想逃。……哦，德沃德先生，我羞死了！」

随后，她忽然不顾死活地攥住我的手：

「德沃德先生，您千万别告诉任何人，永远别告诉任何人。……答应我！」

「打死也不说，」我回答。「您不过是又冷又饿又困，才觉得自己是害怕。您并不是真害怕。不过，要不要我去叫醒特伦查德，让他到您这儿来？……他不知要多高兴呢？……」

她猛地从我身边挣开。「不行！不行！哎呀，您都想到哪儿去了！您千万别说出去，尤其、尤其不能让他知道。……他*千万*不能知道——什么也不能——」

===Original===

The cannon began again. She caught my arm and stood with her body trembling, pressed against mine. I could feel her draw a deep breath. As I looked at her, her face white in the dawn, her large eyes staring like a child’s, her body so young and slender, she seemed another creature, utterly, absolutely apart from the woman of this last fortnight.

“Look here!” I said to her sternly. “You mustn’t go on like this. You’ve got work to do today. You’ve simply got to hold yourself in, to tell yourself that nothing can touch you. Why tonight you’ll laugh at me if I remind you of this. You’ll.﻿ ﻿…”

But there was better tonic than my words, Semyonov’s voice came to us﻿—“Hullo, you there! It’s five o’clock﻿—we’re moving.”

She drew herself sharply away from me. She raised her head, smiled at me, then said:

“Thank you, Mr. Durward. It’s all well now. There’s Dr. Semyonov﻿—let us go back.”

She greeted him with a voice that had in it not the slightest tremor.

===Chinese===

炮又响了。她抓住我的胳膊，身子发着抖贴着我站住。我觉出她深深吸了一口气。我望着她：晨光里一张苍白的脸，一双孩子般圆睁的大眼睛，一副那么年轻、那么单薄的身体——她仿佛换了一个生灵，同这半个月来的那个女人判然两人，隔着十万八千里。

「听着！」我严厉地对她说。「您不能这样下去了。您今天还有工作要做。您就得管住自己，对自己说：什么都挨不着我。瞧着吧，今夜我要再提这回事，您还要笑话我呢。您准会。……」

可有比我的话更灵的药——谢苗诺夫的声音传了过来：「喂，那边的！五点了——开拔了。」

她霍地从我身边抽身。她抬起头，朝我一笑，说道：

「谢谢您，德沃德先生。现在全好了。谢苗诺夫医生来了——我们回去吧。」

她招呼他时的嗓音里，没有一丝一毫的颤动。

===Original===

There comes now a difficult matter. During the later months when I was to reflect on the whole affair I saw quite clearly that that hour between our leaving the wooden house and arriving in the trenches bridged quite clearly for me the division in this business between imagination and reality: that is, I was never after this to speak of war as I would have spoken of it an hour before. I was never again to regard the paraphernalia of it with the curiosity of a stranger﻿—I had become part of it. This hour then may be regarded as in some ways the most important of all my experiences. It is certainly the occasion to which if I were using my invention I should make the most. Here then is my difficulty.

I have nothing to say about it. There’s nothing at all to be made of it.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

现在碰到一件难办的事。后来那几个月里，当我回顾这整桩事情时，我看得分明：从离开那座小木屋到抵达战壕之间的那一个钟头，恰恰为我架起了桥，跨过这件事里想象与现实的分界；这就是说，从此以后我再谈起战争，再不能像一个钟头之前那样谈法了。我也再不能用一个外人的好奇去打量它的种种行头——我已成了它的一部分。因此，这一个钟头，在某种意义上可以看作我全部经历里最要紧的一段。倘若我是在虚构故事，这自然是我要大肆铺陈的场合。于是，难处就在这儿。

我对它却无话可说。它根本成不了任何东西。……

===Original===

I may say at once that there was no atom of drama in it. At one moment I was standing with Marie Ivanovna under the sunrise, at another I was standing behind a trench in the heart of the forest with a battery to my left and a battery to my right, a cuckoo somewhere not very far away, and a dead man with his feet sticking out from under the cloth that covered him peacefully beneath a tree at my side. There had, of course, been that drive in the wagons, bumping over the uneven road whilst the sun rose gallantly in the heavens and the clanging of the iron door grew, with every roll of our wheels, louder and louder. But it was rather as though I had been lifted in a sheet from one life﻿—a life of speculation, of viewing war from a superior and safe distance, of viewing indeed all catastrophe and reality from that same distance﻿—into the other. I had been caught up, had hung for a moment in midair, had been “planted” in this new experience. For us all there must have been at this moment something of this passing from an old life into a new one, and yet I dare swear that not for any one of us was there any drama, any thrill, any excitement. We stood, a rather lonely little group, in the forest clearing whilst the soldiers in the trench flung us a careless glance, then turned back to their business of the day with an indifference that showed how ordinary and drab a thing custom had made it.

===Chinese===

我可以直截了当地说：这里面没有一丝一毫的戏剧性。前一刻，我还同玛丽·伊万诺夫娜一道站在日出底下；下一刻，我已站在森林深处一道战壕的背后，左边一个炮兵连，右边一个炮兵连，不远处什么地方有一只布谷鸟，我身边一株树下安然盖着一块布，布底下露出死人的一双脚。这中间自然还有一段坐大车的路程，一路颠簸在坑洼不平的路上，太阳威风凛凛地升上天穹，铁门的轰响随着车轮每一圈滚动，越响越近。可这与其说是走过去的，不如说是我被人兜在一床单子里，从一种生活里提了出来——那是一种揣测的生活，从一个居高临下、安全无虞的距离上观望战争、观望一切灾祸与真实的生活——放进了另一种生活。我被提了起来，在半空悬了一瞬，便被「栽」进了这崭新的经历里。在这一刻，我们大家想必都多少体验了这种从旧生活跨入新生活的渡越；然而我敢起誓，我们中间没有任何一个人觉出半点戏剧、半点震颤、半点兴奋。我们这一小群人孤零零地站在林间空地上，战壕里的士兵朝我们漫不经心地瞥了一眼，便回头去干他们白天的营生，那份漠然表明，习惯已把这营生磨成了多么平常、多么灰暗的一件事。

===Original===

Yes, we made a desolate little group. Semyonov had gone to a house on the farther side of the road up which we had come, a house that flew the Red Cross flag. We had only the right to care for the wounded of certain Divisions and our presence had to be reported. We were left then, Marie Ivanovna, Anna Petrovna, Andrey Vassilievitch, Trenchard and I, all rather close together, uncomfortable, desolate and shy, as boys feel on their first day at school. The battery on our left was very near to us and we could see the sharp flash of its flame behind the trees. The noise that it made was terrific, a sharp, angry, clumsy noise, as though some huge giant clad in mail armour was flinging his body, in a violent rage, against an iron door that echoed through an empty house﻿—my same iron door that I had heard all night. The rage of the giant spread beyond his immediate little circle of trees and one wondered at the men in the trenches because they were indifferent to his temper.

The noise of the more distant batteries was still, as it had been before, like the clanging of many iron doors very mild and gentle against the clamour of our own enraged fury. The Austrian reply seemed like the sleepy echo of this confusion, so sleepy and pleasant that one felt almost friendly to the enemy.

===Chinese===

是啊，我们这一小群人凄凄惶惶。谢苗诺夫到我们来时那条大路的另一头一户人家去了，那房子上飘着红十字会旗。我们只有权救护某些师的伤员，我们来了也得先报到备案。于是剩下了我们几个——玛丽·伊万诺夫娜、安娜·彼得罗夫娜、安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇、特伦查德和我——紧挨在一处，局促、凄惶、腼腆，像男孩子们头一天上学的光景。左边那个炮兵连离我们很近，我们看得见树背后它火光凌厉的一闪。它的声音大得吓人，尖利、暴怒、笨重，仿佛一个披锁子甲的巨大巨人，正狂怒地把整个身子朝一扇铁门上撞，铁门的轰响回荡在一座空房子里——正是我听了一整夜的那扇铁门。巨人的怒火漫出了他近旁那一小圈树木，战壕里的士兵对他的脾气居然无动于衷，倒叫人纳闷。

更远的那些炮连的声音，还同先前一样，像许多扇铁门在撞，跟我们自家这头暴怒的雷霆一比，温和轻柔得很。奥地利人的还击像是这场鼎沸里昏昏欲睡的回声，那么困倦、那么安详，几乎叫人对敌人心生好感。

===Original===

Our own battery was inconsistent in his raging. Had he only chosen to fling himself at his door every three minutes, say, or even every minute, we could have prepared ourselves, but he was moved by nothing, apparently, but his own irrational impulse. There would be a pause of two minutes, then three furious explosions, then a pause of five minutes, then another explosion.﻿ ﻿… I mastered quickly my impulse to leap into the air at every report, by a kind of prolonged extension in my mind of one report into another. Little Andrey Vassilievitch was not so successful. At each explosion his body jerked as though it had been worked by wires; then he glanced round to see whether anyone had noticed his agitation, then drew himself up, brushed off imaginary dust from his uniform, coughed and frowned. Trenchard stood close to Marie Ivanovna and looked at her anxiously once or twice as though he would like to speak to her, but she, holding herself very stiffly, watched with sternness the whole world as though she personally had arranged the spectacle and was responsible for its success.

===Chinese===

我们自家的炮连发起怒来没有定准。他哪怕只肯每隔三分钟——比方说——或者哪怕每隔一分钟朝他的门上撞一回，我们也好有个准备；可他分明全凭自己那阵没来由的性子行事。停上两分钟，接着三声狂怒的爆响，再停五分钟，又是一声。……每一声炮响都叫我跳起来的那股冲动，我很快就制住了，用的法子是在心里把上一声拖长，一直拖到下一声里去。小个子安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇就不这么成功了。每响一声，他的身子就抽动一下，像被线牵着的傀儡；随即他四下张望，看有没有人留意他的失态，然后挺直身子，掸掸军装上并不存在的尘土，咳嗽一声，皱起眉头。特伦查德紧挨着玛丽·伊万诺夫娜站着，有一两次忧心忡忡地望望她，好像有话想同她说；她却绷得笔直，严峻地监视着整个世界，仿佛这场大观是她亲自张罗的，成败都要她一力承担。

===Original===

Soon Semyonov came back and said that he must go on to some further trenches to discover the best position for us. To my intense surprise Andrey Vassilievitch asked whether he might accompany him. I fancy that he felt that he would venture anything to escape our adjacency to the battery.

So they departed, leaving us more forlorn than before. We sat down on the stretchers: Anna Petrovna, fat, heavy, phlegmatic, silent; Marie Ivanovna silent too but with a look now of expectation in her eyes as though she knew that something was coming for her very shortly; Trenchard near her, trying to be cheerful, but conscious of the dead soldier under the tree from whom he seemed unable to remove his eyes. There was, in the open space near us, a *kipiatilnik*, that is, a large boiler on wheels in which tea is made. To this the soldiers were crowding with their tin cans; the cuckoo, far away now, continued his cry.﻿ ﻿…

At long intervals, out of the forest, a wounded soldier would appear. He seemed to be always the same figure, sometimes wounded in the head, sometimes in the leg, sometimes in the stomach, sometimes in the hand﻿—but always the same, with a look in his eyes of mild protest because this had happened to him, also a look of dumb confidence that someone somewhere would make things right for him. He came either to us or to the Red Cross building across the road, according to his company. One soldier with a torn thumb cried bitterly, looking at his thumb and shaking his head at it, but he alone showed any emotion. The others suffered the sting of the iodine without a word, walking off when they were bandaged, or carried by our sanitars on the stretchers, still with that look of wonder and trust in their eyes.

===Chinese===
