不多时谢苗诺夫回来了，说他还得再往前去几处战壕，为我们找个最好的位置。安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇问能不能同他一起去，叫我大为惊讶。我猜想，只求躲开这个炮连，他什么都肯豁出去了。

他们就这么走了，把我们撂下，比先前更凄凉。我们在担架上坐下来：安娜·彼得罗夫娜，胖胖的，沉甸甸的，痰堵似的迟钝，一言不发；玛丽·伊万诺夫娜也不说话，眼里却添了一种期待的神色，仿佛知道有什么事情马上要轮到她头上；特伦查德坐在她近旁，强打精神，却总惦着树下那个死掉的士兵，眼睛像是怎么也挪不开。我们近旁的空地上有一座 *kipiatilnik*（俄语：开水锅炉），也就是一只带轮子的大锅炉，茶就在里头煮。士兵们端着白铁缸子朝它围拢过去；那只布谷鸟，如今远了，还在叫个不停。……

隔上好长时间，森林里就会走出一个伤兵。他仿佛永远同是那一个身影：有时头上挂彩，有时腿上，有时腹部，有时手上——可永远是那副模样，眼神里带着一缕温和的委屈，委屈这事偏偏落在自己头上，又带着一种无言的信赖，信总有什么人在什么地方会替他把一切安排妥帖。按着各人所属的连队，他们有的到我们这儿来，有的到路对面的红十字会房子去。有一个大拇指撕裂了的士兵哭得好伤心，望着自己的大拇指直朝它摇头；只有他一个人露了感情。其余的人碘酒蜇得再疼也不吭一声，裹好了绷带便走开，或者由我们的卫生员用担架抬走，眼里仍旧是那种又惊奇又信赖的神色。

===Original===

And how glad we were when there was any work to do! The sun rose high in the sky, the morning advanced, Semyonov and Andrey Vassilievitch did not return. For the greater part of the time we did not speak, nor move. I was conscious of an increasing rage against the battery. I felt that if it was to cease I might observe, be interested, feel excitement﻿—as it was, it kept everything from me. It kept everything from me because it insistently demanded my attention, like a vulgar garrulous neighbour who persists in his tiresome story. Its perpetual hammering had soon its physical effect. A sick headache crept upon me, seized me, held me. I might look at the soldiers, sleeping now like dead men in the trench, I might look at the Red Cross flag lazily flapping in the breeze across the road, I might look at the corpse with the soiled marble feet under the tree, I might look at Trenchard and Marie Ivanovna silent and unhappy on the stretchers, on Anna Petrovna comfortably slumbering with an open mouth, I might listen to the distant batteries, to the sudden quick impatient chatter of the machine guns, to the rattling give-and-take of the musketry somewhere far away where the river was, I might watch the cool green hollows of the forest glades, the dark sleepy shadows, the bright patches of burning sky between the branches, I might say to myself that all these things together made the impression of my first battle﻿ ﻿… and then would know, in my heart, that there was no impression at all, no thrill, no drama, no personality﻿—only a sick throb in my head and a cold hand upon my chest and a desire to fling myself into any horror, any danger, if I could but escape this indigestible monotony.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

所以一有活儿干，我们就多么高兴！太阳升到头顶，上午渐渐过去，谢苗诺夫和安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇没有回来。大半时间我们一言不发，一动不动。我觉出对那炮连的火气越积越大。我感到，它要是肯停一停，我也许还能看看、想想、激动激动——如今倒好，它把一切都从我手里夺走了。它把一切都夺走，因为它不由分说地霸占着我的注意，像个粗俗多嘴的邻居，死缠着讲他那套腻味的闲话。它没完没了的锤打，很快就在我身上见了功效。一场要命的头痛爬上来，攫住我，把我抓牢。我可以看战壕里睡得像死人似的士兵，可以看路对面红十字旗在微风里有气无力地拍动，可以看树底下那具大理石般脚踝污了的尸体，可以看担架上闷闷不乐、相对无言的特伦查德和玛丽·伊万诺夫娜，看张着嘴酣睡的安娜·彼得罗夫娜，可以听远处的炮连，听机枪突然爆出的那阵急躁的嗒嗒声，听远处河边什么地方的步枪一来一往的脆响，可以望林间空地里凉爽的绿荫、幽暗欲睡的暗影、枝桠间一块块燃烧的天空，我可以对自己说，这一切合起来，便是我第一场战斗的印象……随后心里便明白：根本没有什么印象，没有震颤，没有戏剧，没有个性——只有脑袋里病态的悸动，胸口上一只冰冷的手，还有一股子冲动：哪怕投进任何恐怖、任何危险里去，只要能躲开这没法消化的单调。……

===Original===

Once Trenchard, treading very softly as though everyone around him were asleep, came across and talked to me.

“You know,” he said in a whisper, “this isn’t at all what I expected.”

“You needn’t whisper,” I answered irritably, “that battery’s making such a noise that I can’t hear anything you say.”

“Yes, isn’t it!” he said with a little sigh. “It’s very unpleasant indeed. Do you think Semyonov’s forgotten us? We’ve been here a good many hours and we aren’t doing very much.”

“No,” I answered. “We’re doing nothing except get sick headaches.”

There was a pause, then he said:

===Chinese===

有一回，特伦查德蹑手蹑脚地走过来同我说话，放轻脚步，仿佛周围的人全睡着了。

「您知道，」他悄声说，「这跟我想的完全不是一回事。」

「用不着悄悄说，」我不耐烦地答道，「那炮连闹得这么凶，您说什么我反正也听不见。」

「可不是嘛！」他轻轻叹了口气。「实在很不舒服。您说谢苗诺夫是不是把我们忘了？我们在这儿待了好多个钟头，也没干什么正经事。」

「哪儿的话，」我回答。「我们干的事就只有一件：熬出满头的头痛。」

停了片刻，他说：

===Original===

“Where is everything?”

“Everything?﻿—What?”

“Well, the battle, for instance!”

“Oh, that’s down the hill, I suppose. We’re trying to cross the river and they’re trying to prevent us.”

“Yes,” he answered. “But that isn’t exactly what I mean.﻿ ﻿… It’s hard to explain, but even if we were to see our soldiers trying to cross the river and the Austrians trying to prevent them that wouldn’t be﻿—well, wouldn’t be exactly the real thing, would it? It would only be a kind of sideshow, rather unimportant like that dead man there!”

But my headache prevented my interest in his speculations. I said nothing.

===Chinese===

「都在哪儿呢？」

「一切？——什么一切？」

「喏，比如，战斗啊！」

「哦，那在山脚下呢，大概。我们正想法子渡河，他们正想法子拦我们。」

「是啊，」他答道。「可我要说的又不完全是这个。……这很难讲清楚；不过，就算我们亲眼看见我们的士兵抢渡、奥地利人阻拦，那也不算——呃，也不算真正的那件东西，对吧？那不过是一场余兴节目，无关紧要，就像那边那具死人一样！」

可我的头痛叫我提不起兴致理会他的玄想。我什么也没说。

===Original===

He added as though to himself:

“Perhaps each individual soldier sees the real thing for himself but can’t express what he sees.﻿ ﻿…”

As I still made no answer, with another little sigh he got up and walked back, on tiptoe, to the side of Marie Ivanovna.

Then suddenly, in the early hours of the afternoon, to our intense relief, Semyonov and Andrey Vassilievitch appeared. Semyonov was, as ever, short, practical, and unemotional.

“Been a long time, I’m afraid. We found it difficult to see exactly where would be the best place. And, after all, we’ve got to separate.﻿ ﻿… One Sister’s wanted at the Red Cross over there. They’ve asked for our help. The other will come with me on to the Position until this evening. You three gentlemen, if you’ll be so good, will wait here until a wagon comes. Then it will take you down to the trenches at the bottom of the hill. Then, if you don’t mind, I would like you to wait until dusk when we shall go out to fetch the wounded.﻿ ﻿… Is that clear?”

===Chinese===

他又说，像是自言自语：

「也许每一个士兵自己都看见了真正的那件东西，只是说不出他看见的是什么。……」

见我还是不答话，他又轻轻叹了口气，站起身，踮着脚，回到玛丽·伊万诺夫娜身边去了。

随后，下午刚到的时分，谢苗诺夫和安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇突然回来了，我们如释重负。谢苗诺夫照例是那样简短、务实、不动声色。

「去了很久，抱歉。究竟哪儿最合适，实在难看准。再说，我们终究得分开。……那边红十字会需要一位护士。人家求我们帮忙。另一位跟我上阵地去，待到今天傍晚。你们三位先生呢，劳驾，就在这儿等大车来。车会把你们送到山脚下的战壕那儿。然后，若是不嫌麻烦，我想请你们一直等到黄昏，我们再出去抬伤员。……清楚了吗？」

===Original===

We answered yes.

“Now which Sister will come with me? Marie Ivanovna, I think it would interest you. No danger, except a stray shrapnel or two. Will you come?”

There leapt upon us then, with an agitation that seemed to silence the very battery itself, Trenchard’s voice:

“No.﻿ ﻿… No﻿ ﻿… Marie. No, it’s dangerous. Semyonov says so. Your first day.﻿ ﻿…”

He spoke in English, his voice trembling. I turned to see his face white, his eyes wide open and at the same time blind; he passionately addressed himself to Marie Ivanovna and to her alone.

===Chinese===

我们回答：清楚了。

「那么哪位护士跟我去？玛丽·伊万诺夫娜，我想您会觉得有意思的。没有危险，顶多飞来一两发榴霰弹。您来吗？」

就在这时，特伦查德的声音扑到我们身上来，那一阵激动，仿佛连炮连本身都被压得哑了：

「不。……不……玛丽。不，危险。谢苗诺夫自己说的。这还是您头一天。……」

他说的是英语，声音发颤。我回过头，看见他脸色煞白，两眼圆睁，睁着却又什么都没看见；他热烈地只朝着玛丽·伊万诺夫娜一个人说话，只朝着她一个人。

===Original===

But she turned impatiently.

“Why, of course, Doctor. I’m ready at once.”

Trenchard put his hand on her arm.

“You are not to go﻿—Marie, do you hear? I have a right﻿ ﻿… I tell you, you are not to go!”

“Don’t be so stupid, John,” she shook off his arm. “Please, Doctor, I’m ready.”

Semyonov turned to Trenchard with a smile: “Mr. (they all called him Mr. now), it will be quite well﻿ ﻿… I will look after her.”

===Chinese===

可她不耐烦地转过身去。

「这还用说，医生。我马上就好。」

特伦查德把手搭上她的胳膊。

「您不能去——玛丽，听见没有？我有这个权利……我告诉您，您不能去！」

「别这么傻，约翰，」她甩开他的胳膊。「请吧，医生，我准备好了。」

谢苗诺夫含笑转向特伦查德：「先生（如今大家都这么称呼他了），不会有事的……我会照看她。」

===Original===

“You﻿ ﻿… you (Trenchard could not control his voice), you can’t prevent shrapnel﻿—bullets. You don’t care, you.﻿ ﻿…”

Semyonov’s voice was sharp: “I think it better that Sister Marie Ivanovna should come with me. You understand, the rest of you.﻿ ﻿… We shall meet at dusk.”

Trenchard only said “Marie﻿ ﻿…” then turned away from us. Anna Petrovna, who had said nothing during this scene and had, indeed, seemed to be oblivious of it, plunged with her heavy clumsy walk across the road to the Red Cross house. The Doctor and Marie Ivanovna disappeared behind the trench. I was, as was always my case with Trenchard, both sympathetic and irritated. It was difficult for him, of course, but what did he expect the girl to do? Could he have supposed for a single moment that she would remain? Could it be possible that he knew her so little as that? And why make a scene now before Semyonov when he obviously could do nothing? I knew, moreover, with a certainty that was almost ironic in its clarity, that Marie Ivanovna did not love, did not, perhaps, even care for him. By what moment in Petrograd, a moment flaming with their high purposes and the purple shadows of a Russian “white night,” had she been entranced into some glorious vision of him? On the very day that followed, she had known, I was convinced, her mistake. At the station she had known it, and instead of the fine Sir Galahad “without reproach” of the previous night she saw some figure that, had she been English born, would have appeared to her as Alice’s White Knight perchance, or at best the warmhearted Uncle Toby, or that most Christian of English heroes﻿—Parson Adams. I could imagine that life had been so impulsive, so straightforward, so simple a thing to her that this sudden implication in an affair complicated and even dishonest caused her bitter disquiet. Looking back now I could trace again and again the sudden flashes, through her happiness, of this distress.

He perhaps should have perceived it, but I could understand that he could not believe that his treasure had at last after all these years been given to him for so brief a moment. He could not, he would not, believe it. Well, I knew that his eyes must very soon be opened to the truth.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

「您……您（特伦查德管不住自己的声音了），您又挡不住榴霰弹——挡不住子弹。您不在乎，您……」

谢苗诺夫的声音陡然一厉：「我看，让玛丽·伊万诺夫娜护士跟我去更好。你们其余的人，听明白了。……黄昏时见。」

特伦查德只说了声「玛丽……」便从我们身边转过脸去。安娜·彼得罗夫娜在这一场里一言不发，其实仿佛压根儿没瞧见，迈着她那沉重笨拙的步子穿过大路，到红十字会房子去了。医生和玛丽·伊万诺夫娜没入战壕后头不见了。我对待特伦查德向来是又同情又气恼，这回也不例外。他自然不好受，可他指望那姑娘怎么做呢？他难道有一时半刻指望过她会留下来？他难道可能对她了解到这种地步都不及？再说，明摆着毫无办法，为什么偏当着谢苗诺夫的面闹这么一场？何况我还知道——知道得清清楚楚，清楚得近乎刻薄——玛丽·伊万诺夫娜并不爱他，甚至，恐怕连喜欢也谈不上。彼得格勒的那一时半刻——被他们崇高的憧憬和俄国「白夜」的紫色幽光映得通明的一刻——究竟凭着哪一刻，她才被他迷进一场关于他的辉煌幻梦里去的？我深信，就在第二天，她已经明白了自己的错。在车站她就已经明白了；前一夜那位纤尘不染的漂亮加拉哈德爵士（Sir Galahad），此刻在她眼里换成了另一个身影——她若是英国人生养的，这身影在她看来，说不定就是爱丽丝的白骑士，至多是热心肠的托比叔叔（Uncle Toby），再不就是英国英雄里顶基督徒的一位——帕森·亚当斯（Parson Adams）。我可以想象，生活之于她，本是一件多么率性、多么爽直、多么简单的事，如今突然缠进一桩复杂、甚至不太光明的纠葛里，叫她暗暗叫苦。如今回头看，我在她的幸福里，能一次又一次辨出这种苦恼的骤然一闪。

他本该也看出来的；可我明白，他不肯相信：他盼了这么些年的宝贝，好不容易到手，竟只给了他这么短短一晌。他不能相信，也不肯相信。唉，我知道，要不了多久，他的眼睛就得被迫睁开，看见真相。……

===Original===

As I turned to see him sitting on the stretcher with his back to me, his head hanging a little as though it were too heavy for his neck, his back bent, his long arms fallen loose at his sides, I thought that Alice’s White Knight he, in solemn truth, presented.

He had a talent for doing things to his uniform. His cap, instead of being raised in front, was flat, his jacket bulged out above his belt, and the straps on his boot had broken from their holdings. He filled the pockets of his trousers, in moments of absentminded absorption, with articles that he fancied that he would need﻿—sometimes food, black bread and sausage, sometimes a large pocketknife, a folding drinking glass, a ball of string, a notebook. These things protruded, or gave his clothes a strange bulky look, fat in some places, thin in others. As I saw him his shoulder-blades seemed to pierce his coat: I could fancy with what agitation his hands were clenched.

===Chinese===

我回过头，看见他背朝我坐在担架上，头微微耷拉着，仿佛脖子撑不住它的分量，背驼着，两条长胳膊松松地垂在身侧——我想，说句不掺假的话，他还真是一副爱丽丝的白骑士的做派。

他天生有本事把自己那身军装糟蹋得不成样子。帽子前檐该翘着的地方塌得平平的，军装上衣在腰带上方鼓出一坨，靴子上的扣带也从钩子上绷脱了。他一走神，就往裤袋里塞他觉得自己用得着的东西——有时是吃食，黑面包和香肠；有时是一把大弹簧折刀、一只折叠水杯、一团细绳、一个笔记本。这些东西在口袋里支棱着，把他的衣服撑出一副古怪的臃肿相：这里鼓一块，那里瘪一块。我望着他，只觉得他的肩胛骨简直要顶穿上衣；我想得出他的手在怎样焦灼地攥紧。

===Original===

We sat down, the three of us together, and again the battery leapt upon us. Now the sun was hot above the trees and the effect of the noise behind us was that we ourselves, every two or three minutes, were caught up, flung to the ground, recovered, breathless, exhausted, only to be hurled again!

How miserable we were, how lost, how desolate, Trenchard hearing in every sound the death of his lady, Andrey Vassilievitch dreaming, I fancy, that he had been caught in some cage out of which he would never again escape. I, sick, almost blind with headache, and yet exasperated, irritated by the emptiness of it all. If only we might run down that hill! There surely we should find.﻿ ﻿…

At the very moment when the battery had finished as it seemed to me its work of smashing my head into pulp the wagon arrived.

===Chinese===

我们三个挨着坐下，那炮连又朝我们扑来。此刻太阳在树顶上晒得正毒，身后那声响的效力便是：每隔两三分钟，我们便被它掀起来，掼倒在地，再爬起来，喘不上气，筋疲力尽——接着又被掀起来！

我们多么凄惶，多么没着没落，多么孤苦无依：特伦查德在每一声响里都听出他心上人的死讯；安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇，我猜想，正梦见自己落进了一只笼子，再也休想逃出去；我呢，病着，头痛得几乎睁不开眼，却又被这一切的空洞搅得火冒三丈。要是能顺着那山坡跑下去该多好！到了那儿，我们准能找着。……

就在炮连眼看要把我的脑袋砸成浆糊的当口，大车到了。

===Original===

Now, I thought to myself as I climbed on to the straw, I shall begin to be excited! We, all three of us, kneeling on the cart, peered forward into the dim blue afternoon. We were very silent﻿—only once Trenchard said to me, “Perhaps we shall find her down here: where we’re going. What do you think, Durward?”

“I’m afraid not!” I answered. “But still she’ll be all right. Semyonov will look after her!”

“Oh! Semyonov!” he answered.

===Chinese===

好了，我一面往干草上爬，一面想，这下我总该兴奋起来了！我们三个跪在车上，朝那蓝蒙蒙的下午里张望。我们都极沉默——只有一回，特伦查德对我说：「说不定到了下面，我们要去的地方，能碰上她。您说呢，德沃德？」

「怕是碰不上！」我答道。「不过她不会有事的。谢苗诺夫会照看她！」

「哦！谢苗诺夫！」他答道。

===Original===

How joyful we were to leave our battery behind us. As the trees closed around it we could fancy its baffled rage. Other batteries now seemed to draw nearer to us and the whole forest was filled with childish quarrelling giants; but as we began to bump down the hill out of the forest stranger sounds attacked us. On either side of us were cornfields and out of the heart of those from under our very feet as it seemed there were explosions of a strange stinging metallic kind﻿—not angry and human as the battery had been, but rather like some huge bottle cracking in the sun. These huge bottles﻿—one could fancy them green and shining somewhere in the corn﻿—cracked one after another; positively the sound intensified the heat of the sun upon one’s head. There were too now, for the first time in our experience, shrapnel. They were not over us, but ran somewhere on our right across the valley. Their sound was “fireworks” and nothing more﻿—so that alarm at their gentle holiday temper was impossible. Brock’s Fireworks on a Thursday evening at the Crystal Palace, oneself a small boy sitting with both hands between one’s knees, one’s mouth open, a damp box of chocolates on one’s lap, the murmured “Ah﻿ ﻿…” of the happy crowd as the little gentle “Pop!” showed green and red against the blue night sky. Ah! there was the little “Pop!” and after it a tiny curling cloud of smoke in the air, the whole affair so gentle, so kind even. There! sighing overhead they go! Five, six little curls of smoke, and then beneath our very horses’ feet again a huge green bottle cracking in the sun!

And with all this noise not a living soul to be seen! We had before us as we slowly bumped down the hill a fair view. The river was hidden from us, but there was a little hamlet guarded happily by a green wood; there was a line of fair hills, fields of corn, and the long dusty white road. Not a soul to be seen, only our bumping cart and, now and then, against the burning sky those little curling circles of smoke. The world slumbered.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

把那炮连甩在身后，我们多么欢喜。树木把它围拢起来时，我们仿佛还听得出它那无可发泄的狂怒。别的炮连如今像是朝我们逼近，整座森林里挤满了拌嘴赌气的孩儿巨人；可等我们颠颠簸簸驶下山、出了林子，更古怪的声音又来袭击我们了。两旁是麦田，那些怪响仿佛就从麦田深处、从我们脚底下炸开，带着一种奇异的、扎人的金属味——不像炮连那样暴怒而有人气，倒像一只大瓶子在太阳底下爆裂。这些大瓶子——你尽可以想象它们绿莹莹地立在麦子里的什么地方亮闪闪——一只接一只地炸开；那声音当真把太阳晒在头顶上的热又加了几分。还有，我们生平头一回，遇上了榴霰弹。它们不在我们头顶上，是在我们右边什么地方掠过谷地。那声音不过是「放烟火」，仅此而已——那份节日的温存脾气，叫人想惊慌也惊慌不起来。星期四晚上水晶宫（Crystal Palace）的布罗克烟火（Brock's Fireworks）：自己还是个小男孩，两手夹在膝盖中间坐着，嘴张着，腿上搁一盒受了潮的巧克力，随着轻轻一声「砰！」在蓝色夜空里绽出绿和红，幸福的人群里嗡起一阵「啊……」。啊！就是这轻轻的一声「砰！」，随后半空里卷起一缕小小的烟，整件事这么轻柔，甚至这么和善。瞧！它们叹息着打头顶上过去了！五缕、六缕小小的烟圈，跟着，又在我们马蹄底下，一只绿色大瓶子在太阳里爆裂！

闹出这么大动静，却看不见一个活人！我们慢慢颠下山坡，眼前是一片明朗的景色。河流叫什么挡住了看不见，可有一座小村庄，由一片葱茏的绿树快活地守着；有一列明媚的山冈，一片片麦田，还有那条长长的、尘土飞扬的白路。不见人影，只有我们这辆颠簸的大车，还有不时衬着灼热天空出现的那些小小的烟圈。整个世界在打盹。……

===Original===

Suddenly from the ditch at the side of the road a soldier appeared, spoke to our driver and disappeared again.

“What did he say?” I asked.

“He says, your Honour, that we must hasten. We may be hit.”

“Hit here﻿—on this road?”

“*Tak totchno.*”

“Well, hurry then.”

I caught a little frightened sigh behind me from Andrey Vassilievitch, whom the events of the day had frozen into horror-stricken silence. We hurried, bumping along; at the bottom of the hill there was a farmhouse. From behind it an officer appeared.

===Chinese===

忽然，路边的沟里冒出一个士兵，同我们的车夫说了句什么，又不见了。

「他说什么？」我问。

「他说，长官，咱们得赶快。说不定挨上一下。」

「挨打？这儿——这条路上？」

「*Tak totchno*.」

「那就快点吧。」

我听见身后安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇一声惊惧的轻叹——这一天的事，早把他冻成了一声不吭的惊惶。我们加紧颠簸着赶路；山脚下有一座农庄。农庄后头转出一位军官。

===Original===

“What are you doing there? You’re under fire.﻿ ﻿… Red Cross? Ah yes, we had a message about you. Dr. Semyonov?﻿ ﻿… Yes. Please come this way. Hurry, please!”

We were led across the farmyard and almost tumbled into a trench at the farther end of it.

It wasn’t until I felt someone touch my shoulder that I realised my position. We were sitting, the three of us, in a slanting fashion with our backs to the earthworks of the trench. To our right, under an improvised round roof, a little dried-up man like a bee, with his tunic open at the neck and a beard of some days on his chin, was calling down a telephone.

Next to me on the left a smart young officer, of a perfect neatness and even dandiness, was eating his supper, which his servant, crouching in front of him, ladled with a spoon out of a tin can. Beyond him again the soldiers in a long line under the farm wall were sewing their clothes, eating, talking in whispers, and one of them reading a newspaper aloud to himself.

A barn opposite us in ruins showed between its bare posts the green fields beyond. Now and then a soldier would move across the yard to the door of the farm, and he seemed to slide with something between walking and running, his shoulders bent, his head down. The sun, low now, showed just above the end of the farm roof and the lines of light were orange between the shadows of the barn. All the batteries seemed now very far away; the only sound in the world was the occasional sigh of the shrapnel. The farmyard was bathed in the peace of the summer evening.

===Chinese===

「你们在那儿干什么？你们在炮火底下。……红十字会？啊对，有过你们的通知。谢苗诺夫医生？……好。请这边走。快，请快！」

人家领我们穿过场院，走到尽头，我们几乎是跌进一道战壕里。

直到有人碰了碰我的肩膀，我才明白自己的处境。我们三个歪歪斜斜地坐着，背靠战壕的土垒。右边一顶搭起来的圆顶棚底下，一个蜜蜂似的干瘦矮小的男人，军装领口敞着，下巴上蓄着好些天的胡子，正对着电话往下喊。

我左手边是一位精干的年轻军官，穿戴齐整得完美，简直称得上讲究，正在吃晚饭；他的勤务兵蹲在他跟前，用一把勺子从白铁罐里舀给他。再过去，农庄墙根底下一长列士兵，有的缝衣裳，有的吃东西，有的低声说话，还有一个在自顾自念报纸。

对面一座谷仓塌了，光秃秃的柱子间露出后头的绿野。时不时有个士兵穿过场院朝农庄门口走去，他那走法介于走和溜之间，肩缩着，头低着。太阳已经低了，刚刚露出农庄屋脊的一头，光线在谷仓的暗影之间是橙色的。所有的炮连如今都仿佛离得很远；世上仅有的声音，是榴霰弹偶尔的一声叹息。场院浸在夏日黄昏的安宁里。

===Original===

The Colonel, when he had finished his conversation with some humorous sally that gave him great pleasure, greeted us.

“Very glad to see you, gentlemen.﻿ ﻿… Two Englishmen! Well, that’s the Alliance in very truth﻿ ﻿… yes.﻿ ﻿… How’s London, gentlemen? Yes, *golubchik*, that small tin﻿—the grey one. No, *durak*, the *small* one. Dr. Semyonov sent a message. Pray make yourselves comfortable, but don’t raise your heads. They may turn their minds in this direction at any moment again. We’ve had them once already this afternoon. Eh, Piotr Ivanovitch (this to the smart young officer), that would have made your Ekaterina Petrovna jump in her sleep﻿—ha, ha, ha﻿—oh, yes, but I can see her jumping.﻿ ﻿… Hullo, telephone﻿—Give it here! That you, Ivan Leontievitch? No﻿ ﻿… very well for the moment.﻿ ﻿… Two Englishmen here sitting in my trench﻿—truth itself! Well, what about the Second Rota? Are they coming down?﻿ ﻿… *Yeh Bogu*, I don’t know! What do you say?﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

上校打完了电话——收尾那句俏皮话让他自己乐不可支——招呼了我们。

「很高兴见到诸位先生。……两位英国人！好哇，这可真是同盟的成色了……是的。……伦敦怎么样啊，先生们？对，*golubchik*，那只小罐——灰的。不对，*durak*，*小*的那只。谢苗诺夫医生捎过话来了。诸位请自便，只是别抬头。他们随时可能再往这边动动念头。今天下午已经领教过一回了。呃，彼得·伊万诺维奇（Piotr Ivanovitch）——这句是对那位精干年轻军官说的——这一下保管叫你的叶卡捷琳娜·彼得罗夫娜（Ekaterina Petrovna）在梦里都跳起来——哈哈哈——哦没错，我看得见她跳的样子。……喂，电话——拿过来！是你吗，伊万·列昂季耶维奇（Ivan Leontievitch）？不……眼下还好。……我战壕里坐着两个英国人——千真万确！那么，第二连呢？他们下来不下来？……*Yeh Bogu*（俄语：说真的），我不知道！你说什么？……」

===Original===

The young officer, in a very gentle and melodious voice, offered Trenchard, who was sitting next to him, some supper.

“One of these cutlets?”

Trenchard, blushing and stammering, refused.

“A cigarette, then?”

Trenchard again refused and Piotr Ivanovitch, having done his duty, relapsed into his muffled elegance. We sat very quietly there; Trenchard staring with distressed eyes in front of him. Andrey Vassilievitch, very uncomfortable, his fat body sliding forward on the slant, pulling itself up, then sliding again﻿—always he maintained his air of importance, giving his cough, twisting the ends of his moustache, staring, fiercely, at someone suddenly that he might disconcert him, patting, with his plump little hands, his clothes.

The shadows lengthened and a great green oak that hung over the barn seemed, as the evening advanced, to grow larger and larger and to absorb into its heart all the flaming colours of the day, to press them into its dark shadow and to hide them, safe and contented, until another morning.

===Chinese===

那位年轻军官用非常温和悦耳的嗓音，请坐在他旁边的特伦查德吃晚饭。

「来一块肉饼？」

特伦查德红着脸、结结巴巴地谢绝了。

「那么，来支烟？」

特伦查德又谢绝了，彼得·伊万诺维奇尽过了礼数，便缩回他那含蓄的气派里去。我们安安静静坐在那儿；特伦查德痛苦地直望着前面。安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇浑身不自在，胖身子顺着斜坡往前出溜，挣起来，又出溜——架子却始终端着：咳嗽一声，捻捻小胡子梢，忽然狠狠盯谁一眼，好把人盯得发窘，再用胖乎乎的小手拍拍衣裳。

影子越拉越长，一棵巨大的绿橡树探在谷仓上空，随着夜色渐深，仿佛越长越大，把白昼全部燃烧的颜色都吸进它心里，压进它黑黑的浓荫里藏起来，稳稳当当、心满意足，藏到下一个早晨。

===Original===

I sat there and gradually, caught, as it seemed to me, into a world of whispers and half-lights, I slipped forward a little down into the dark walls of the trench and half-slumbered, half clung still to the buzzing voice of the Colonel, the languid replies of the young officer. I felt then that someone was whispering to me that my real adventure was about to begin. I could see quite plainly, like a road up which I had gone, the events of the day behind me. I saw the ride under the stars, the cold red dawn. Marie Ivanovna standing beneath my cart, the sudden battery and the desolate hours of waiting, the wounded men stumbling out of the forest, the ride down the hill and the green bottles bursting in the sun, the sudden silences and the sudden sounds, my own weariness and discomfort and loneliness and now Something﻿—was it the dark green oak that bent down and hid the world for me?﻿—whispered, “You’re drawing near﻿—you’re close﻿—you’re almost there.﻿ ﻿… In a moment you will see﻿ ﻿… you will see﻿ ﻿… you will see.﻿ ﻿…”

Somewhere the soldiers were singing, and then all sounds ceased. We were standing, many of us, in the dark, the great oak and many other giant trees were about us and the utter silence was like a sudden plunge into deep water on a hot day. We were waiting, ready for the Creature, breathless with suspense.

“Now!” someone cried, and instantly there was such a roar that I seemed to be lifted by it far into the sky, held, rocked, then dropped gently. I woke to find myself standing up in the trench, my hands to my ears. I was aware first that the sky had changed from blue into a muddy grey, then that dust and an ugly smell were in my eyes, my mouth, my nose. I remembered that I repeated stupidly, again and again: “What? what? what?” Then the grey sky slowly fell away as though it were pushed by some hand and I saw the faint evening blue, with (so strange and unreal they seemed) silver-pointed stars. I caught my breath and realised that now the whole right corner of the barn was gone. The field stretched, a dark shadow, to the edge of the yard. In the ground where the stakes of the barn had been there was a deep pit; scattered helter-skelter were bricks, pieces of wood, and over it all a cloud of thin fine dust that hovered and swung a little like grey silk. The line of soldiers was crouched back into the trench as though it had been driven by some force. From, as it appeared, a great distance, I heard the Colonel’s voice: “*Slava Bogu*, another step to the right and we’d not have had time to say ‘goodbye.’﻿ ﻿… Get in there, you﻿ ﻿… with your head out like that, do you want another?” I was conscious then of Andrey Vassilievitch sitting huddled on the ground of the trench, his head tucked into his chest.

===Chinese===

我坐在那儿，渐渐的，仿佛被摄进了一个耳语和微光的世界，身子朝前一滑，滑进战壕幽暗的壁影里，半睡半醒，一只耳朵还挂着上校嗡嗡的话音和年轻军官懒懒的应答。那时我觉得，有什么人在对我耳语，说我真正的奇遇就要开始了。这一天的事，像一条我走过的路，清清楚楚摆在身后。我看见星光底下的夜行，冷冷的红色黎明；玛丽·伊万诺夫娜站在我的车旁；突然降临的炮连和凄惶的等待时辰；伤兵们跌跌撞撞走出森林；下山路上在太阳里爆裂的绿瓶子；一阵阵的骤静和骤响；我自己的疲惫、不适和孤单——而此刻有个什么——是那棵俯下身来替我遮住世界的大绿橡树么？——在耳语：「你在靠近——近了——马上就到了。……再有一忽儿你就看见了……你就看见了……你就看见了。……」

什么地方士兵们在唱歌，随后一切声音都停了。我们许多人站在黑暗里，大橡树和许多别的巨树环着我们，那彻底的寂静，像大热天一头扎进深水里。我们等待着，准备迎那东西，屏住呼吸，心悬在半空。

「来了！」有人喊了一声，顿时一声炸响，我仿佛被它掀起，掀到高高的天上，悬着，荡着，又轻轻放下。我醒过来，发现自己站在战壕里，两手捂着耳朵。我先觉察到天已从蓝色变成了泥灰色，随后觉察到眼睛里、嘴里、鼻子里全是尘土和一股难闻的气味。我记得自己傻里傻气地翻来覆去说：「什么？什么？什么？」随后灰色的天慢慢褪下去，像被一只手推开，我看见了淡淡的暮蓝，还有（显得那么奇异、那么不真切）银尖的星星。我喘了口气，才明白过来：谷仓整个右边一角没有了。田地黑压压地一直延展到场院边上。原先立着谷仓柱子的地方，陷下去一个深坑；砖头、木片狼藉四散，上面悬着一层细细的粉尘，微微摆动，像灰色的绸子。那一列士兵缩回战壕里蜷着，像被什么力量搡进去的。从仿佛很远很远的地方，我听见上校的声音：「*Slava Bogu*，再偏右一步，我们连道声『再见』都来不及了。……给我进去，你……脑袋那么探着，还想再来一下？」随后我觉察到安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇蜷坐在战壕地上，脑袋深深埋进胸口。

===Original===

“You’re not hurt, are you?” I said, bending down to him.

He got up and to my surprise seemed quite composed. He was rubbing his eyes as though he had waked from sleep.

“Not at all,” he answered in his shrill little voice. “No.﻿ ﻿… What a noise! Did you hear it, Ivan Andreievitch?”

Did I hear it? A ridiculous question!

“But I assure you I was not alarmed,” he said eagerly, turning round to the young officer, who was rather red in the face but otherwise unruffled. “The first time that one has been so close to me. What a noise!”

Trenchard searched in his pockets for something.

===Chinese===

「没伤着吧？」我俯身问他。

他站起身，叫我吃惊的是，看上去相当镇定。他揉着眼睛，像刚睡醒似的。

「一点也没有，」他用他那尖细的小嗓音答道。「没有。……好响啊！您听见了吗，伊万·安德烈耶维奇？」

我听见了没有？问得多余！

「可我向您保证，我一点也没慌，」他急切地说，转向那位年轻军官——军官脸颇有些红，旁的倒还镇定。「头一回有这么一发挨我这么近。好响啊！」

特伦查德在口袋里摸索什么。

===Original===

“What is it?” I asked.

“My handkerchief!” he answered. “So dusty after that. It’s in my eyes!”

He tumbled on to the ground a large clasp pocketknife, a hunk of black bread, a cigarette-case and some old letters. “I had one,” he muttered anxiously. “Somewhere, I know.﻿ ﻿…”

I heard the Colonel’s voice again. “No one touched! There’s some more of their precious ammunition wasted.﻿ ﻿… What about your Ekaterina, Piotr Ivanovitch﻿—Ho, ho, ho!﻿ ﻿… Here, *golubchik*, the telephone!﻿ ﻿… Hullo! Hullo!”

For myself I had the irritation that one might feel had a boy thrown a stone over the wall, broken a window and run away. Moreover, I felt that again I had missed﻿—**it**. Always round the corner, always just out of sight, always mocking one’s clumsy pursuit. And still, even now, I felt no excitement, no curiosity. My feet had not yet touched the enchanted ground.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

「找什么？」我问。

「手绢！」他答道。「那一下下来全是土。我眼睛里全是！」

一只大弹簧折刀、一大块黑面包、一只烟盒和几封旧信，扑簌簌落到地上。「我本来是有一块的，」他焦灼地咕哝。「搁在哪儿了，我知道。……」

我又听见上校的声音。「一个人也没碰着！他们那金贵的弹药又糟蹋了几发。……你的叶卡捷琳娜怎么样啊，彼得·伊万诺维奇——嚯，嚯，嚯！……来，*golubchik*，电话！……喂！喂！」

我自己呢，只有一股子闷气，就像谁家孩子隔着墙扔石头砸破了窗子、撒腿跑了，你拿他没辙。而且我觉得，我又错过了**它**。总是在拐角上，总在视线外一点点，总在讥笑你那笨拙的追赶。而且，即便到了这会儿，我也不兴奋，不好奇。我的脚还没有踏上魔境（Enchanted Ground）。……

===Original===

The trench had at once slipped back into its earlier composure. The dusk was now creeping down the hill; with every stir of the breeze more stars were blown into the sky; the oak was all black now like a friendly shadow protecting me.

“There’ll be no more for a while,” said the Colonel. He was right. There was stillness; no battery, however distant, no pitter-patter of rifle fire, no chattering report of the machine guns.

Men began to cross the yard, slowly, without caution. The dusk caught us so that I could not see the Colonel’s face; a stream that cut the field, hidden in the day, was now suddenly revealed by a grinning careless moon.

Then a soldier crossed the yard to us, told us that Dr. Semyonov wished us to start and had sent us a guide; the wagons were ready.

At that instant, whence I know not, for the first time that day, excitement leapt upon me.

===Chinese===

战壕立时恢复了先前的镇定。暮色正悄悄爬下山去；风每动一动，天上就多吹亮几颗星；橡树如今全黑了，像一道护着我的友善的影子。

「暂时不会再有了，」上校说。他说对了。四下一片沉静；没有炮声，再远也没有；没有步枪的噼啪，没有机枪的嗒嗒。

人们开始穿过场院，慢慢地，不再躲闪。暮色罩住我们，我看不清上校的脸了；白天藏着的那条横过田野的小溪，此刻忽然让一轮没心没肺嬉笑着的月亮照了出来。

随后一个士兵穿过场院朝我们走来，说谢苗诺夫医生要我们出发，还给我们派了个向导；大车都备好了。

就在那一瞬，不知打哪儿来的，这一天里头一回，兴奋扑上了我。

===Original===

Events had hitherto passed before me like the shadowed film of a cinematograph; it had been as though someone had given me glimpses of a life, an adventure, a country with which I should later have some concern but whose boundaries I was not yet to cross. Now, suddenly, whether it was because of the dark and the silence I cannot say, I had become, myself, an actor in the affair. It was not simply that we were given something definite to do﻿—we had had wounded during the morning﻿—it was rather that, as in the children’s game we were “hot,” we had drawn in a moment close to someone or something of whose presence we were quite distinctly aware. As we walked across the yard into the long low field, speaking in whispers, watching a shaft of light, perhaps some distant projector that trembled in pale white shadows on the horizon, we seemed to me to be, in actual truth, the hunters of Trenchard’s dream.

Never, surely, before, had I known the world so silent. Under the hedges that lined the field there were soldiers like ghosts; our own wagons, with the sanitars walking beside them, moved across the ground without even the creak of a wheel. Semyonov was to meet us in an hour’s time at a certain crossroad. I was given the command of the party. I was now, in literal truth, breathlessly excited. My heart was beating in my breast like some creature who makes running leaps at escape. My tongue was dry and my brain hot. But I was happy﻿ ﻿… happy with a strange exaltation that was unlike any emotion that I had known before. It was in part the happiness that I had known sometimes in Rugby football or in tennis when the players were evenly matched and the game hard, but it was more than that. It had in it something of the happiness that I have known, after many days at sea, on the first view of land﻿—but it was more than that. Something of the happiness of possessing, at last, some object which one has many days desired and never hoped to attain﻿—but more, too, than that. Something of the happiness of danger or pain that one has dreaded and finds, in actual truth, give way before one’s resolution﻿—but more, again, than that. This happiness, this exultation that I felt now but dimly, and was to know more fully afterwards (but never, alas, as my companions were to know it) is the subject of this book. The scent of it, the full revelation of it, has not, until now, been my reward; I can only, as a spectator, watch that revelation as it came afterwards to others more fortunate than I. But what I write is the truth as far as I, from the outside, have seen it. If it is not true, this book has no value whatever.

===Chinese===

在这之前，种种事件都像电影里黯淡的影子，从我眼前过去；仿佛有谁让我瞥了一眼一种生活、一场奇遇、一个国度——我日后会同它有些干系，却还不许跨过它的边界。此刻，突然地——是不是因为天黑和寂静，我说不上——我自己成了这件事里的一个角色。倒不单单是因为有了件确凿的差事——上午我们也接过伤员——而是，就像孩子们玩的游戏里喊的「近了，热了」，我们一下子逼近了某个人、或某个东西，而它就在跟前，我们感觉得清清楚楚。我们穿过场院，走进那片长长的低洼田地，耳语着，望着一道直竖的光柱——也许是远处的一架探照灯，在天边上化作苍白的白影颤抖着——在我看来，我们当真成了特伦查德梦里的那些猎人。

毫无疑问，我从不曾知道世界能这样安静。沿田边的树篱底下趴着些士兵，像一群鬼魂；我们自己的大车，卫生员走在旁边，碾过地面，连一声轮响都没有。谢苗诺夫约好一个钟头后在某个十字路口同我们会合。这一队人交给了我指挥。此刻我当真是不折不扣地、屏住呼吸地兴奋了。我的心在胸膛里跳，像一头左冲右突、连蹦带跳想逃出去的活物。我舌头发干，脑子发热。可我快活……快活得带着一种奇异的昂扬，同我从前体验过的任何情绪都不一样。它有几分像拉格比橄榄球赛或网球赛上偶尔尝到的那种快活——双方旗鼓相当、战得难解难分之时；可又不止于此。它有几分像在海上熬了许多天之后初见陆地的那种快活——可又不止于此。有几分像盼望了许多日子、从不敢指望到手的东西终于攥在手里时的快活——可又不止于此。有几分像你提心吊胆的危难或苦痛，当真在你的决心面前退让了的快活——可又不止于此。这种快活，这种昂扬，此刻我还只是隐隐约约地觉着，要到后来才体味得更充分（可叹，永远不及我的同伴们体味的那样充分）——正是这本书要写的东西。它的气息，它全然的启示，至今不曾成为我的报偿；我只能像个旁观者，看它后来如何降临到比我幸运的人们身上。但我写下的，是我这个局外人亲眼所见的实情。倘若它不是真的，这本书便一文不值。

===Original===

We were warned by the soldier who guarded us not to walk in a group and we stole now, beneath a garden-wall, white under the moon, in a long line. I could hear Trenchard behind me stumbling over the stones and ruts, walking as he always did with little jerks, as though his legs were beyond his control. We came then on to the high road, which was so white and clear in the moonlight that it seemed as though the whole Austrian army must instantly whisper to themselves: “Ah, there they are!” and fire. The ditch to our right, as far as I could see, was lined with soldiers, hidden by the hedge behind them, their rifles just pointing on to the white surface of the land. Our guide asked them their division and was answered in a whisper. The soldiers were ghosts: there was no one, save ourselves, alive in the whole world.﻿ ﻿…

Then a little incident occurred. I was walking in the rear of our wagons that I might see that all were there. I felt a touch on my arm and found Andrey Vassilievitch standing in the middle of the road. His face, staring at me as though I were a stranger, expressed desperate determination.

“Come on,” I said. “We’ve no time to waste.”

“I’m not coming,” he whispered back. His voice was breathless as though he had been running.

===Chinese===

护送我们的士兵告诫我们不要聚成一群走，于是此刻我们贴着一道花园墙脚下，排成长长一列，蹑手蹑脚地潜行，墙在月光里泛着白。我听得见身后特伦查德在石头和车辙上磕磕绊绊，走路还是他那老样子，一顿一顿的，仿佛两条腿不听他调遣。随后我们上了大道。大道在月光下白得那么分明，仿佛整支奥地利军队立刻就要交头接耳：「啊，他们在那儿！」随即开火。我们右边的沟里，望到多远都是士兵，藏在身后树篱的掩护下，枪口刚好指着那发白的地面。向导低声问他们是哪个师的，得到的是一声耳语的回答。士兵们是鬼魂：整个世界上，除了我们自己，没有一个活人。……

随后出了个小插曲。我走在车队末尾，好看着一个都不掉队。忽然胳膊上被碰了一下，回头看见安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇站在路中央。他盯着我的那张脸，仿佛我是个陌生人，一副绝望的、下了决心的神情。

「走吧，」我说。「我们没工夫耽搁。」

「我不去了，」他低声回我。他上气不接下气，像刚跑完一程。

===Original===

“Nonsense,” I answered roughly, and I put my hand on his arm. His body trembled in jerks and starts.

“It’s madness﻿ ﻿… this road﻿ ﻿… the moon.﻿ ﻿… Of course they’ll fire.﻿ ﻿… We’ll all be killed. But it isn’t﻿ ﻿… it isn’t﻿ ﻿… I can’t move.﻿ ﻿…”

“You *must* move.﻿ ﻿… Come, Andrey Vassilievitch, you’ve been brave enough all day. There’s no danger, I tell you. See how quiet everything is. You *must*.﻿ ﻿…”

“I can’t.﻿ ﻿… It’s nothing﻿ ﻿… nothing to do with me.﻿ ﻿… It’s awful all day﻿—and now this!”

===Chinese===

「胡说，」我粗暴地答道，一把握住他的胳膊。他的身子一阵一阵、抽风似的发抖。

「这是疯了……这条路……这月亮。……他们当然会开枪。……我们全得死。可不是这么回事……不是……我动不了。……」

「您*必须*动。……来，安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇，您今天一整天够勇敢的了。没有危险，我告诉您。瞧，四下多安静。您*必须*走。……」

「我走不了。……没什么……跟我没关系。……一整天都可怕极了——现在又来这个！」

===Original===

I thought of Marie Ivanovna early in the morning. I looked down the road and saw that the wagons were slowly moving into the distant shadows.

“You *must* come,” I repeated. “We can’t leave you here. Don’t think of yourself. And nothing can touch you﻿—nothing, I tell you.”

“I’ll go back, I must. I can’t go on.”

“Go back? How can you? Where to? You can’t go back to the trench. We shan’t know where to find you.” A furious anger seized me; I caught his arm. “I’ll leave you, if you like. There are other things more important.”

I move away from him. He looked down the long road, looked back.

===Chinese===

我想起清晨的玛丽·伊万诺夫娜。我朝路那头望去，大车正慢慢没入远处的暗影里。

「您*必须*来，」我又说了一遍。「我们不能把您撂在这儿。别光想自己。什么都挨不着您——什么也挨不着，我告诉您。」

「我要回去，非回去不可。我走不下去了。」

「回去？怎么回？回哪儿去？战壕您回不去了。回头我们上哪儿找您。」一股狂怒攫住了我；我抓住他的胳膊。「您愿意，我就把您撂下。还有更要紧的事等着我。」

我抽身离开他。他望了望长长的大路，又回头望了望。

===Original===

“Oh, I can’t﻿ ﻿… I can’t,” he repeated.

“What did you come for?” I whispered furiously. “What did you think war was?﻿ ﻿… Well, goodbye, do as you please!”

As I drew away I saw a look of desperate determination in his eyes. He looked at me like a dog who expects to be beaten. Then what must have been one of the supreme moments of his life came to him. I saw him struggle to command, with the effort of his whole soul, his terror. For a moment he wavered. He made a hopeless gesture with his hand, took two little steps as though he would run into the hedge amongst the soldiers and hide there, then suddenly walked past me, quickly, towards the wagons, with his own absurd little strut, with his head up, giving his cough, looking, after that, neither to the right, nor to the left.

In silence we caught up the wagons. Soon, at some crossroads, they came to a pause. The guide was waiting for me. “It would be better, your Honour,” he whispered, “for the wagons to stay here. We shall go now simply with the stretchers.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

「唉，我不行……我不行，」他翻来覆去地说。

「那您来干什么？」我愤怒地低声说。「您以为战争是什么？……好吧，再见，随您的便！」

我抽身走开时，看见他眼里那股绝望的决断。他望着我，像一条等着挨打的狗。随后，他一生中最了不起的时刻之一——必是如此——降临到他的头上。我看着他调动整个灵魂的力气，去制服他的恐怖。有一瞬间他动摇了。他做了个绝望的手势，朝树篱那边、朝士兵堆里挪了两小步，好像要钻进去藏起来，随后却猛地从我身边走过去，快步朝大车走去，迈着他那副可笑的小官步，昂着头，咳嗽一声，打那以后，便目不斜视，左也不看，右也不看。

我们默不作声地赶上了大车。不多时，在一处十字路口，车停了下来。向导在等我。「最好，长官，」他悄声说，「大车留在这儿。我们现在只带担架走。……」

===Original===

We left the wagons and, some fifteen of us, turned off down a lane to the left. Sometimes there were soldiers in the hedges, sometimes they met us, slipping from shadow to shadow. Always we asked whether they knew of any wounded. We found a wounded soldier groaning under the hedge. One leg was soaked in blood and he gave little shrill desperate cries as we lifted him on to the stretcher. Another soldier, lying on the road in the moonlight, murmured incessantly: “*Bojé moi! Bojé moi! Bojé moi!*” But they were all ghosts. We alone, in that familiar and yet so unreal world, were alive. When a stretcher was filled, four sanitars turned back with it to the wagons, and we were soon a very small party. We arrived at a church﻿—a large fantastic white church with a green turret that I had seen from the opposite hill in the morning. Then it had seemed small and very remote. I had been told that much firing had been centring round it, and it seemed now for me very strange that we should be standing under its very shadow, its outline so quiet and grave under the moon, with its churchyard, a little orchard behind it, and a garden, scenting the night air, close at hand. Here in the graveyard there was a group of wounded soldiers, in their eyes that look of faithful expectation of certain relief. Our stretchers were soon full.

We were about to turn back when suddenly the road behind us was filled with shadows. As we came out of the churchyard an officer stepped forward to meet us. We saluted and shook hands. He seemed a boy, but stood in front of his men with an air as though he commanded the whole of this world of ghosts.

===Chinese===

我们撇下大车，一行十五来个人，折进左边一条小巷。树篱里有时有士兵，有时他们迎面遇上我们，从一道影子滑进另一道影子。我们逢人便问，可知道哪儿有伤员。我们找到一个伤兵，在树篱底下呻吟。一条腿浸透了血，我们把他抬上担架时，他发出一声声尖细绝望的叫喊。另一个伤兵躺在月光里的大路上，没完没了地喃喃着：「*Bojé moi! Bojé moi! Bojé moi!*」可他们全是鬼魂。在那个熟悉得亲切、却又如此不真切的世界里，唯有我们是活的。一副担架抬满了，四个卫生员便抬着折回大车那儿去，我们这一小队人很快就只剩了没几个。我们走到一座教堂跟前——一座宽大、奇异的白教堂，带一座绿塔楼，早上我从对面山冈上望见过它。那时它显得那么小、那么遥远。我听说，多少天来炮火一直围着它打转；如今我们竟站在它的影子里，它的轮廓在月色下那么安静、那么庄重，教堂墓地就在跟前，后头一座小果园，还有一座花园，夜气里都是它的香气——这一切，在我看来真是奇异极了。墓地里聚着一小群伤兵，眼里是那种笃定会得救的忠实期待。我们的担架很快就满了。

我们正要折返，身后的路忽然挤满了人影。我们走出教堂墓地时，一位军官迎上前来。我们敬礼、握手。他还是个少年模样，站在他的士兵们前头，那气派，仿佛这整个鬼魂的世界都归他统辖。

===Original===

“What are you doing here?” he asked.

We explained.

“Well, if you’ll excuse me, you’d better make haste. An attack very shortly﻿ ﻿… yes. I should advise you to be out of this. Petrogradsky Otriad? Yes﻿ ﻿… very glad to have the pleasure.﻿ ﻿…”

We left him, his men a grey cloud behind him, and when we had taken a few steps he seemed, with his young air of importance, his happy serious courtesy, to have been called out of the ground, then, with all his shadows behind him, to have been caught up into the air. These were not figures that had anything to do with the little curling wreaths of smoke, the bottles cracking in the sun, our furious giants of the morning.

“Ah, *bojé moi, bojé moi*!” sighed the wounded.﻿ ﻿… It was impossible, in such a world of dim shadow, that there should ever be any other sound again.

===Chinese===

「你们在这儿做什么？」他问。

我们解释了。

「那么，恕我直言，你们最好抓紧。马上要进攻了……是的。我劝你们赶紧离开这儿。彼得格勒支队（Petrogradsky Otriad）？好……有幸相见，非常高兴。……」

我们离开了他，他的士兵在他身后像一朵灰云；我们才走出几步，他连同他那股少年老成的气派、那愉快而郑重的客气，就仿佛先是被人从地底下唤出来的，此刻又带着身后那一团团的影子，被收到天上去了。这样的人物，同那些小小的烟圈、同太阳里爆裂的瓶子、同我们清晨那些狂怒的巨人，全不相干。

「啊，*bojé moi, bojé moi*！」伤兵们叹息着。……在这片朦胧影子的世界里，仿佛再不可能有别的什么声音了。

===Original===

My excitement had never left me; I had had no doubt, during this last half-hour, that I was on the Enchanted Ground of the Enemy, so stray and figurative had been my impressions all day. Now they were all gathered into this half-hour and the whole affair received its climax. Ah, I thought to myself, if I might only stay here now I should draw closer and closer﻿—I should make my discovery, hunt him down. But just when I am on the verge I must leave it all. Ah, if I could but stay!

Nevertheless we hastened. The world, in spite of the ghosts, was real enough for us to be conscious of that attack looming behind us. We found our wagons, transferred our wounded, then hurried down the road. We found the crossroads and there, waiting for us, Semyonov and Marie Ivanovna. Standing in the moonlight, commanding, as it seemed to me, all of us, even Semyonov, she was a very different figure from the frightened girl of the early morning. Now her life was in her eyes, her body inflamed with the fire of the things that had come to her. So young in experience was she, so ignorant of all earlier adventure, that she could well be seized, utterly and completely, by her new vision﻿ ﻿… possessed by some vision she was.

===Chinese===

我的兴奋一直没有离开过我；这最后半个钟头里，我毫不怀疑，我已经踏上了敌人的魔境——我这一天的印象，原本是那样零散、那样带着比喻的意味，如今全都收拢进这半个钟头里，整桩事情升到了顶点。啊，我暗自想，要是此刻能留在这儿就好了，我会越逼越近——我会发现他，把他追到手。可偏偏在眼看就要得手的边上，我却必须丢开这一切。啊，要是我能留下来该多好！

尽管如此，我们还是加紧了脚步。这个世界，尽管满是鬼魂，还是够真实的——我们感觉得到，身后那场进攻正在逼近。我们找到大车，移交了伤员，便顺着大路疾走。我们到了十字路口，谢苗诺夫和玛丽·伊万诺夫娜正在那儿等我们。她站在月光里，在我看，统辖着我们所有的人，连谢苗诺夫也在内——同清晨那个吓坏了的姑娘相比，她已是另一个人了。如今她的生命都到了她眼睛里，她的身体被降临到她身上的种种事情燃得通红。论阅历她是那么年轻，先前的种种历练一概不知，所以尽可以被她的新幻象整个儿攫住……她确是被什么幻象附了体。

===Original===

And that vision was not Trenchard. Seeing her, he hurried towards her, with a glad cry:

“Ah, you are safe!”

But she did not notice him.

“Quick, this way!﻿ ﻿… Yes, the stretchers here.﻿ ﻿… No, I have everything.﻿ ﻿… At once. There is little time!”

The wounded were laid on the stretchers in the square of the crossroads. Semyonov and Marie Ivanovna bandaged them under the moonlight and with the aid of electric-torches. On every side of me there were little dialogues: “No﻿ ﻿… not there. More this way. Yes, that bandage will do. It’s fresh. Hold up his leg. No, *durak*, under the knee there.﻿ ﻿… Where’s the lint?﻿ ﻿… Turn him a little﻿—there﻿—like that. *Horosho, golubchik. Seitchass!* No, turn it back over the thigh. Now, once more﻿ ﻿… that’s it. What’s that﻿—bullet or shrapnel?﻿ ﻿… Take it back again, over the shoulder.﻿ ﻿… Yes, twice!”

===Chinese===

而那幻象并不是特伦查德。他一看见她，欢呼一声，急忙朝她奔去：

「啊，您没事！」

可她没有理会他。

「快，这边！……对，担架放这儿。……不，我什么都齐了。……马上。时间不多了！」

伤员们在十字路口的空场上被安放到担架上。谢苗诺夫和玛丽·伊万诺夫娜就着月光、借着电筒替他们包扎。我四下里都是一段段小小的对话：「不……不在那儿。往这边点。对，这条绷带行。是新的。把他腿抬住。不对，*durak*，膝盖底下那儿。……绒布在哪儿？……把他稍微翻一下——对——就这样。*Horosho, golubchik. Seitchass!*（俄语：好了，小鸽子。马上！）不，再翻回大腿上边。来，再来一回……这就对了。这是什么——子弹还是榴霰弹？……再绕回去，从肩上。……对，两道！」

===Original===

Once I caught sight of Trenchard, hurrying to be useful with the little bottle of iodine, stumbling over one of the stretchers, causing the wounded man to cry out.

Then Semyonov’s voice angrily:

“*Tchort!* Who’s that?﻿ ﻿… Ah, Meester! of course!”

Then Marie Ivanovna’s voice: “I’ve finished this, Alexei Petrovitch.﻿ ﻿… That’s all, isn’t it?”

These voices were all whispers, floating from one side of the road to the other. The wounded men were lifted back on to the wagons. We moved off again; Semyonov, Trenchard, Marie Ivanovna and I were now sitting together.

===Chinese===

有一眼我瞥见特伦查德，攥着那只小碘酒瓶赶着去帮忙，绊在一张担架上，惹得那个伤兵大叫起来。

接着是谢苗诺夫怒冲冲的声音：

「*Tchort*（俄语：见鬼）！谁？……啊，米斯特！除了你还有谁！」

随后是玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的声音：「这个我包完了，阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇。……就这些了吧？」

这些声音全是耳语，从大路这边飘到那边。伤员们又被抬回大车上。我们重新开拔；谢苗诺夫、特伦查德、玛丽·伊万诺夫娜和我坐在一处的车上。

===Original===

We left the flat fields where we had been so busy. Very slowly we began to climb the hill down which I had come this afternoon. Behind me was a great fan of country, black now under a hidden moon, dead as though our retreat from it, depriving it of the last proofs of life, had flung it back into nonexistence. Before us was the black forest. Not a sound save the roll of our wheels and, sometimes, a cry from one of the wounded soldiers, not a stir of wind.﻿ ﻿…

I looked back. Without an instant’s warning that dead world, as a match is set to a waiting bonfire, broke into flame. A thousand rockets rose, soaring, in streams of light into the dark sky; the fields that had been vapour ran now with light. A huge projector, the eye, as it seemed to me, of that enemy for whom I had all day been searching, slowly wheeled across the world, cutting a great path across the plain, picking houses and trees and fields out of space, then dropping them back again. The rockets were gold and green, sometimes as it seemed ringed with fire, sometimes cold like dead moons, sometimes sparkling and quivering like great stars. And with this light the whole world crackled into sound as though the sky, a vast china plate, had been smashed by some angry god and been flung, in a million pieces, to earth. The rifle-fire rose from horizon to horizon like a living thing. Now the shrapnel rose, breaking on the dark sky in flashes of fire. Suddenly some house was burning! The flames rose in a column, breaking into tongues that advanced and retreated, climbed and fell again. In the farthest distance other houses had caught and their glow trembled in faint yellow light fading into shadow when the projector found them. With a roar at our back our own cannon began; the world bellowed and shook and trembled at our feet.

===Chinese===

我们离开了那片忙碌了许久的平川田地。车子很慢很慢地，开始爬我下午下来的那面山坡。我身后，一整幅扇面展开的原野，在藏起来的月亮底下黑着，死了一般——仿佛我们从那里退走，夺走了它最后一点生命的凭据，便把它扔回了不存在里。我们前面，是黑黑的森林。没有一点声音，只有车轮的辘辘，偶尔一声伤兵的呻唤；没有一丝风。……

我回头望去。毫无半点预兆，那个死了的世界，像一根火柴凑向备好的篝火，轰地燃烧起来。千百枚信号弹腾空而起，曳着一道道光流，飞进黑沉沉的夜空；先前雾气似的田野，如今到处流着光。一架巨大的探照灯——我看，那就是我寻了一整天的那个敌人的眼睛——缓缓扫过这个世界，在平原上豁开一条大路，把房屋、树木、田地从虚空里拣出来，又把它们抛回去。信号弹有金有绿，有时仿佛箍着一圈火，有时冷得像死掉的月亮，有时又闪烁颤抖，像巨星。伴随着这片光，整个世界噼里啪啦炸出声来，仿佛天空——一只巨大的瓷盘——被哪个愤怒的神掼碎，摔作千万片，撒落到大地上。步枪的火力从一个天际升到另一个天际，像一头活物。榴霰弹升起来了，在暗夜的天幕上迸出一簇簇火光。忽然有什么房子烧着了！火焰升成一根火柱，崩出一条条火舌，忽进忽退，忽升忽落。最远处又有几处房子着了火，火光微微发黄地颤动着，探照灯寻到它们时，又淡入暗影。我们身后一声轰鸣，我们自己的大炮开腔了；世界吼叫起来，在我们脚底下发抖、震颤。

===Original===

We reached the top of the hill. I caught one final vision, the picture seeming to sway with all its lights, its shadows, its giant eye that governed it, its colours and its mist, like a tapestry blown by wind. I saw in our wagon, their faces lighted by the fire, Semyonov and Marie Ivanovna. Semyonov knelt on the wooden barrier of the cart, his figure outlined square and strong. She was kneeling behind him, her hands on his shoulders. Her face was exultant, victorious. She seemed to me the inspirer of that scene, to have created it, to hold it now with the authority of her gaze.

Behind her Trenchard was in shadow.

We were on the hilltop, the cannon, as it seemed, on every side of us. We hung for a moment so, the sky flaming up to our feet. Then we had fallen down between the woods, every step muffling the sounds. Everything was dark as though a curtain had been dropped.

===Chinese===

我们到了山顶。我捕捉到最后一眼：整幅图景连同它的光、它的影、它那主宰一切的巨眼、它的色彩和薄雾，仿佛在摇曳，像一挂被风吹动的织锦。我看见我们的车上，火光映着谢苗诺夫和玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的脸。谢苗诺夫跪在车前的木栏上，身影方正而遒劲，衬在夜色里。她跪在他身后，两手搭着他的肩。她的脸昂扬欢喜，是一张打了胜仗的脸。在我看来，她就是那一整场场面的灵感之源，是它的创造者，此刻正用她目光的威权把它握在手里。

她身后的特伦查德，隐在暗影里。

我们在山顶上，大炮仿佛围着我们四面八方。有那么一瞬，我们就这样悬着，天火烧到我们脚边。随后我们降进森林之间，每走一步，声响便被捂去一分。四下里黑了下来，像落下了一道幕布。

===Original===

Semyonov turned round to me.

“Well,” he said, “there’s your battle.﻿ ﻿… You’ve been in the thick of it today!”

I saw his eyes turned to Marie Ivanovna as though already he possessed her.

I was suddenly tired, disappointed, exhausted.

“We’ve not been in the thick of it,” I answered. “We have missed it﻿—all day we have missed it!”

I tried to settle down in my wagon. “I beg your pardon,” I said irritably to Trenchard, “but your boot is in my neck!”

===Chinese===

谢苗诺夫回过身来对着我。

「怎么样，」他说，「这就是你们的战斗。……你们今天可是身临大战了！」

我看见他的眼睛转向玛丽·伊万诺夫娜，仿佛已经把她占有了。

我突然倦了，失望了，筋疲力尽了。

「我们哪里身临了大战，」我回答。「我们错过了它——一整天，我们都在错过它！」

我试着在车里躺舒坦些。「对不起，」我没好气地对特伦查德说，「您的靴子顶着我脖子了！」

## I The Lovers / 一 恋人们

===Original===
Semyonov and Marie Ivanovna did not offer us a picture of idealised love﻿—they did not offer us a picture of anything, and although they were, both of them, most certainly changed, they could not be said in any way to do what the Otriad expected of them. The Otriad quite frankly expected them to be ashamed of themselves. To expect that of Semyonov at any time showed a lamentable lack of interest in human character, but, as I have already said, our Otriad was always excited by results rather than causes. Semyonov had never shown himself ashamed of anything, and he most certainly did not intend to begin now. He had never disguised his love for Marie Ivanovna and now she was his “spoils”﻿—won by his own strong piratical hand from the good but rather feeble barque Trenchard﻿—he manifested his scorn of us more openly than ever.

He seemed to have grown rather stronger and stouter during these last months, and his square stolidity was a thing at which to marvel. Had he been taller, had his beard been pointed rather than square, he would have been graceful and even picturesque﻿—but his figure, as he strode along, showed foursquare, as though it had been hewn out of wood; one of those pale, almost white, honey-coloured woods would give the effect of his fair beard and eyebrows. His thick red lips were more startling than ever, curved as they usually were in cynical contempt of some foolish victim. How he did despise us!

When one of our childish quarrels arose at mealtimes he would say nothing, but would continue stolidly his serious business of eating. He was very fond of his food, which he ate in the greediest manner. When the quarrel was subsiding, as it usually did, into the first glasses of tea, he would look up, watch us with his contemptuous blue eyes, laugh and say: “Well, and now?﻿ ﻿… Who is it next?”﻿—and everyone would be clumsily embarrassed.

We were often, as are all Russian companies, ridiculously amused about nothing. At the most serious crises we would, like Gayev in *The Cherry Orchard*, suddenly break into stupid bursts of laughter, quite aimless but with a great deal of sincerity. Whirls of laughter would invade our table. “Oh, do look at Goga!” someone would say, and there we all were, perhaps for a quarter of an hour! Semyonov, strangely enough, shared this childish habit, and there was nothing odder than to see the man lose control of himself, double himself up, laugh until the tears ran down his face﻿—simply at nothing at all!

===Chinese===
谢苗诺夫和玛丽·伊万诺夫娜并没有向我们呈现一幅理想化爱情的图景——他们什么图景也没有向我们呈现；而且，尽管他们俩无疑都变了，却怎么也说不上做到了支队指望他们做的事。支队坦率得很，指望着他们俩自惭形秽。无论什么时候指望谢苗诺夫自惭形秽，都显出对人性可悲地缺乏兴味，不过，正如我已经说过的，我们的支队向来只为结果兴奋，而不问缘由。谢苗诺夫从来没有在任何事情上露出过羞愧，眼下自然更不打算开这个头。他从不掩饰自己对玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的爱，如今她是他的「战利品」了——是他凭自己那只海盗式的强壮大手，从那艘善良却相当不中用的「特伦查德号」三桅帆船上夺来的——于是他对我们的轻蔑，流露得比以往任何时候都更露骨。

这几个月里，他似乎变得更结实、更粗壮了些，他那方方正正的木然沉稳，真是一件令人叹为观止的东西。倘若他个子再高些，倘若他的胡须是尖的而不是方的，他本可以显得优雅，甚至有几分画意——可是他大步走去的时候，那身形四四方方，仿佛是用木头砍削出来的；而要造成他那淡色胡须和眉毛的效果，须得用一种浅色的、近乎白色的蜜色木头。他那两片厚厚的红嘴唇比以往任何时候都更触目，照例弯着，对某个愚蠢的牺牲者挂着冷嘲式的轻蔑。他是多么看不起我们啊！

吃饭的时候，我们那些孩子气的口角一起，他一言不发，只顾埋头继续他吃饭这桩正经营生。他非常讲究吃，吃相贪馋得很。等到口角照例平息下去、大家喝起头几杯茶的时候，他才抬起头来，用他那双轻蔑的蓝眼睛打量我们，笑着说道：「好啦，眼下呢？……下一个轮到谁？」——于是人人都笨拙地窘迫起来。

我们常常像所有俄国人聚在一处的场合一样，为着毫无来由的事情笑得不成样子。在最严重的关头，我们会像《樱桃园》（The Cherry Orchard）里的加耶夫（Gayev）那样，突然爆出一阵阵傻笑，全无目的，却极其真挚。一阵阵笑的漩涡会卷上我们的餐桌。「哎呀，快看戈加！」不知谁会说上一句，于是我们全都笑倒了，也许一笑就是一刻钟！说来奇怪，谢苗诺夫竟也沾着这孩子气的习气，再没有比看见这个人失态更奇怪的了：他笑得直不起腰，笑得眼泪顺着脸颊往下淌——而所笑的，根本什么也不是！

===Original===
The truth is that now I was very far from hating him. There were moments, certainly, when he was rude to the Sisters, when he was abominably greedy, when he was ruthlessly selfish, when he poured scorn upon me; at such times I thought him, as Trenchard has expressed it, a “beastly” man. He certainly had no great opinion of myself. “You think yourself very clever, Ivan Andreievitch. Yes, you think you’re watching all of us and studying all our characters. And I suppose there’ll be a book one day, another of those books by Englishmen about poor Russians﻿—and you’ll flatter yourself that now at last one true picture has been given﻿ ﻿… but let me tell you that you’ll never know anything really about us so long as you’re a sentimentalist!”

Yes, there were moments when I hated him, but those moments never continued for long. For one thing one could not hate so magnificent, so honest, so uncompromising, so efficient a worker! He was worthy of some very high position in the army, and he could certainly have attained any height had he chosen. He had a genius for compelling other men to obey him, he was never perturbed by unexpected mischance, he paid no attention at all to what other people thought of him, and he seemed incapable of fatigue. I often wondered what he was doing here, why he had chosen so small an Otriad as ours in which to work, why he stayed with us when he, so openly, despised us all. Until the arrival of Marie Ivanovna there was no answer to these questions﻿—after that the answer was obvious enough. Again, one could not hate a man of his sterling independence of character. We were, all of us I think, emotionalists, of one kind or another, and went up and down in our feelings, alliances, severances, trusts and distrusts, as a thermometer goes up and down. We were good enough people in our way, but we were most certainly not “a strong lot.” Even Nikitin, the best of the rest of us, was a dreamy idealist, far enough from life as it was and quite unprepared to come down from his dreams and see things as they were.

But Semyonov never relaxed for an instant from his position. He asked no man’s help nor advice, minded no man’s scorn, sought no man’s love. During my experience of him I saw him moved only once by an overmastering emotion, and that was, of course, his love for Marie Ivanovna. That, I believe, *did* master him, but deep down, deep down, he kept his rebellions, his anxieties, his surmises; only as the light of a burning house is seen by men, pale and faint upon the sky many miles from the conflagration, did we catch signs of his trouble. If I had not had those talks with Trenchard and read his diary I should have known nothing. Even now I can offer no solution.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
实情是，如今我远谈不上恨他。当然有过那样的时刻：他对护士们粗暴无礼，他贪馋得可憎，他自私得冷酷无情，他把讥诮劈头盖脸浇到我身上；每逢那种时候，我就觉得他——用特伦查德的话说——是个「畜生」一样的人。他对我本人也确实评价不高。「你自以为聪明得很，伊万·安德烈耶维奇。是的，你以为你在冷眼旁观我们所有人，研究我们每个人的性格。我料想将来总有一天会出一本书，又是英国人写可怜的俄国人的那一种书——你还会自鸣得意，以为如今终于有了一幅真实的图画……可我告诉你，只要你还是个感伤主义者，你就永远休想真正懂得我们！」

是的，有过我恨他的时刻，可那些时刻从不长久。一来，这样一个出色、这样诚实、这样绝不通融、这样干练的实干家，谁恨得起来！凭他，在军队里居极高的职位都绰绰有余，只要他愿意，任何高位都稳能达到。他有一种天才，能叫别人服从他；意外的变故从来搅不乱他；别人怎么看他，他全然置之度外；而且他仿佛根本不知疲倦为何物。我常常纳闷，他在这儿做什么，为什么偏偏挑中我们这么小的一个支队来做事，为什么他明明如此公开地看不起我们大家，却还留在我们中间。在玛丽·伊万诺夫娜到来之前，这些问题无从回答——自那以后，答案就再明显不过了。再者，他那种纯粹靠自身独立性立身的汉子，你也恨不起来。我想，我们这些人，个个都是这样那样的感情用事者，情感、亲疏、结盟、决裂、信任与猜忌，全像寒暑表似的起起落落。我们都算得上好人，但断断算不上「坚强的一伙」。就连我们中间最好的尼基京，也是个耽于梦幻的理想主义者，离生活的本来面目远之又远，全然没打算从他的梦乡里下来，看一看事物的实相。

而谢苗诺夫却连一瞬也不曾放松过他的姿态。他不求任何人的帮助，不听任何人的主意，不理会任何人的轻蔑，也不寻求任何人的爱。在我与他相处的全部经历里，我只见过他被一种压倒一切的情感撼动过一次，那自然就是他对玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的爱。我相信，那情感*确实*主宰了他；可是在最深处，最深的最深处，他仍旧守着自己的抗拒、自己的忧虑、自己的猜疑；我们所能捕捉到的他内心烦乱的迹象，就像人们望见一所焚烧的房子的火光——在离大火许多里外的天空中，淡淡地、幽幽地映着。倘若不是同特伦查德有过那几次谈话、读过他的日记，我对这一切本会一无所知。直到如今，我也拿不出什么解答。……

===Original===
Meanwhile he showed fiercely and openly enough his love for Marie Ivanovna. He behaved to her with the vulgarest ostentation, as a rich merchant behaves when he has snatched some priceless picture from a defeated rival. As he laughed at us he seemed to say: “Now, I have really a thing of value here. You are, all of you, too stupid to realise this, but you must take my word for it. Show yourself off, my dear, and let them all see!”

Marie Ivanovna most certainly did *not* “show herself off.” The beginning of his trouble was that he could not do with her as he pleased. She had fallen into his hands so easily that he thought, I suppose, that “she had been dying of love for him” from the first moment of seeing him. But this was I believe very far from the truth. My impression of her acceptance of him was that she had done it “with her eyes fixed upon something else.” That *she* had not realised all the consequences of accepting *him* any more than she had realised the consequences of her accepting Trenchard was obvious from the first. She simply was ignorant of life, and at the same time wanted to cram into her hands the full sense of it (as one crushes rose-leaves) as quickly as possible. She admired Semyonov﻿—it may be that she loved him; but she certainly had not surrendered herself to him, and in her lively ignorant way she was as strong as he.

During the first weeks of her engagement she was, as she had been at her first arrival amongst us, as happy and lighthearted as a child. She knew that we disapproved of her treatment of Trenchard, but she thought that we must see, as she did, that “she had behaved in the only possible way.” Once again she was straight and honest to the world﻿—and she could behave now like a real friend of her John. That strange irrational temper that she had shown during the Retreat had now entirely disappeared. She approved of us all and wished us to approve of her﻿—which we, as we were Russians and could not possibly dislike pleasant agreeable people whatever there might be against them, speedily did. She was charming to us. I can see her now, leaning her chin on her hands; looking at us, the colour, shell-pink, coming and going delicately in her cheek, like flame behind china. Her delicacy, her height, her slender figure, her wide childish eyes, her charmingly ugly large mouth and short nose, her black hair, the appeal of her ignorance and strength and credulity﻿—ah! she won our hearts simply whenever she pleased! Of course we disliked her when she was rude to us, our self-respect demanded it, but let her “come round” and round we came too.

Her treatment of Semyonov was strange. She was quite fearless, laughing at his temper, his sarcasm, rebuking his selfishness and bad manners, avoiding his coarse and unhesitating lovemaking, and above all, trusting him in the oddest way as though, in spite of his faults, she placed all her reliance on him and knew that he would not fail her. Nothing annoyed him more than her behaviour to Trenchard. It would, of course, be absurd to say that he was jealous of Trenchard; he despised the man too deeply and was, himself, too sure of his lady to know jealousy; but he was irritated by the attention paid to him, irritated even by the attention he himself paid to him.

===Chinese===
与此同时，他对玛丽·伊万诺夫娜的爱，表露得足够炽烈、足够公开。他对她的那副做态，俗气地炫耀到了极点，活像一个阔绰的商人，刚从败阵的对手手里抢到一幅无价的名画。他一面讥笑我们，一面仿佛在说：「瞧，我这回可真真攥着了一件值钱的宝贝。你们这些人全蠢得太厉害，认识不到这一点，可你们只管信我的话。亲爱的，好好亮亮相，让他们都开开眼！」

而玛丽·伊万诺夫娜最肯定*没有*「亮什么相」。他的烦恼，恰恰始于他无法随心所欲地摆布她。她那么轻易地落进了他的手心，我想他大约因此认定，「她从见他的头一眼起，就为他爱得要死了」。但这离实情我想差得很远。我得到的印象是，她接受他的时候，是「眼睛望着别处」应承下来的。*她*接受*他*时没有掂量过全部后果，正如她当初接受特伦查德时没有掂量过后果一样，这一点从头起就显而易见。她压根儿不懂生活，却又恨不得把生活的全部滋味尽快攥进手心（就像把玫瑰花瓣攥出汁来）。她钦慕谢苗诺夫——也许她爱他；但她断然没有把自己整个交付给他，而且以她那种活泼的无知，她同他一样强硬。

订婚后的头几个星期，她像初到我们中间时一样，快活得像个孩子。她知道我们不赞成她对特伦查德的做法，可她以为，我们必定同她一样看得明白：「她的做法是唯一可能的做法。」她又变得对世界坦率而诚实——如今她可以像一个真正的朋友那样对待她的约翰了。大撤退期间她在我们面前发作过的那种莫名其妙的坏脾气，如今消失得干干净净。她赞成我们所有人，也盼着我们赞成她——而我们，因为是俄国人，无论对讨人喜欢的人有什么闲话可讲，终究没法不喜欢他们，于是很快便赞成了她。她待我们十分可爱。我如今还能看见她：下巴支在双手上，望着我们，脸颊上那贝壳般的粉红色细细地来来去去，像瓷后面透出的火光。她的娇嫩，她的个子，她苗条的身段，她那双孩子气的大眼睛，她那张丑得迷人、又阔又大的嘴和短短的鼻子，她的黑发，还有她的无知、她的力量、她的轻信合成的动人劲儿——啊！她只要乐意，随时随地都能把我们的心赢过去！当然，她对我们无礼的时候，我们也讨厌她，我们的自尊心要求如此；可只要她「回过头来」一哄，我们也就跟着回过头去。

她对待谢苗诺夫的方式很奇怪。她全无惧色，笑话他的脾气、他的挖苦，数落他的自私和坏举止，躲开他那粗鲁而不由分说的求爱，而顶奇怪的是，她以顶古怪的方式信赖着他，仿佛纵然他有种种毛病，她仍旧把全部依靠放在他身上，知道他不会辜负她。最叫他恼火的，莫过于她对特伦查德的态度。当然，说他吃特伦查德的醋未免荒唐；他把那个人鄙视得太深，自己又对他的女人太有把握，根本不懂得什么叫嫉妒；可是别人对那个人的关注叫他恼火，就连他自己对那个人的关注也叫他恼火。

===Original===
“Wherever I go there’s that man,” he said once to me. “Why doesn’t he go back to his own country?”

“I suppose,” I would answer hotly, “he has other things to do than to consider your individual wishes, Alexei Petrovitch.”

Then he would laugh: “Well, well, Ivan Andreievitch, you sentimentalists all hang together.”

“Why can’t you leave him alone?” I remember that I continued.

“Because he doesn’t leave me alone,” he answered shortly.

===Chinese===
「不管我到哪儿，总有那个人。」他有一次对我说。「他干吗不回他自己的国家去？」

「我料想，」我总是气冲冲地答道，「他除了掂量您个人的意愿之外，还有别的事情要做，阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇。」

于是他就笑：「得了，得了，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，你们这些感伤主义者倒是一个鼻孔出气。」

「你为什么不能放过他呢？」我记得我当时接着说。

「因为他不放过我。」他简短地答道。

===Original===
It was, of course, Marie Ivanovna who brought them together. She could not see, or rather she *would* not see, that friendship between two such men was an impossibility. For herself she liked Trenchard better than she had ever done. She had now no responsibility towards him; we were all fond of him, pleased ourselves by saying that “he was more Russian than English.” The Sisters mended his clothes, cared for his stomach, and listened with pleased gravity to his innocent chatter. Marie Ivanovna was now really proud of him. There were great stories of the courage and enterprise he had shown during the night when he had been with Nikitin. Nikitin, in his lofty romantic fashion, spoke of him as though he had been the hero of the Russian army. Trenchard was, of course, quite unspoiled by this praise and popularity. He remained for me at least very much the same innocent, clumsy, pathetic, and frequently irritating figure that he had been at the beginning. I will honestly confess that I was often heartily tired of his Glebeshire stories, tired too of a certain childish obstinacy with which he clung to his generally crude and half-baked opinions.

But then I do not care to be contradicted by people of whom, intellectually, I have a low estimation; it is one of my most unfortunate weaknesses. I had no opinion of Trenchard’s intellect at all, and in that I was quite wrong. Semyonov at this time flung Nikitin, Andrey Vassilievitch, Trenchard and myself into one basket. We were all “crazy romantics” and there came an occasion, which I have reason most clearly to remember, when he told us what he thought of us. We were together, Semyonov, Nikitin, Trenchard and I, after breakfast, smoking cigarettes, enjoying half an hour’s idleness before setting about our various business. It was a blazing hot morning and the air quivered, like a silver curtain before our eyes, separating us from the dim blue forest of S﻿—﻿— beyond the river, the Nestor itself, the deep green slopes of our own hill. We had been silent, then Trenchard said a foolish thing: “War brings all the best out of people, I think,” he said. God knows what private line of thought he had been pursuing, some sentimental reflections, I suppose, that were in him perfectly honest and sincere. But he did not look his best that morning, sitting back in his chair with his mouth open, his forehead damp with the heat, his tunic up about his neck and a rather dirty blue pocket-handkerchief in his hand.

===Chinese===
把他们俩拉到一处的，当然是玛丽·伊万诺夫娜。她看不出——或者不如说，她*不肯*看出——这样两个男人之间的友谊是根本不可能的。至于她自己，她比以往任何时候都喜欢特伦查德。她如今对他再没有责任了；我们都喜欢他，还乐于说什么「他与其说像英国人，倒不如说更像俄国人」。护士们替他缝补衣裳，操心他的肠胃，带着愉悦的郑重听他天南海北地闲聊。玛丽·伊万诺夫娜如今是真心为他感到骄傲。他同尼基京一起度过那一夜时所表现出的胆识与果敢，已经传出了种种美谈。尼基京以他那高远的浪漫派头谈起他来，仿佛他竟是俄国军队的英雄。这些赞誉和名头自然丝毫没有惯坏特伦查德。至少在我看来，他仍旧是起初那个又天真、又笨拙、又可怜、又时常惹人恼火的角色。我愿意老老实实承认，他那些格利比郡的故事我常常听得腻透了，还有他死抱着自己那一套多半粗陋半生不熟的意见时那种孩子气的固执，也叫我腻烦。

不过话说回来，我本就不喜欢被那些我在智力上看低的人反驳；这是我最要命的一个弱点。我当时对特伦查德的智力毫无评价，这一点我大错特错。那段日子里，谢苗诺夫把尼基京、安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇、特伦查德和我一股脑儿扔进同一只篮子里。我们全是些「疯疯癫癫的浪漫派」。有这么一个场合——我有理由记得清清楚楚——他把他对我们的想法当面说了出来。那天早饭后，谢苗诺夫、尼基京、特伦查德和我坐在一起，抽着烟，在各自着手办事之前享受半小时的清闲。那是个火热灼人的上午，空气颤动着，像一挂银色的幕布悬在我们眼前，把我们同河对岸 S—— 那片朦胧的蓝色森林、同涅斯托尔河本身、同我们自己这座山深沉的绿坡隔了开来。我们本来都没作声，随后特伦查德说了句傻话。「战争把人们身上最好的东西都激发出来了，我想。」他说。天知道他心里原先转着什么念头，我猜想是些感伤的遐想吧，在他身上倒完全是诚恳真挚的。可那天早晨他的样子实在不出色：仰靠在椅子里，嘴张着，额头汗津津的，军装上衣支在脖子边，手里捏着一条相当脏的蓝手帕。

===Original===
I saw Semyonov’s lip curl.

“Yes. That’s very interesting, Mr.,” he said. “I’m glad at any rate that we’ve had the honour of seeing the best of *you*. That’s very pleasant to know.”

“What I mean﻿—” said Trenchard, blushing and stammering. “What﻿ ﻿… that is﻿—”

“I agree with Mr.,” suddenly said Nikitin, who had been dreamily watching the blue forest. “War *does* bring out the best in the human character﻿—always.”

===Chinese===
我看见谢苗诺夫的嘴唇撇了撇。

「是啊。这可真有意思，先生。」他说，「不管怎样，我很高兴我们总算有幸见识了*您*身上最好的东西。知道这个，实在叫人愉快。」

「我的意思是——」特伦查德红着脸、结结巴巴地说。「什么……那个，就是说——」

「我同意这位先生的意见。」一直在出神望着那片蓝色森林的尼基京突然开口。「战争*确实*能把人性中最好的东西引发出来——向来如此。」

===Original===
Semyonov turned smilingly to him. “Yes, Vladimir Stepanovitch, we know your illusions. Forgive me for insisting that they are illusions. I would not disturb your romantic happiness for the world.”

“You can’t disturb me, Alexei Petrovitch,” Nikitin answered sleepily. “What a hot morning!”

“No,” said Semyonov. “I would be very wrong to disturb you. Believe me, I’ve never tried. It’s very agreeable to me to see you and Mr. so happy together and it must be pleasant for both of you to feel that you’ve got a nice God all of your own who sleeps a good deal but still, on the whole, gives you what you want. We may wonder a little what Mr. has done to be so favoured﻿—never very much I fancy﻿—but still I like the friendliness and comfort of it and I’m really lucky to have the good fortune of your acquaintance. So nice for Russia too to have plenty of people about who don’t do any work nor take any trouble about anything because they’ve got a nice fat God who’ll do it all for them if they’ll only be patient. Thats why we’re beating the Germans so handsomely﻿—the poor Germans, who only, ignorant heathens as they are, believe in themselves.”

===Chinese===
谢苗诺夫笑吟吟地转向他。「是的，弗拉基米尔·斯捷潘诺维奇，您的种种幻梦我们是知道的。请原谅我一口咬定那是幻梦。说什么我也决不肯搅扰您那浪漫的幸福。」

「您搅扰不了我，阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇。」尼基京困倦地答道，「多热的早晨啊！」

「是啊，」谢苗诺夫说，「搅扰您就是我的大错特错了。请相信我，我从来没有那样试过。看见您同这位先生在一起这般幸福，我心中很惬意；你们两位想必也觉着愉快，觉得自己有一位专属的好上帝，他大半时间都在睡觉，不过总的说来，你们要什么，他给什么。这位先生究竟立了什么功劳蒙受这样的恩宠，我们未免有点纳闷——我想从来也没有多少——不过我喜欢这份其乐融融的舒坦劲儿，能高攀诸位的交情，实在是我天大的造化。俄国也是好福气，四下里有的是这样的人：什么活儿也不干，什么事也不操心，因为他们有一位和和气气、胖胖乎乎的上帝，只要他们肯耐心等着，一切他都会替他们办好。我们就是这样漂漂亮亮地揍着德国人的——可怜的德国人，他们不过是些无知的异教徒，只晓得信他们自己。」

===Original===
He looked at us all with a friendly patronising contempt.

“That’s your point of view, Alexei Petrovitch,” Nikitin answered rather hotly. “Think as you please of course. But there’s more in life than you can see﻿—there is indeed.”

“Of course there is,” said Semyonov lazily, “much more. I’m an ignorant, rough man. I like things as they are and make the best of them, so, of course, I’m not clever. Mr.’s clever, aren’t you, Mr.? All the same he doesn’t know how to put his boots on properly. If he put his boots on better and knew less about God he might be of more use at the Front, perhaps. That’s only my idea, and I daresay I’m wrong.﻿ ﻿… All the same, for the sake of the comfort *and* the pockets of all of us I do hope you’ll really rouse your God and ask Him to do something sensible﻿—something with method in it and a few more bullets in it and a little more efficiency in it. You might ask Him to do what He can.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===
他带着友好的、居高临下的轻蔑，把我们挨个儿看了一遍。

「那是您的看法，阿列克谢·彼得罗维奇。」尼基京相当激动地回答，「您爱怎么想，当然随您。可生活里有的东西，远不是您看得见的——当真有的。」

「当然有，」谢苗诺夫懒洋洋地说，「多得呢。我是个无知、粗鲁的人。我喜欢事物的本来面目，随遇而安，所以嘛，我当然不聪明。这位先生就聪明，是不是，先生？只是呢，他连自己的靴子都穿不周正。他要是靴子穿得像样些，对上帝少懂得一些，在前线或许还能多几分用处。这不过是我一己之见，多半是我错了。……不过，为了我们大家的安逸*和*钱袋起见，我倒真心盼望你们把你们的上帝唤醒，求他做点合情合理的事——要有点章法，多添几发子弹，再加一点效率。你们不妨求他尽力而为。……」

===Original===
He looked at us, laughing; then he said to Trenchard, “But don’t you fear, Mr. You’ll go to heaven all right. Even though it’s the wise men who succeed in this world, I don’t doubt it’s the fools who have their way in the next.”

He left us.

Semyonov was with every new day more baffled by Marie Ivanovna. In the first place she quietly refused to obey him. We were now much occupied with the feeding of the peasants in a village stricken with cholera on the other side of the river. A gloomy enough business it was and I shall have, very shortly, to speak of it in detail. For the moment it is enough to say that two of us went off every morning with a kitchen on wheels, distributed the food, and returned in the afternoon. Semyonov intensely disliked Marie Ivanovna’s share in this work, but he could not, of course, object to her taking, with the other Sisters, the risks and unpleasantness of it. He made, whenever it was possible, objections, found her work at the hospital where he himself was, occupied her in every possible way. But he did this against her will. She seemed to find a very especial pleasure and excitement in the cholera work; she wished often to take the place of some other Sister. Indeed everything on the other side of the river seemed to have a great fascination for her. She herself told me: “The moment I cross the bridge I feel as though I were on enchanted ground.” On the occasions when I accompanied her to the cholera village she was radiant, so happy that she seemed to have nothing further in the world to desire. She herself was puzzled. “What is it?” she said to me. “Is it the forest? It must be, I think, the forest. I would remain on this side forever if I had my way.”

===Chinese===
他笑着看了看我们；随后他对特伦查德说：「不过您也别怕，先生。您上天堂是一定上得去的。尽管今世成事的是智者，我却毫不怀疑，来世称心如意的，必是痴人。」

他离我们去了。

谢苗诺夫一天比一天捉摸不透玛丽·伊万诺夫娜。首先，她不动声色地拒绝服从他。我们当时正忙着给河对岸一座闹霍乱的村子里的农民供饭。那是件够阴郁的差事，过不多久我就得细细谈到它。眼下只需说一句：我们每天早晨有两个人带着一辆伙房车出去，分发食物，下午回来。谢苗诺夫极其反感玛丽·伊万诺夫娜在这项差事里担的份儿，可他当然没法反对她同别的护士一起承担其中的风险和不快。他一有机会就提出异议，给他自己所在的医院里给她找活儿，千方百计占住她。可这些都是违背她心愿的。她似乎偏偏在霍乱工作里尝到一种特别的快乐和兴奋；她常常主动要替下别的某位护士。的确，河对岸的一切仿佛都对她有一股强大的魔力。她亲口对我说过：「一跨上那座桥，我就觉得自己踏上了着了魔的土地。」有几回我陪她去霍乱村，她满面生辉，快活得仿佛世上再没有别的可求了。她自己也好生纳闷。「这是怎么回事呢？」她对我说，「是森林吗？我想一定是森林。要是随我，我情愿永远待在这一边。」

===Original===
When I saw Semyonov’s anxiety about her I could not but remember that little scene at the battle of S﻿—﻿— when he had taken her off with him, leaving Trenchard in so pitiful a condition. Certainly Time brings in his revenges! And Marie Ivanovna would listen to nothing that he said.

“I want you at the hospital this morning,” he would say.

===Chinese===
每逢看见谢苗诺夫为她悬心，我就不由得想起 S—— 之战中的那一幕小小的场景：他当时把她带走了，把特伦查德撂在那么可怜的境地。时间老人果然是要讨还旧账的！而玛丽·伊万诺夫娜呢，他的话她一个字也听不进。

「今天上午我要你待在医院里。」他总这么说。

===Original===
“Do you really want me?” she would ask, looking up, laughing, in his face.

“Of course I do.”

“Well, you should have told me last night. This morning I go with Anna Petrovna to the cholera. All is arranged.”

“I’m afraid you must change your plans.”

===Chinese===
「你真的要我吗？」她会抬起头，笑着仰望他的脸问。

「当然真的。」

「那你昨晚就该告诉我。今天上午我要同安娜·彼得罗夫娜去霍乱村。一切都安排好了。」

「恐怕你必须改变计划。」

===Original===
“I’m afraid not.”

“Goga may go.﻿ ﻿…”

“No, I wish to go.”

===Chinese===
「恐怕不必。」

「戈加可以去嘛。……」

「不，我要亲自去。」

===Original===
And she went. He had certainly never before in his life been thus defied. He simply did not know what to do about it. If he had thought that bullying would frighten her he would, I believe, have bullied her, but he knew quite well that it wouldn’t. And then, as I now began to perceive (I had at first thought otherwise), he was for the first time in his life experiencing something deeper and more confusing than his customary animal passions. He may at first have wanted Marie Ivanovna as he wanted his dinner or his supper﻿ ﻿… now he wanted her differently. New emotions, surprising confusing emotions stirred in him. At least that is how I interpret the uneasiness, the hesitation, which I now seemed to perceive in him. He was no longer sure of himself.

I witnessed just at this time a little scene that surprised me. I had been in the bandaging room alone one evening, cutting up bandages. I was going through the passage into the other part of the house when a sound stopped me. I could not avoid seeing beyond the open door a little scene that happened so swiftly that I could neither retire nor advance.

===Chinese===
于是她就去了。他一生中肯定从来没有这样被人违拗过。他简直不知道该拿这个怎么办。倘若他以为威吓能镇住她，我相信他会威吓她的，可他心里很明白那不管用。再者——这一点我如今才开始察觉（起初我想的正好相反）——他生平头一回经历着某种比他惯常的兽性情欲更深刻、更教人迷乱的东西。起初他要玛丽·伊万诺夫娜，也许就像要他的午饭或他的晚饭一样……如今他要她，要得不一样了。新的情感，又惊奇又迷乱的情感，在他心里翻腾起来。至少，我是这样解释我此刻仿佛在他身上察觉到的那种不安、那种踌躇的。他对自己不再有把握了。

恰在这个当口，我目睹了一幕叫我吃惊的小场景。一天傍晚，我独自在包扎室里裁绷带。我正穿过过道往房子另一头去，一个声音让我停了下来。透过那扇敞开的门，那一幕小场景我想避也避不开——它来得那么快，我退不得，也进不得。

===Original===
Marie Ivanovna and Semyonov were coming together towards the bandaging room. She was in front of him when he put his hand on her arm.

“Do you love me?” he said in a low voice.

She turned round to him, laughing.

“Yes,” she said, looking at him.

===Chinese===
玛丽·伊万诺夫娜和谢苗诺夫正一道朝包扎室走来。她走在他前头，他伸手按住了她的手臂。

「你爱我吗？」他低声说。

她回过身来朝着他，笑着。

「爱。」她说，望着他。

===Original===
“Then kiss me.”

“No, not now.”

“Why not now?”

“I don’t want to.”

“Why don’t you want to?”

She shook her head, still laughing into his eyes.

===Chinese===
「那就吻我。」

「不，现在不行。」

「为什么现在不行？」

「我不想。」

「你为什么不想？」

她摇了摇头，仍旧笑着望进他的眼睛。

===Original===
“But if I command you?”

“Ah! *command!*﻿ ﻿… Then I certainly will not.”

His hand tightened on her arm and she did not draw away.

“Kiss me.”

===Chinese===
「那要是我命令你呢？」

「啊！*命令*！……那我偏不。」

他扣着她手臂的手紧了紧，她没有抽开。

「吻我。」

===Original===
“No.”

“I say yes.”

“I say no.”

He suddenly caught her, held her to him as though he would kill her and kissed her furiously, on her eyes, her mouth, her hair. With his violence he pushed back her headdress. I could see his back bent like a bow, and his thick short legs wide apart, every muscle taut. She lay quite motionless, as though asleep in his arms, giving him no response﻿—then quite suddenly she flung her hands round his neck and kissed him as passionately as he had kissed her. At last they parted, both of them laughing.

===Chinese===
「不。」

「我说行。」

「我说不行。」

他突然一把搂住她，把她紧紧箍在怀里，仿佛要掐死她似的，狂暴地吻她的眼睛、她的嘴、她的头发。他那一阵猛劲儿，把她头上的头巾都撞歪了。我看得见他弓一样弯着的脊背，两条粗壮的短腿叉得开开的，每一条肌肉都绷得紧紧的。她一动不动地躺着，仿佛在他怀里睡着了，毫不回应——忽然间，她猛地张开双臂搂住他的脖子，回吻他，吻得同他吻她一样热烈。末了两人分开，都笑了。

===Original===
He looked at her, and then with a gentleness and courtesy that I had never seen in him before nor dreamed that he possessed, very softly kissed her hand.

“I love you and﻿—and you love me,” he said.

“Yes﻿ ﻿… I love you,” she answered gravely. “At least, part of me does.”

“It shall be all of you soon,” he answered.

===Chinese===
他望着她，随后带着一种我在他身上从未见过、做梦也想不到他会有的温柔与斯文，轻轻地吻了吻她的手。

「我爱你，而且——而且你也爱我。」他说。

「是的……我爱你。」她郑重地回答，「至少，我身上有一部分在爱。」

「要不了多久，就整全是你了。」他答道。

===Original===
“If there’s time enough,” she replied.

“Time!﻿ ﻿… I’ll follow you wherever you go﻿—”

“I really believe you will,” she answered, laughing again. They waited then, looking at one another. A bell rang. “Ah! I’m hungry.﻿ ﻿… Supper time.﻿ ﻿…” To my relief they passed away from the bandaging room towards the other part of the house.

===Chinese===
「只要时间够用。」她答道。

「时间！……你去哪儿，我就跟到哪儿——」

「这我倒真信。」她答道，又笑起来。随后他们站定，彼此对望。铃响了。「啊！我饿了。……吃晚饭的时辰到了。……」叫我松了口气的是，他们从包扎室门前走过去了，往房子另一头去了。

===Original===
Meanwhile his irritation at Marie Ivanovna’s kindness to Trenchard increased with every hour. His attitude to the man had changed since Trenchard’s night at the Position; he was vexed, I think, to hear that the fellow had proved himself a man﻿—and a practical man with common sense. Semyonov was honest about this. He did not doubt Nikitin’s word, he even congratulated Trenchard, but he certainly disliked him more than ever. He thought, I suppose, as he had thought about Nikitin: “How can a man with his wits about him be at the same time such a fool?” And then he saw that Marie Ivanovna was delighted with Trenchard’s little piece of good luck. She laughed at Semyonov about it. “We all know you’re a very brave man,” she cried. “But you’re not so brave as Mr.” And Semyonov, because he knew that Trenchard was a fool and that he himself was not, was vexed, as a bull is vexed by a red flag. These things made him think a great deal about Trenchard. I have seen him watching him with angry and puzzled gaze as though he would satisfy himself why this gnat of a man worried him!

===Chinese===
与此同时，玛丽·伊万诺夫娜对特伦查德的亲热，时时刻刻加深着他的恼怒。自从特伦查德在阵地上度过那一夜之后，他对这个人的态度变了；听说这家伙证明了自己是个男子汉——而且是个有常识的实干的人，我想他是憋了一肚子火。这一点上谢苗诺夫是诚实的。他不怀疑尼基京的话，甚至向特伦查德道了贺，但他无疑比以往更讨厌他了。我想，他心里大约同当初想到尼基京时一样：「一个脑子这么清楚的人，怎么同时又能是这样一个傻子？」随后他又看见，特伦查德这桩小小的走运让玛丽·伊万诺夫娜喜滋滋的。她拿这个取笑谢苗诺夫。「我们都知道你是个非常勇敢的人。」她嚷道，「可你还没有这位先生勇敢呢。」谢苗诺夫呢，因为他知道特伦查德是个傻子、而他自己不是，心里的火气就像公牛见了红旗。这些事逼着他没完没了地琢磨特伦查德。我见过他气冲冲、百思不解地盯着他看，仿佛定要弄个明白，这个蚊子似的人物凭什么搅得他不得安宁！

===Original===
Then, finally, was Andrey Vassilievitch.﻿ ﻿… The little man had not given me much of his company during these last weeks. I fancy that since that night at the battle of S﻿—﻿— when he had revealed his terror he had been shy of me although, God knows, he had no need to be. He never forgot if anyone had seen him in an unfortunate position, and, although he bore me no grudge, he was nervous and embarrassed with me. It happened, however, that during this same week of which I have been speaking I had a conversation with him. I was standing alone by the Cross watching a long trail of wagons cross the bridge far beneath me, watching too a high bank of black cloud that was passing away from the sky above the forest, blown by a wind that rolled the surface of the river into silver. He too had come to look at the view and was surprised and disturbed at finding me there. Of course he was exaggerated in expressions of pleasure: “Why, Ivan Andreievitch, this is delightful!” he cried. “If I only had known we might have walked here together!”

===Chinese===
再有，最后便是安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇了。……这几个星期，这小个子不大同我做伴了。我想，自从 S—— 之战的那个夜里他露了怯之后，他就有些怕见我，虽然天知道，他大可不必。只要有人撞见了他倒霉的样子，他一辈子忘不掉；他虽然并不记恨我，同我在一起却总是紧张、局促。然而恰在我说到的这个星期里，我同他有过一次谈话。那天我独自站在十字架旁，望着脚下远处一长列大车过桥，也望着森林上空一大垛乌黑的云正在退去，风吹得河面翻起一片银鳞。他也来看风景，见我在这里，又惊又不安。当然，他的高兴话说得过了头：「哎呀，伊万·安德烈耶维奇，这可真叫人愉快！」他喊道，「早知道的话，我们本可以一块儿散步过来的！」

===Original===
We sat down on the stone seat.

“You don’t think it will rain?” he asked anxiously. “No, those clouds are going away, I see. Well﻿ ﻿… this is delightful﻿ ﻿…” and then sat there gloomily looking in front of him.

I could see that he was depressed.

“Well, Andrey Vassilievitch,” I said to him. “You’re depressed about something?”

“Yes,” he said very gloomily indeed. “I have many unhappy hours, Ivan Andreievitch.”

===Chinese===
我们在石凳上坐了下来。

「您看不会下雨吧？」他不无焦虑地问。「不会，那几片云正在散去，我看得见。唔……真是愉快……」随后便坐在那里，阴郁地望着身前。

我看得出，他情绪很低落。

「怎么啦，安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇，」我对他说，「您在为什么事发愁？」

「是啊。」他说，语气确实非常阴沉，「我有许多不痛快的时辰，伊万·安德烈耶维奇。」

===Original===
I did not get up and leave him as I very easily might have done. I had had, since the night when Nikitin had spoken to me so frankly, a desire to know the little man’s side of that affair. In some curious fashion that silent plain wife of his had been very frequently in my thoughts; there had not been enough in Nikitin’s account to explain to me his passion for her, and yet her ghost, as though evoked by the memories both of Nikitin and her husband, had seemed to me, sometimes, to be present with us.﻿ ﻿…

I waited.

“Tell me frankly,” Andrey Vassilievitch said at last, “am I of any use here?”

“Of use?” I repeated, taken by surprise.

“Yes. Am I doing only what anyone else can do as well? Would it be better perhaps if another were here?”

===Chinese===
我没有起身离开他——尽管我本来轻而易举就可以那样做。自尼基京那晚对我推心置腹以来，我一直渴望知道那段情事里这小个子的一面。也不知怎么，他那位沉默的、相貌平平的妻子，频频来到我的思绪里；尼基京的那番讲述，还不足以向我解释清楚他对她的那股痴情；然而她的幽魂，仿佛被尼基京和她丈夫两人的回忆一齐召唤了来，有时竟似乎就伴随在我们身边。……

我等着。

「坦白告诉我，」安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇终于开了口，「我在这里到底还有点用处吗？」

「用处？」我吃了一惊，重复道。

「是的。我做的事，是不是随便哪个人都能做得一样好？换一个人在这里，是不是或许更好些？」

===Original===
“No, certainly not,” I answered warmly. “Your business training is of the greatest value to us. Molozov has said to me ‘that he does not know what we should do without you.’ ”

(This was not strictly true.)

“Ah!” the little man was greatly pleased. “I am glad, very glad﻿—to hear what you say. Semyonov made me feel﻿—”

“You should not be influenced,” I hurriedly interrupted him, “by what Semyonov thinks. It is of no importance.”

“He has a bad character,” Andrey Vassilievitch said suddenly with great excitement, “a bad character. And why cannot he leave me alone? Why should he laugh always? I do my best. I am quiet and not in his way. I can do things that he cannot. I am not big as he but at least I do not rob men of their women.”

===Chinese===
「不，当然不是。」我热烈地答道，「您受过商务训练，对我们价值极大。莫洛佐夫就对我说过：『要是没有您，我们真不知道该怎么办。』」

（这话并不全然属实。）

「啊！」小个子高兴极了。「听您这么说，我很高兴，非常高兴——谢苗诺夫让我觉得——」

「您不该受影响，」我急忙打断他，「不该受谢苗诺夫那些想法的影响。那无关紧要。」

「他是个品性恶劣的人。」安德烈·瓦西里耶维奇突然万分激动地说，「品性恶劣。他为什么就不能放过我？他为什么总是嘲笑？我是尽了力的。我安安静静，不碍他的事。他干不成的事，我干得成。我不像他那样人高马大，可至少我不抢人家的女人。」

===Original===
